#Questions & Answers from Recent Interview Rounds

Source: FE.txt — panelist Q&A set used in Round 1 screening. Format: Q → A → Rationale → 📄 Evidence

#FE-1. What is the DOM? How does it differ from HTML source, and how does JS interact with it?

Q: What is the DOM? How does it differ from the HTML source, and how does JavaScript interact with it?

A: HTML is the source text. The DOM (Document Object Model) is the in-memory tree the browser constructs from that HTML — it's a live, programmatic API. JS queries (querySelector, getElementById) and mutates (createElement, appendChild, setAttribute) the DOM at runtime. After JS runs, the DOM can be radically different from the original HTML — e.g., a React SPA starts as a nearly empty HTML file but builds a full tree client-side.

Rationale: Tests whether the candidate understands browser rendering pipeline vs static markup. Interviewers probe for confusion like "the DOM is the HTML" — a red flag for debugging skill. Also baseline for VDOM discussion (Q7).

📄 Evidence: MDN — Introduction to the DOM · WHATWG DOM Living Standard

#FE-2. Difference between var, let, and const — which to reach for first?

Q: Explain the difference between var, let, and const. Which should you reach for first, and why?

A: var is function-scoped, hoisted, and re-declarable — avoid in modern code. let is block-scoped and mutable. const is block-scoped, must be initialized, and the binding is immutable (though object contents can still mutate). Default to const — most bindings don't need reassignment, and const makes intent explicit. Reach for let only when reassignment is genuinely required.

Rationale: Probes knowledge of scoping, hoisting bugs, and idiomatic modern JS. var in a loop body is a classic interview trap (setTimeout inside a for loop). Correct answer signals discipline: "const by default" is the widely accepted community standard (Airbnb, Google style guides).

📄 Evidence: MDN — var · MDN — let · MDN — const · You Don't Know JS — Scope & Closures

#FE-3. Difference between == and === — give a surprising == result.

Q: What's the difference between == and === in JavaScript? Give an example where == produces a surprising result.

A: == performs type coercion before comparing; === checks both type and value without coercion. Surprising results: 0 == "" → true, 0 == "0" → true, [] == false → true, null == undefined → true, but null == 0 → false. Always prefer === to avoid these traps.

Rationale: Classic JS gotcha question — tests awareness of type coercion rules (Abstract Equality Comparison algorithm). The null == undefined edge case is specifically useful in banking null-checks. Interviewers also watch for confidence: candidates who say "just use ===" without explaining why are less convincing than those who cite specific coercion examples.

📄 Evidence: MDN — Equality comparisons · ECMA-262 Abstract Equality Comparison §7.2.13

#FE-4. What is a Promise? How does async/await relate, and what happens if you await a non-Promise?

Q: What is a Promise? Explain how async/await relates to Promises, and what happens if you await something that isn't a Promise.

A: A Promise is an object representing the eventual completion (or failure) of an async operation — it has three states: pending, fulfilled, rejected. async/await is syntactic sugar over Promises: an async function always returns a Promise, and await pauses execution until a Promise settles. Awaiting a non-Promise wraps it in Promise.resolve() — await 5 behaves identically to await Promise.resolve(5) — so it's safe but the value resolves immediately.

Rationale: Core async literacy test. Interviewers look for: (1) confirmation that async functions always return a Promise, (2) that await doesn't block the thread — it yields to the event loop, (3) error handling (try/catch vs .catch()). In banking apps, unhandled Promise rejections can silently swallow payment errors.

📄 Evidence: MDN — Promise · MDN — async function · Jake Archibald — Tasks, microtasks, queues and schedules

#FE-5. What is event bubbling? stopPropagation() vs preventDefault()?

Q: What is event bubbling? What's the difference between event.stopPropagation() and event.preventDefault()?

A: When an event fires on an element, it propagates up through its ancestors in the DOM, triggering their listeners — this is the bubbling phase. stopPropagation() stops that upward propagation; the event won't reach ancestor handlers. preventDefault() cancels the browser's default action (e.g., form submission, anchor navigation) but does not stop propagation. They solve independent problems and can be combined.

Rationale: Tests understanding of the DOM event model (capture → target → bubble). Common trap: candidates conflate the two. preventDefault() is critical for custom form handling in banking UIs (prevent double-submit). stopPropagation() matters for modal overlays and nested clickable components.

📄 Evidence: MDN — Event.stopPropagation() · MDN — Event.preventDefault() · MDN — Event bubbling

#FE-6. What is event delegation? Why is it useful for dynamic content?

Q: What is event delegation in the DOM? Why is it useful, especially for dynamic content?

A: Attach one listener to a parent element and use event.target inside it to determine which descendant triggered the event. Relies on bubbling. Three advantages: (1) memory-efficient — one listener for N elements instead of N listeners; (2) works automatically for dynamically added children (a list item added after page load is caught by the parent listener); (3) simplifies cleanup (remove one listener, not N).

Rationale: Practical DOM optimization question. Interviewers probe whether candidates would naively add a listener per item in a 1000-row table. In React this is less manual (React uses a single root listener internally), but understanding the underlying mechanism matters for vanilla JS tasks and performance debugging.

📄 Evidence: MDN — Event delegation · JavaScript.info — Event delegation

#FE-7. What is the Virtual DOM? What does React do when state changes?

Q: What is the Virtual DOM? At a high level, what is React doing when state changes?

A: The VDOM is an in-memory tree of lightweight JS objects mirroring the UI structure. On state change, React builds a new VDOM tree, diffs it against the previous snapshot (reconciliation / "diffing"), and applies the minimum set of real DOM mutations needed. Direct DOM updates are expensive; batching + diffing is faster. Keys in lists help the diff algorithm distinguish moved, new, and removed nodes efficiently — without keys, React falls back to O(n³) tree comparison.

Rationale: Essential React internals question. Interviewers listen for: VDOM ≠ real DOM, reconciliation is the diff step, keys are performance-critical (missing key bugs cause subtle UI state bugs). Candidates who only say "React updates the DOM when state changes" without mentioning diffing are flagged as shallow.

📄 Evidence: React Docs — Preserving and Resetting State · React Docs — Reconciliation (legacy) · Dan Abramov — React as a UI Runtime

#FE-8. Difference between localStorage, sessionStorage, and cookies — when to use each?

Q: What's the difference between localStorage, sessionStorage, and cookies? When would you use each?

A:

localStorage sessionStorage Cookies
Capacity ~5–10 MB ~5 MB ~4 KB
Expiry Never (manual clear) Tab close Configurable (expires/max-age)
Sent to server No No Yes (every request to domain)
JS access Yes Yes Yes unless HttpOnly

Use cookies for server-side auth tokens (set HttpOnly + Secure + SameSite=Strict to prevent XSS/CSRF). Use localStorage for client-only persistent state (theme, locale). Use sessionStorage for per-tab temporary state (multi-step form drafts).

Rationale: Storage security is critical in banking. Interviewers specifically watch for: (1) storing JWTs in localStorage = XSS-vulnerable, (2) HttpOnly cookies = JS-inaccessible = XSS-safe, (3) SameSite flag mitigates CSRF. Correct answer signals security awareness beyond convenience.

📄 Evidence: MDN — Web Storage API · MDN — HTTP Cookies · OWASP — Session Management Cheat Sheet

#FE-9. Debouncing vs throttling — use case for each?

Q: What's the difference between debouncing and throttling? Give a use case for each.

A: Debounce — wait until N ms of silence then fire once. The timer resets on every new event. Fire only after activity stops. Throttle — fire at most once per N ms regardless of how many events occur. Caps rate even during continuous activity.

  • Debounce use case: Search-as-you-type — only send API call when user pauses typing (e.g., 300 ms after last keystroke), preventing a request per keystroke.
  • Throttle use case: scroll, resize, or mousemove handlers — cap to 60fps (≈16 ms) so the handler doesn't fire hundreds of times per second during continuous events.

Rationale: Rate-limiting is a fundamental performance and API cost question. Interviewers probe: can candidate correctly identify which pattern to apply? Common mistake: using debounce on scroll (feels laggy) or throttle on search (fires mid-typing, wastes requests). Both are available in Lodash/Underscore and can be hand-rolled in ~10 lines.

📄 Evidence: CSS-Tricks — Debouncing and Throttling Explained · Lodash — debounce · Lodash — throttle

#0. How to read this prep — Q&A format key

Every interview question in this doc follows a 4-part structure:

  1. Q — the question (boldface).
  2. A: — concise spoken answer (1–3 sentences or table).
  3. Why it matters (rationale) — when this comes up, what trade-off or trap is being probed. Often inline or implicit in the answer text; new sections (Part A.5, Part D, Part E.5, Part G, Part G.7) call it out explicitly.
  4. 📄 Evidence — official documentation, blog post, or canonical reference for further reading.

When rationale is implicit in the A, the answer text contains the why directly (e.g., "Use for X because Y"). When rationale needs more weight (architecture decisions, gotchas, banking-specific concerns), it appears as its own line.

#1. JD Distilled — What They Actually Test

Priority Area Why it matters in this JD
🔴 HIGH React.js + TypeScript Primary strength. "2+ yrs React/Next with TS, NextJS preferred"
🔴 HIGH JS ES6, HTML5, CSS3 "Efficient in all areas of web dev"
🔴 HIGH Performant/testable/reusable code "Software engineering fundamentals"
🔴 HIGH Unit testing (Jest + Testing Library) Explicit criterion
🟠 MED WCAG 2.0 AA Accessibility Explicit; "intermediate ok"
🟠 MED REST API design, Microservices "Strong understanding"
🟠 MED Cloud (AWS/GCP/Azure), CI/CD, type system DevOps skillset
🟡 LOW Java 8+, Spring Boot "Nice-to-have / willingness required" — you only AI-code this
🟡 LOW GraphQL, Banking domain Desirable

Strategy: Be deep on React/TS/JS (your strength → score high). Be competent on Java — show structured fundamentals + genuine willingness to learn, NOT fake expertise. They explicitly said Java is "willingness required," so honesty + learning attitude wins culture points.

#Spoken-answer formula (use for every technical Q)

For every conceptual question, structure your spoken answer in this exact order — keeps you crisp and senior-sounding:

  1. Definition — one sentence, technical but plain.
  2. When to use — the canonical scenario.
  3. Trade-off — what you give up vs the alternative.
  4. Example — a real codebase / enterprise-style use case.

"Closures are functions that remember their lexical scope after the outer function returns (def). I use them for private state, factories, and React hooks (when). Trade-off: captured variables can leak memory if you keep stale references (trade). Example: a debounce wrapper captures the timer ID in its closure across calls (example)."

Live coding mindset:

  • Correctness first, then clarity, then semantic HTML/a11y, then edge cases.
  • If unsure on latest version: anchor on fundamentals first, then add "latest on stable is React 19.2 / Next.js 16.x".
  • Always speak your reasoning out loud — the interviewer scores communication, not just code.

#1.5.2 — WCAG 2.0 vs 2.1 — small but JD-specific nuance

Q-JD-1. The JD says "WCAG 2.0 Level AA" — what's different from 2.1, and does it matter? A: WCAG 2.1 (2018) is a superset of 2.0 (2008) — every 2.0 success criterion is in 2.1 unchanged. 2.1 added 17 new criteria, mostly mobile + low-vision (target size ≥ 44×44, content reflow at 320px, autocomplete attributes, status messages via aria-live). If you target 2.1 AA you automatically meet 2.0 AA. Why it matters: many enterprise JDs are conservative in wording ("2.0 AA"), but every modern audit tool (axe, Lighthouse) defaults to 2.1. State 2.0 AA confidently and add: "Our build also meets 2.1 AA which extends 2.0 with mobile + zoom criteria". 📄 w3.org/TR/WCAG21 → "What's New in 2.1"; w3.org/TR/WCAG20.

#1.5.2a — Modern WCAG 2.2 plan for frontend implementation

Q-JD-1a. What is the latest WCAG version, and what should I say in a modern frontend interview? A: As of 2026-06-06, the latest stable WCAG version is WCAG 2.2. It became a W3C Recommendation on 2023-10-05; W3C's WCAG Overview and FAQ were updated in May 2026 and still explicitly encourage teams to use the latest version of WCAG 2.2. WCAG 3.0 is not ready yet — W3C says it is still years away from completion. So the safest interview answer is: "If a JD says WCAG 2.0 AA, I satisfy that baseline, but for modern frontend work I design and test against WCAG 2.2 because it is the current W3C recommendation and remains backward-compatible with 2.1 and 2.0." Why it matters: This shows standards awareness without sounding like you're correcting the JD. It also lets you talk about newer 2.2 concerns that matter in real apps: focus visibility, target size, dragging alternatives, redundant entry, consistent help, and accessible authentication. 📄 Evidence: W3C WCAG Overview · W3C WCAG 2 FAQ · What's New in WCAG 2.2

#Why we should have WCAG

Reason Practical frontend meaning
It gives a shared quality bar Designers, frontend, QA, and content use one standard instead of subjective "looks accessible to me" calls.
It reduces exclusion in real usage WAI frames accessibility around how people actually perceive, navigate, understand, and operate interfaces across visual, auditory, physical, speech, and cognitive needs.
It improves engineering decisions early Proper semantics, labels, headings, tables, focus order, and keyboard support are cheaper to build in from the start than retrofit later.
It covers modern app risk better in 2.2 WCAG 2.2 adds focus visibility, target size, drag alternatives, redundant entry, consistent help, and accessible authentication concerns that hit modern SPA flows directly.
It is future-safe for older specs W3C says content that conforms to WCAG 2.2 also conforms to 2.1 and 2.0, so targeting 2.2 is the cleanest way to satisfy older policy wording.

#How can we implement it in real frontend code?

Component / pattern What to do in real code What to avoid
Buttons, links, icon buttons Use native <button> for actions and <a> for navigation. Give icon-only buttons an accessible name. Keep visible focus and minimum target size. Clickable <div> / <span>, removing focus outline, tiny hit areas, relying on color only.
Forms (input, select, checkbox, radio) Use <label> + id, aria-describedby for helper/error text, inline error identification, autocomplete where meaningful, and preserve entered values when possible. Placeholder-as-label, unlabeled controls, only red borders for errors, wiping user input after validation failure.
Modal / dialog On open: move focus into the dialog. Trap Tab within it. Support Escape. On close: restore focus to the trigger. Use role="dialog" + aria-modal="true" + visible title. See Drill B in §9.5. Leaving focus behind the overlay, no Escape key, focusing document.body, non-semantic close controls.
Autocomplete / combobox Treat it as an input with an associated popup, with correct expanded/collapsed state, active option tracking, keyboard arrows, Enter, Escape, and live updates only when needed. See Q-GFE-16 in §9.7. Fake dropdowns with no keyboard support, stuffing ARIA on a generic div tree, announcing too much in aria-live.
Tabs / accordion Use the APG keyboard model: semantic trigger buttons, state exposure (aria-selected / aria-expanded), and predictable focus movement. See Q-GFE-13 and Q-GFE-14. Tabs made from plain text nodes, mouse-only switching, hiding state visually only.
Data tables / dense data Prefer native HTML <table> whenever the data is actually tabular. Use real headers and only add sorting state such as aria-sort when interactive. Rebuilding tables from nested divs just for styling, using table roles when native HTML would work, no header associations.
Toasts / status messages / async save Use aria-live / status regions intentionally for save success, validation status, and background updates. Keep announcements short and deduplicated. Silent async changes, multiple competing live regions, announcing entire screen fragments on every render.
Drag-and-drop / sortable UIs Provide a non-drag alternative such as move up/down buttons or a menu action, because WCAG 2.2 requires a simple pointer alternative for drag interactions. Drag-only reorder flows, tiny drag handles, no keyboard or alternate path.
Authentication / verification flows Offer alternatives to cognitive-function tests, support paste/password managers/one-time-code autofill, and avoid forcing users to memorize or re-enter data unnecessarily. CAPTCHA or OTP flows that block paste, disabling password managers, redundant re-entry without reason.

#Best practice vs bad practice

Best practice Bad practice
Semantic HTML first; add ARIA only when HTML has no native equivalent Using ARIA to patch broken semantics
Build focus management into routing, overlays, menus, and async flows Treating focus as a browser problem, then debugging it late
Keep accessible names, descriptions, and error text stable and intentional Auto-generating noisy screen-reader text from random DOM fragments
Test with automation and manual keyboard / screen-reader / zoom checks Assuming axe/Lighthouse alone proves accessibility
Bake a11y contracts into shared components in the design system Fixing every screen ad hoc and reintroducing the same bug elsewhere
Prefer native controls before custom widgets Re-creating buttons, selects, dialogs, tabs, or tables from divs for styling freedom

#Some aspects WCAG does not fully cover, and we still need to cover in code

Gap beyond "pass the checklist" What we should do in code / process
Conformance does not guarantee full usability W3C explicitly recommends usability testing in addition to success-criteria testing. Include people with disabilities in test groups when possible.
Support varies across browser + assistive-technology combinations Test real combinations that matter to the product; APG itself says its examples are not production-ready code and must be tested before production use.
Product workflow quality Make multi-step flows resilient: preserve progress, prevent accidental destructive actions, restore focus sensibly, and expose pending/success/error states clearly.
Cognitive load beyond narrow pass/fail checks Keep help in the same place, reduce redundant data entry, use plain language, and keep error recovery obvious, especially in banking/auth flows.
Design-system enforcement Encode labels, focus rings, keyboard behavior, live-region behavior, and size constraints into shared components so teams cannot easily regress them.
Performance that affects accessibility Keep interactions responsive, avoid layout shifts that hide focus, and ensure loading states do not trap or confuse assistive-tech users.

Interview soundbite: "WCAG is my baseline, not my finish line. I start with semantic HTML, layer ARIA only for real widgets, encode the rules into shared components, and then manually test focus, keyboard flow, zoom, and screen-reader announcements on the actual user journey."

Standards-specific details worth dropping in an interview:

  • WCAG 2.2 adds 9 new success criteria over 2.1.
  • WCAG 2.2 makes 4.1.1 Parsing obsolete/removed.
  • W3C says WCAG 2.2 content also conforms to 2.1 and 2.0.
  • W3C says WCAG 3 is still years away, so teams should not wait for it.

📄 Evidence: W3C Accessibility Principles · WAI-ARIA APG Introduction · WAI-ARIA Combobox Pattern · WAI-ARIA Dialog Pattern · WAI-ARIA Table Pattern · Understanding Conformance

#1.5.3 — Gap-fill Q&A (technologies named in JD but missing from the prep)

#GraphQL (JD: "RESTful APIs design (GraphQL desirable)")

Q-JD-2. What is GraphQL and how does it differ from REST?

REST GraphQL
Endpoint shape Many URLs (/users, /users/1/posts) Single endpoint /graphql
Over-/under-fetching Common (rigid response) Client picks exact fields
Schema Optional (OpenAPI) Mandatory (SDL) — strong typing end-to-end
Caching HTTP cache friendly Client-cache (Apollo, urql, Relay)
Versioning URL /v2/... Schema evolution + deprecation
Mutations / queries HTTP verbs Explicit query / mutation / subscription
Real-time SSE / WebSocket bolt-on subscription first-class
graphql
# Schema (server)
type Account { id: ID!, balance: Float!, owner: User! }
type Query { account(id: ID!): Account }
type Mutation { transfer(from: ID!, to: ID!, amount: Float!): Boolean! }
ts
// Client (Apollo)
const { data, loading } = useQuery(gql`
  query GetAccount($id: ID!) {
    account(id: $id) { id balance owner { name } }
  }
`, { variables: { id: '123' } });

Why: Enterprise BFF gateways often combine REST + GraphQL. Common interview probe: "When would you choose GraphQL over REST?" → answer: client-driven mobile apps + heterogeneous frontends with different field needs. Trade-offs: complex caching, query-cost / N+1 risks (mitigate via DataLoader). 📄 graphql.org/learn; apollographql.com/docs; "GraphQL: The Documentary".

Q-JD-3. GraphQL N+1 problem and DataLoader? A: A naive resolver hits the DB per-parent (users → 100 users → 100 posts queries). DataLoader batches + deduplicates within a single request tick, turning N+1 into 2 queries. 📄 github.com/graphql/dataloader.

#Other clouds (JD image: GCP, AWS or Azure)

Q-JD-4. What are the GCP equivalents to the AWS services I named in Q56?

Concern AWS GCP Azure
Compute (VM) EC2 Compute Engine Virtual Machines
Object storage S3 Cloud Storage Blob Storage
Serverless functions Lambda Cloud Functions / Cloud Run Azure Functions
Managed SQL RDS Cloud SQL Azure SQL Database
NoSQL DynamoDB Firestore / Bigtable Cosmos DB
CDN CloudFront Cloud CDN Azure CDN
IAM IAM Cloud IAM Entra ID (was AD)
API gateway API Gateway API Gateway / Apigee API Management
Container orchestration ECS / EKS GKE AKS
Secrets Secrets Manager / SSM Secret Manager Key Vault
Pub/sub SNS / SQS / Kinesis Pub/Sub Service Bus / Event Grid

Why: "Cloud First" mantras are platform-agnostic — JDs often list all three. State you have AWS depth + structural understanding of the equivalents on GCP/Azure. 📄 cloud.google.com → "GCP for AWS Professionals"; learn.microsoft.com → "Azure for AWS Professionals".

Q-JD-5. How would you deploy a Next.js app to each of the three clouds?

Cloud Path
AWS ECS Fargate (Docker) + ALB + CloudFront, or Amplify Hosting, or OpenNext → Lambda
GCP Cloud Run (containerized — best fit) + Cloud CDN, or App Engine Flex
Azure Container Apps / App Service for Containers + Azure CDN

All three: source maps disabled, env vars in cloud secret store, IaC via Terraform. 📄 nextjs.org/docs → "Deploying"; OpenNext docs.

#Frontend testing tools (JD: "Selenium, Mocha/Chai/Jest/Playwright/Cypress")

Q-JD-6. Compare Jest vs Mocha+Chai vs Vitest — when to use which.

Jest Mocha + Chai Vitest
Bundled runner ✅ ❌ — need Mocha runner + Chai assertions + sinon mocks ✅
Speed OK OK Fastest (Vite-powered, ESM-native)
Snapshot ✅ Plugin ✅
TS / ESM support OK (ts-jest) OK Native
Most React projects ✅ default Older / pre-CRA codebases Modern Vite/Next.js apps

Soundbite: "Jest is my default — comes with runner, assertions, mocks, snapshots in one package. Mocha+Chai is the older à-la-carte alternative, often paired with Sinon for spies. Vitest is the modern speed-first replacement, native ESM." 📄 jestjs.io; mochajs.org; chaijs.com; vitest.dev.

Q-JD-7. End-to-end testing — Cypress vs Playwright vs Selenium?

Cypress Playwright Selenium
Architecture Runs IN the browser (no driver) Drives browsers via DevTools Protocol WebDriver protocol
Browsers Chromium, Firefox, WebKit (limited) All 3 (Chromium / WebKit / Firefox) All major
Multi-tab / multi-domain Limited ✅ ✅
API JS / TS JS / TS / Python / .NET / Java All major langs
Speed Fast Fastest Slowest (legacy WebDriver protocol)
Best for Single-page React apps with strong DX Cross-browser, multi-tab, parallel grids Java-heavy enterprise pipelines
ts
// Playwright sample — banking transfer flow
test('user can transfer money', async ({ page }) => {
  await page.goto('/dashboard');
  await page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Transfer' }).click();
  await page.getByLabel('Amount').fill('100');
  await page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Submit' }).click();
  await expect(page.getByText('Transfer successful')).toBeVisible();
});

Why: JDs commonly list all three. Playwright is the modern recommendation; Selenium still appears in legacy Java-based test suites; Cypress is loved for React DX. 📄 playwright.dev; cypress.io; selenium.dev.

Q-JD-8. What are mocks vs spies vs stubs in test frameworks?

  • Spy — wrap a real function; observe calls without changing behavior (jest.spyOn(api, 'fetchUser'))
  • Stub — replace return value (spy.mockReturnValue(fakeUser))
  • Mock — full fake implementation, often whole module (jest.mock('./api')) 📄 Sinon docs (canonical glossary); Jest "Mock Functions".

Q-JD-9. What is snapshot testing — and what are the traps? A: expect(tree).toMatchSnapshot() writes a serialized version on first run; future runs compare. Trap: snapshots become rubber-stamped — devs commit the diff without reading. Better: targeted assertions on roles/text via React Testing Library; reserve snapshots for stable UI primitives. 📄 jestjs.io → "Snapshot Testing".

#Legacy frontend stack named in JD (Bootstrap, Foundation, jQuery)

Q-JD-10. The JD lists Bootstrap, Foundation, jQuery — am I expected to use these on day one? A: No — these appear because large enterprises run legacy systems alongside modern React/Next.js. Be ready to speak about them at a sentence level:

Tech What it is Modern equivalent in your stack
Bootstrap CSS framework with utility classes + components (modal, navbar) Tailwind CSS / shadcn/ui / klara-theme
Foundation Zurb's CSS framework, less popular than Bootstrap Same
jQuery DOM manipulation + AJAX library (pre-modern-JS) Native fetch + document.querySelector + React
JSON Data interchange format — string ↔ JS object via JSON.parse / JSON.stringify Still ubiquitous

Soundbite: "I'm primarily Tailwind + shadcn/ui on greenfield, but I've maintained Bootstrap codebases — both share the utility-class mental model. I haven't used jQuery in years; in legacy refactors I replace $('.foo').on('click', ...) with React event handlers + refs." Why: shows pragmatism and familiarity without overclaiming. 📄 getbootstrap.com; get.foundation; jquery.com.

#UI design literacy (JD image criterion)

Q-JD-11. The JD says "UI design literacy" — what does that mean and how do I demonstrate it? A: UI design literacy = knowing the vocabulary and principles even if you don't draw the mockups. Key items:

Topic What to know
Design tokens Color/spacing/typography vars; theming via CSS custom properties
Visual hierarchy Size, contrast, whitespace, alignment, repetition
Type scale Modular ratio (1.125, 1.25, 1.333) for h1→p sizes
Color theory Primary / secondary / semantic (success/warning/danger), 4.5:1 contrast
Spacing scale 4-pt or 8-pt grid (4/8/12/16/24/32...)
Component anatomy Button states (default/hover/focus/active/disabled/loading), input affordances
Responsive Mobile-first breakpoints, container queries (see Q31e)
Design systems Atomic design (atoms → molecules → organisms), Material, Apple HIG
Tools you can name Figma, Storybook, Chromatic
Banking-relevant In-house design system, accessibility-first, dense-data tables

Demonstrate by referencing your work: "I built X following our design tokens; collaborated with designers in Figma; documented variants in Storybook with a11y annotations." 📄 Material Design guidelines; Apple HIG; Refactoring UI by Adam Wathan; "Atomic Design" by Brad Frost.

#1.5.4 — JD soft requirements (communication, code review, architecture, prototyping)

Q-JD-12. The JD emphasizes "global development teams" + "strong English communication" — how to score well? A: Three behaviors interviewers grade:

  1. Repeat-back / clarify — paraphrase the question before answering: "So the constraint here is X — is that right?". Catches miscommunication and shows attentive listening.
  2. Structured speaking — Definition → When → Trade-off → Example (the same 4-step formula in §1).
  3. Async-friendly habits — mention writing clear PR descriptions, ADRs (Architecture Decision Records), thorough commit messages, design docs in Confluence/Notion.

Watchouts: don't over-fill silence; pause to think is fine; ask "want me to go deeper on X?" before tangenting. Why: Distributed teams collaborate across timezones — written precision matters as much as spoken fluency. 📄 "Writing for Engineers" (Stripe blog); ADR template (github.com/joelparkerhenderson/architecture-decision-record).

Q-JD-13. The JD says "peer reviews of others' code, recommend changes, challenge decisions" — how do I show this in interview? A: Frame your code-review philosophy with three tiers, signaling you can be direct but kind:

Tier Signal in your comment
Blocker "This is unsafe / breaks contract / missing test for X." Must fix before merge.
Suggestion "Consider Y because Z." Non-blocking; author decides.
Nit / praise "Nit: rename for clarity" / "Nice handling of edge case." Optional.

Tactics that win: review PRs small and often, ask questions instead of declaring ("Curious why you chose X over Y?"), back claims with evidence (link the doc/MDN), separate style from correctness, push back when wrong but yield gracefully when convinced. Have a STAR story ready: "In a PR I disagreed with the auth approach — I asked for a 15-min sync, sketched the threat model, and we adopted a middle-ground design." Why: Enterprise panels explicitly grade "ability to challenge" — the JD calls it out twice. 📄 Google's "Code Review Developer Guide" (google.github.io/eng-practices/review); "Conventional Comments" (conventionalcomments.org).

Q-JD-14. The JD says "freedom to make architectural and code-level decisions about the component you're working on" — how do I demonstrate component-level architecture chops? A: Show you decompose with intent:

  1. Boundary first — what does this component own, what does it delegate? (See Q15h's Repository / Service / BFF / Provider table.)
  2. Public API — props/types, events, slots — minimize surface area.
  3. State location — colocate vs lift vs URL vs Context (Q20k, Q20l, Q15e).
  4. Side-effects isolation — fetch in Server Component or hook layer; UI is presentational.
  5. Test seams — repository/service interfaces are where you mock.
  6. Failure modes — loading/empty/error are first-class, not afterthoughts.
  7. Documentation — Storybook + ADR ("Why we picked compound components over render props for <Tabs>").

Banking example to volunteer: "For the transfer-confirmation modal, I separated <TransferModal> (UI/a11y/portal) from useTransfer() (state machine: idle → confirming → submitting → success/error). The hook is testable in isolation; the modal can be reused elsewhere." Why: Enterprise teams grant component-level architectural freedom — panels want to see the decision-making muscle. 📄 ADR template; "Decisions, Decisions" by Sarah Mei; the Repository / Service / BFF section in Q15h.

Q-JD-15. The JD mentions prototyping — how to talk about it? A: Prototypes ≠ MVPs. A prototype:

  • Tests one specific risk (technical feasibility, UX flow, perf budget)
  • Has explicit throw-away discipline — code is not promoted to prod
  • Lives in a sandbox (Storybook story, CodeSandbox, separate branch) and is time-boxed

Common prototype types in banking: rapid Figma → React POC for stakeholder demo; performance spike (can we render 50k rows?); third-party SDK evaluation (e.g., comparing Stripe Elements vs Adyen). Outcome doc captures the learning, not the code. Why: Agile processes expect spikes/prototypes during planning. Speaking about discipline (kill-criteria, time-box, write-up) signals seniority. 📄 "Prototyping" — Lean UX; Spike (XP).

#3. Part A — React / Next.js / TypeScript Q&A

Format per question: Q → A (concise spoken answer) → 📄 Evidence (doc to cite/read).

#React Core

Q1. Virtual DOM — what & why? And what is Fiber? A: "Virtual DOM" is the conceptual model — React keeps an in-memory representation of the UI tree and applies only minimal real-DOM mutations. But the internal implementation since React 16 is Fiber Architecture, which replaced the old Stack Reconciler.

Old Stack Reconciler (pre-React 16): reconciliation was a synchronous recursive call stack — once started, it ran to completion and couldn't be paused. This caused jank on large trees.

Fiber Reconciler (React 16+, current): each React element has a Fiber node (a plain JS object). The tree is a linked list (child → sibling → return), not a call stack. This allows React to:

  • Pause render work mid-tree and resume later (time slicing — 25ms slices for transitions)
  • Abort low-priority renders when higher-priority work arrives
  • Prioritize via a 31-bit Lanes bitmask (SyncLane for clicks > DefaultLane for setState > TransitionLane for startTransition)
  • Double-buffer via current ↔ workInProgress tree swap — UI only sees completed work

Two phases: Render phase (pure, interruptible — beginWork / reconcileChildren) → Commit phase (synchronous, side effects — DOM mutation, useLayoutEffect, then schedule useEffect).

When an interviewer says "Virtual DOM" — acknowledge the concept, then show depth: "React still uses the in-memory tree concept, but the implementation is Fiber since React 16 — a linked list that enables interruption and prioritization, which is how concurrent features like useTransition and Suspense work."

📄 react.dev → "Render and Commit"; github.com/acdlite/react-fiber-architecture (original Fiber design doc); react.dev → "useTransition".

Q2. useState vs useRef — difference? A: useState triggers re-render on change, value is snapshotted per render. useRef is a mutable box (.current) that persists across renders but does NOT trigger re-render. Use ref for DOM nodes, timers, previous-value tracking, any mutable value the UI doesn't depend on. 📄 react.dev → "Referencing Values with Refs".

Q2a. useState vs useReducer — when to use which?

useState useReducer
Use when Simple: boolean, string, number, 1-2 fields Complex: many related fields, many action types
Update logic setState(newValue) direct dispatch(action) → reducer function
Testability Inline logic Reducer is pure function — easy to unit test
tsx
// useState — simple toggle
const [isOpen, setIsOpen] = useState(false);

// useReducer — shopping cart with multiple actions
type Action =
  | { type: 'add'; item: Product }
  | { type: 'remove'; id: string }
  | { type: 'clear' };

function cartReducer(state: Product[], action: Action): Product[] {
  switch (action.type) {
    case 'add':    return [...state, action.item];
    case 'remove': return state.filter(p => p.id !== action.id);
    case 'clear':  return [];
  }
}
const [cart, dispatch] = useReducer(cartReducer, []);
dispatch({ type: 'add', item: product });

Rule: when setState logic needs conditionals, depends on previous state in multiple ways, or the same "action" affects multiple fields → switch to useReducer. 📄 react.dev → "Extracting State Logic into a Reducer".

Q3. useEffect — when does it run, and the dependency array? A: Runs after commit (paint). [] = once after mount. [a,b] = after mount + whenever a or b change (Object.is compare). No array = every render. Cleanup function runs before next effect + on unmount. Common bugs: missing deps (stale closure), object/array deps causing infinite loops (new ref each render). Fix with useMemo/useCallback or move logic out. 📄 react.dev → "Synchronizing with Effects" + "You Might Not Need an Effect" (critical read — interviewers love this).

Q4. useMemo vs useCallback? A: useMemo(fn, deps) memoizes a computed value. useCallback(fn, deps) memoizes a function reference (equiv. useMemo(() => fn, deps)). Use to avoid expensive recompute, or to keep stable references for memoized children / effect deps. Don't premature-optimize — profile first. 📄 react.dev → "useMemo", "useCallback".

Q5. Why do we need key in lists? What's wrong with index as key? A: key lets React match elements across renders for stable identity. Index-as-key breaks when list reorders/inserts/deletes — React reuses the wrong DOM node, causing stale state in inputs, wrong animations. Use a stable unique id. 📄 react.dev → "Rendering Lists".

Q6. Controlled vs uncontrolled components? A: Controlled = form value driven by React state (value + onChange), single source of truth, easy validation. Uncontrolled = DOM holds the value, read via ref. Controlled preferred for most forms; uncontrolled for simple/perf-sensitive or file inputs. 📄 react.dev → "Reacting to Input with State".

Q7. What is a custom hook? Rules of Hooks? A: A function starting with use that composes built-in hooks to share stateful logic. Rules: call hooks only at top level (not in loops/conditions/nested fns), only from React functions. Enforced by eslint-plugin-react-hooks. Reason: React tracks hooks by call order.

All hooks quick reference:

Hook Purpose
useState Local state — triggers re-render
useReducer Complex state with multiple action types
useEffect Side effects (sync with external systems)
useRef Mutable value without re-render, DOM access
useContext Consume Context value
useMemo Cache expensive computed value
useCallback Cache function reference
useTransition Mark update as non-urgent (interruptible)
useDeferredValue Defer a value to keep UI responsive
useId Generate stable unique IDs for accessibility (SSR-safe)
useActionState Manage form action state (React 19)
useOptimistic Show optimistic value during async action (React 19)
useFormStatus Read pending state from parent <form> (React 19)

useId — why it matters for accessibility:

tsx
// useId generates stable id across server/client (no hydration mismatch)
function FormField({ label }: { label: string }) {
  const id = useId(); // e.g., ":r0:"
  return (
    <>
      <label htmlFor={id}>{label}</label>
      <input id={id} type="text" />
    </>
  );
}
// Safe to render multiple times — each gets unique id

Don't use for list key. Only for DOM id attributes requiring uniqueness. 📄 react.dev → "Reusing Logic with Custom Hooks", "Rules of Hooks", "useId".

Q8. How does Context work? When does it cause re-renders? A: createContext + <Provider value> + useContext. Every consumer re-renders when the provider's value changes identity. Pitfall: inline object value value={{a,b}} = new ref each render → all consumers re-render. Fix: useMemo the value, or split contexts. Context ≠ state manager; for complex global state use Redux Toolkit/Zustand. 📄 react.dev → "Passing Data Deeply with Context", "Scaling Up with Reducer and Context".

Q9. React 18 → React 19 → 19.2 — what changed across versions?

React 18 (2022): Automatic batching (setStates in promises/timeouts batched → one render). useTransition/startTransition — mark work as non-urgent (TransitionLane → interruptible). useDeferredValue. Streaming SSR with Suspense. Concurrent renderer enabled by default.

React 19 (current stable, 19.x): Focus on forms + server-oriented primitives:

  • useActionState(action, initialState) — manages pending/result state for async actions (form submits). Replaces manual loading/error state boilerplate.
  • useOptimistic(state, updateFn) — show optimistic value immediately while async action runs; auto-reverts on error.
  • useFormStatus() — reads pending state from a parent <form> action (inside form children only).
  • use(promise | context) — call inside render to await a promise or read context conditionally.
  • ref as regular prop (no more forwardRef needed for function components).
  • Improved hydration error messages.

React 19.2 (latest, 2026): Additional primitives:

  • <Activity visible={bool}> — preserves component state while hidden (replaces offscreen DOM removal). Replaces the old "offscreen" proposal. OffscreenLane.
  • useEffectEvent(fn) — extract non-reactive logic from effects without adding to dep array. Replaces useRef hack for event handlers in effects.
  • cacheSignal — AbortSignal tied to React's cache lifecycle (for cache() function + Server Components).
  • React Performance Tracks — DevTools profiler timing lanes.
  • Partial Pre-rendering (PPR) APIs in React DOM (static shell + streaming dynamic slots).
tsx
// React 19 — useActionState example
import { useActionState } from 'react';

async function submitPayment(prevState, formData) {
  const amount = formData.get('amount');
  const res = await fetch('/api/pay', { method: 'POST', body: JSON.stringify({ amount }) });
  if (!res.ok) return { error: 'Payment failed' };
  return { success: true };
}

function PaymentForm() {
  const [state, action, isPending] = useActionState(submitPayment, null);
  return (
    <form action={action}>
      <input name="amount" type="number" />
      <button type="submit" disabled={isPending}>
        {isPending ? 'Processing...' : 'Pay'}
      </button>
      {state?.error && <p>{state.error}</p>}
    </form>
  );
}

// useOptimistic — banking context: instant balance update
function TransferButton({ balance, onTransfer }) {
  const [optimisticBalance, updateOptimistic] = useOptimistic(
    balance,
    (current, transferred) => current - transferred
  );
  return (
    <form action={async (data) => {
      const amount = Number(data.get('amount'));
      updateOptimistic(amount); // show immediately
      await onTransfer(amount); // real async call
    }}>
      <p>Balance: ${optimisticBalance}</p>
      <input name="amount" /><button>Transfer</button>
    </form>
  );
}

📄 react.dev → "React 19 Blog Post"; react.dev → "useActionState", "useOptimistic", "useFormStatus", "use"; react.dev → "Activity" (19.2 release notes).

Q10. Performance optimization techniques in React? A: React.memo for pure components (shallow prop compare → bailout), useMemo/useCallback for stable refs, code-splitting via React.lazy + Suspense, virtualization (react-window) for long lists, useTransition for low-priority state updates, useDeferredValue to show stale UI while new value computes. Avoid inline objects/functions as props to memoized children (new ref = cache miss). Move state down so re-renders stay local. Profile with React DevTools Profiler before optimizing — premature memo is maintenance cost for no gain.

Children composition pattern (free optimization):

tsx
// BAD: ExpensiveTree re-renders when count changes
function App() {
  const [count, setCount] = useState(0);
  return <Layout><ExpensiveTree /></Layout>; // ExpensiveTree inside App = re-renders with App
}

// GOOD: pass ExpensiveTree as children — its JSX was created by CALLER, not App
function App({ children }) {
  const [count, setCount] = useState(0);
  return <Layout>{children}</Layout>; // children ref stable → no re-render
}
<App><ExpensiveTree /></App>

📄 react.dev → "Render and Commit"; "useMemo", "useCallback"; Dan Abramov "Before You memo()" article.

#Fiber Deep-dive (bonus depth — shows senior-level React knowledge)

Q10a. Explain Fiber reconciliation step-by-step. A: When setState fires, React schedules work on the current fiber and starts reconciliation on a WorkInProgress tree. Important interview correction: Fiber did not mainly change React from O(n^2) to O(n). Generic tree-diff algorithms are closer to O(n^3); React has long used an O(n) heuristic based on two assumptions: different element types produce different subtrees, and stable keys let React match children across renders. Fiber changed how React walks and schedules that work: from one recursive synchronous pass to interruptible units of work.

text
setState() called
  → requestUpdateLane()      — assign priority (SyncLane / DefaultLane / TransitionLane)
  → scheduleUpdateOnFiber()  — mark fiber + ancestors as having pending work

RENDER PHASE (interruptible — pure, no side effects):
  → createWorkInProgress(currentFiber) — double-buffer: clone current fiber to WIP
  → workLoopConcurrent():
      while (workInProgress && now() < deadline) {
        performUnitOfWork(fiber)
          → beginWork(): call component fn, compare props old/new
              → props unchanged + no pending update → BAILOUT (skip entire subtree!)
              → props changed → reconcileChildren(): create/update/delete child fibers
          → completeWork(): bubble effects flags up to parent
        — Check 25ms time slice; yield to browser if exceeded (requestIdleCallback / MessageChannel)
      }

COMMIT PHASE (synchronous — no interrupt):
  → commitMutationEffects()  — write DOM: insertions, updates, deletions
  → root.current = finishedWork  ← atomic pointer swap (double-buffer complete)
  → commitLayoutEffects()    — useLayoutEffect, componentDidMount/Update
  → schedule useEffect()     — async, after paint

How to explain the old reconciler vs Fiber in interview:

  1. Generic tree diff is too expensive. A minimal-edit diff across two arbitrary trees is much more expensive than React can afford, so React does not try to compute the perfect tree edit script.
  2. React uses an O(n) heuristic. Same type means reuse and update. Different type means tear down and remount. Children are compared left-to-right, and key lets React preserve identity when items move.
  3. Before Fiber, React still used that heuristic, but it walked the updated subtree recursively in one synchronous tick. Once the old stack reconciler started rendering, it could not pause halfway to let more urgent work run first.
  4. Fiber keeps the same matching rules but changes the execution model. Each fiber is a unit of work linked by child, sibling, and return, so React can pause, resume, abort stale work, and prioritize urgent updates.
  5. The practical win is responsiveness. Typing, clicking, and other urgent updates can preempt background rendering, while the final DOM commit still happens atomically once the render phase completes.

Short interview line: "React did not really go from O(n^2) to O(n) because of Fiber. React already relied on an O(n) heuristic; Fiber reimplemented the reconciler so that same heuristic work could be scheduled incrementally and prioritized."

Key vocabulary to use in interview (NOT "Virtual DOM diffing"):

Old phrase (avoid) Correct phrase
"Builds new Virtual DOM" "Creates WorkInProgress fiber tree"
"Diffs Virtual DOM" "beginWork() + reconcileChildren() on each fiber"
"Patches DOM" "commitMutationEffects() in commit phase"
"Virtual DOM tree" "Fiber linked list (child→sibling→return)"

Q10b. How does startTransition / useTransition work at the Fiber level? A: startTransition marks updates as TransitionLane (low priority, interruptible). When user triggers a SyncLane update (e.g., keypress) mid-transition render, React aborts the in-progress TransitionLane render and starts fresh with SyncLane first. The UI stays responsive. isPending = true while transition work is queued/in-flight.

tsx
const [isPending, startTransition] = useTransition();
// Typing in search box:
startTransition(() => {
  setResults(filterLargeDataset(query)); // TransitionLane — can be interrupted
});
// If user types again → React aborts the filter render, re-runs with new query

📄 react.dev → "useTransition"; github.com/acdlite/react-fiber-architecture.

Q10c. When does React skip (bailout) a subtree re-render? A: React skips a component + its entire subtree when ALL 3 conditions hold:

  1. Props reference unchanged (or React.memo shallow-equal passes)
  2. No pending state update on that fiber
  3. No context value change
tsx
// React.memo + stable props = bailout
const MemoChild = React.memo(function Child({ data }) {
  console.log('render'); // only logs when data REF changes
  return <div>{data.name}</div>;
});

function Parent() {
  const [count, setCount] = useState(0);
  const data = useMemo(() => ({ name: 'static' }), []); // stable ref
  return (
    <>
      <p>{count}</p><button onClick={() => setCount(c => c+1)}>+</button>
      <MemoChild data={data} /> {/* BAILOUT — data ref never changes */}
    </>
  );
}

// Common mistake: inline object = new ref every render = memo useless
<MemoChild data={{ name: 'static' }} /> // ❌ new ref = no bailout

Bailout without memo — children composition (free, no memo needed):

tsx
// ExpensiveTree won't re-render because it's passed as children from OUTSIDE
function Counter({ children }) {
  const [count, setCount] = useState(0);
  return <><p>{count}</p><button onClick={() => setCount(c => c+1)}>+</button>{children}</>;
}
<Counter><ExpensiveTree /></Counter> // ExpensiveTree props never change → bailout

📄 react.dev → "React.memo"; Dan Abramov "Before You memo()".

#Next.js

Q11. App Router vs Pages Router? A: App Router (app/) = React Server Components by default, layouts, nested routing, streaming, server actions. Pages Router (pages/) = older, getServerSideProps/getStaticProps, client components only. New projects use App Router. 📄 nextjs.org/docs → "App Router" intro.

Q12. Server Components vs Client Components? A: Server Components render on server, zero JS shipped, can fetch data directly, no hooks/state/browser APIs. Client Components ('use client') run in browser, support state/effects/events. Default in App Router = Server. Push interactivity to leaf client components to minimize bundle. 📄 nextjs.org/docs → "Server and Client Components".

Q13. Rendering strategies — SSR, SSG, ISR, CSR? A: SSG = build-time HTML (fast, cacheable, static content). SSR = per-request HTML (fresh, personalized). ISR = static + revalidate after N seconds (best of both). CSR = render in browser (dashboards behind auth). Choose by data freshness vs performance. 📄 nextjs.org/docs → "Rendering" / "Caching and Revalidating".

Q14. How does data fetching work in App Router? A: async Server Components await fetch() directly. fetch is extended with caching (cache, next: { revalidate }). Mutations via Server Actions ('use server'). Use loading.tsx for Suspense fallbacks, error.tsx for error boundaries. 📄 nextjs.org/docs → "Data Fetching", "Server Actions and Mutations".

Q15. Next.js performance/SEO features? A: next/image (auto optimization, lazy load), next/font (no layout shift), generateMetadata for SEO, route prefetching via <Link>, automatic code splitting per route, streaming. Core Web Vitals: next/image + priority → LCP, useTransition → INP, next/font → CLS=0.

SEO APIs:

tsx
// Static metadata
export const metadata = { title: 'Page | App', description: '...', openGraph: { images: ['/og.png'] } };

// Dynamic metadata
export async function generateMetadata({ params }) {
  const product = await getProduct(params.id);
  return { title: product.name, description: product.excerpt };
}

// Template in layout.tsx — child pages inherit template
export const metadata = { title: { template: '%s | AcmeBank', default: 'AcmeBank' } };
// Child with title: 'Dashboard' → renders 'Dashboard | AcmeBank'

Sitemap + robots:

ts
// app/sitemap.ts
export default function sitemap(): MetadataRoute.Sitemap {
  return [{ url: 'https://example.com', lastModified: new Date(), priority: 1 }];
}
// app/robots.ts — auto-generated robots.txt

📄 nextjs.org/docs → "Optimizing", "Metadata API".

Q15a. Next.js 14→15→16 breaking changes — what changed?

14→15 breaking changes:

  1. params, searchParams, cookies(), headers() all became async Promises — must await:
tsx
// ❌ Next.js 14
export default function Page({ params }: { params: { id: string } }) {
  return <h1>{params.id}</h1>;
}
// ✅ Next.js 15+
export default async function Page({ params }: { params: Promise<{ id: string }> }) {
  const { id } = await params;
  return <h1>{id}</h1>;
}
// Also: const cookieStore = await cookies(); // was cookies().get() sync
  1. fetch() default changed from force-cache (14) → no-store (15). Must opt-in to cache explicitly.

15→16 breaking changes (cacheComponents):

  • experimental.ppr, experimental.useCache, experimental.dynamicIO all removed
  • New single flag: cacheComponents: true in next.config.ts
  • Old route segment config (export const revalidate, export const dynamic) errors when cacheComponents: true
  • New model: default = dynamic, opt-in to cache with "use cache" directive
tsx
// ❌ ERRORS with cacheComponents: true
export const revalidate = 60;
const data = await fetch(url, { cache: 'force-cache' });
const data = await fetch(url, { next: { revalidate: 60 } });

// ✅ Next.js 16 way
async function getData() {
  'use cache';
  import { cacheLife, cacheTag } from 'next/cache';
  cacheLife({ revalidate: 60 });
  cacheTag('my-data');
  return await fetch(url).then(r => r.json());
}

📄 nextjs.org/blog → "Next.js 15", "Next.js 16".

Q15b. Rendering strategies — when to use which?

Strategy When Code signal
SSG Static content (about, docs, blog) "use cache" no cacheLife (Next.js 16)
ISR CMS content, product catalog "use cache" + cacheLife({ revalidate: 60 })
SSR Personalized, search, checkout No directive (default = dynamic in Next.js 16)
Streaming SSR Multiple slow APIs on same page <Suspense> wrapping async components
PPR Dashboard with static layout + dynamic user data "use cache" on page + <Suspense> for dynamic holes
CSR Admin panels, no SEO, real-time "use client" + useEffect or React Query

Decision matrix:

text
Need SEO?           → YES → SSG/ISR/SSR/Streaming
Content static?     → YES → SSG
Content updates?    → YES → ISR (CMS webhook = revalidateTag)
Personalized?       → YES → SSR or PPR
Mix static+personal → PPR (static shell + dynamic Suspense holes)
Real-time/no SEO    → CSR

PPR flow (Next.js 16 killer feature for banking):

tsx
// app/account/page.tsx
"use cache"
import { Suspense } from 'react';
import { cacheLife } from 'next/cache';

export default async function AccountPage() {
  cacheLife({ revalidate: 300 }); // Layout cached 5min
  return (
    <main>
      <AccountLayout />          {/* Static shell — CDN ~50ms TTFB */}
      <Suspense fallback={<BalanceSkeleton />}>
        <LiveBalance />           {/* Dynamic hole — streams from server */}
      </Suspense>
      <Suspense fallback={<TxnSkeleton />}>
        <RecentTransactions />    {/* Dynamic hole — independent stream */}
      </Suspense>
    </main>
  );
}
// LiveBalance has no "use cache" → auto dynamic, uses cookies() for auth

Timeline: User sees layout instantly (CDN) → balance streams in ~200ms → transactions in ~400ms. 📄 nextjs.org/docs → "Rendering", "Caching", "Partial Prerendering".

Q15b-i. ISR stale-while-revalidate — walk me through exactly what each user sees.

revalidate = 60 seconds. Scenario:

text
Request 1 at t=0s   → Cache MISS (first request after build)
                      Server renders page, stores in cache, responds with fresh HTML.

Request 2 at t=30s  → Cache HIT (30s < 60s TTL)
                      Serves cached HTML instantly. No render.

Request 3 at t=65s  → Cache EXPIRED (65s > 60s TTL)
                      Serves STALE cached HTML immediately (user sees old data).
                      Simultaneously triggers background regeneration.

Request 4 at t=66s  → Background render completed.
                      Serves NEW cached HTML. All subsequent requests get fresh page.

Key insight: the user at Request 3 always gets a stale response — that is intentional and the trade-off of ISR. The regeneration is asynchronous, not blocking. This is "stale-while-revalidate": serve fast (stale), then update in background.

Spoken answer: "ISR serves the last cached version to every user, even after the TTL expires. The first request after expiry triggers background regeneration — that user still gets stale, the next user gets fresh. It's a performance-first trade-off: every request is fast, data is eventually consistent." 📄 nextjs.org/docs → "Incremental Static Regeneration".

Q15b-ii. Caching model comparison — Next.js 14 vs 15 vs 16.

Next.js 14 Next.js 15 Next.js 16 (cacheComponents: true)
Enable PPR experimental: { ppr: true } experimental: { ppr: true, useCache: true, dynamicIO: true } cacheComponents: true — single flag, replaces all
Per-route opt-in export const experimental_ppr = true Same Not needed — automatic
PPR status Canary only Stable channel, still experimental Production-ready
Default fetch behavior force-cache (auto-cached) no-store (not cached) Dynamic by default — no caching
Cache opt-in fetch(url, { cache: 'force-cache' }) Same, but must be explicit "use cache" directive on function/component
ISR (time-based) export const revalidate = N Same "use cache" + cacheLife({ revalidate: N })
On-demand invalidation revalidateTag() + fetch tag Same cacheTag('x') inside "use cache" + revalidateTag('x')
Force dynamic export const dynamic = 'force-dynamic' Same Default — no directive needed
Skip cache on fetch fetch(url, { cache: 'no-store' }) Same Default — no directive needed
Dynamic detection cookies() / headers() opt-out Same + dynamicIO flag Same — any non-cached async = dynamic

Migration mapping (14-15 → 16):

Old pattern Next.js 16 equivalent
export const revalidate = 60 "use cache" + cacheLife({ revalidate: 60 })
export const dynamic = 'force-dynamic' Remove — default is already dynamic
fetch(url, { cache: 'force-cache' }) "use cache" on the enclosing function
fetch(url, { next: { revalidate: 60 } }) "use cache" + cacheLife({ revalidate: 60 })
fetch(url, { cache: 'no-store' }) Remove — default is already no-cache
fetch(url, { next: { tags: ['x'] } }) cacheTag('x') inside a "use cache" function

📄 nextjs.org/blog → "Next.js 15", "Next.js 16"; nextjs.org/docs → "Caching".

Q15b-iii. cacheLife — what preset strings are available?

The documented named preset is 'hours' (revalidates every hour):

tsx
async function getData() {
  'use cache';
  cacheLife('hours'); // preset string — revalidates every ~1 hour
  return await fetch(url).then(r => r.json());
}

For precise control, use the object form:

tsx
cacheLife({ revalidate: 60 })    // 60 seconds
cacheLife({ revalidate: 3600 })  // 1 hour (equivalent to 'hours' preset)
cacheLife({ revalidate: 86400 }) // 24 hours

The object form accepts revalidate (seconds until background revalidation) and optionally stale (seconds the CDN/browser can serve stale). Object form is preferred in production — explicit, no dependency on preset naming. 📄 nextjs.org/docs → "cacheLife".

Q15b-iv. Server Component import constraint — why can a Client Component NOT import a Server Component?

Rule: A Client Component cannot import a Server Component directly. A Server Component can import a Client Component.

Why: Module imports are resolved at bundle time. If a Client Component imported a Server Component, the bundler would pull server-only code (DB clients, secrets, fs, Node.js APIs) into the client JS bundle — a security violation and a runtime error.

Wrong pattern:

tsx
// ClientWrapper.tsx — ERROR
'use client';
import { ServerCard } from './ServerCard'; // ServerCard calls db.query() — cannot bundle server code

export function ClientWrapper() {
  return <ServerCard />;
}

Correct pattern — pass Server Component as children:

tsx
// ServerParent.tsx (Server Component — no directive)
import { ClientWrapper } from './ClientWrapper'; // Server importing Client is fine

export default async function ServerParent() {
  const data = await db.query('...'); // server-only, safe
  return (
    <ClientWrapper>
      <ServerCard data={data} /> {/* Server Component passed as children */}
    </ClientWrapper>
  );
}

// ClientWrapper.tsx
'use client';
export function ClientWrapper({ children }: { children: React.ReactNode }) {
  const [open, setOpen] = useState(false);
  return <div onClick={() => setOpen(!open)}>{children}</div>;
  // `children` is a serialised RSC payload — no server code in client bundle
}

Why this works: children arrives as a serialised RSC payload. React rendered the Server Component on the server and converted it to a JSON-like wire format before the Client Component receives it. The Client Component renders an opaque node — it never executes the server code.

Import direction Allowed Why
Server Component imports Client Component Yes Safe — client code has no server secrets
Client Component imports Server Component No Server code would enter client bundle
Server Component passed as children to Client Component Yes Already serialised before client receives it

📄 react.dev → "Importing and Exporting Components"; nextjs.org/docs → "Composing Client and Server Components".

Q15b-v. Show me each rendering strategy in code — SSG, ISR, SSR, Streaming SSR, PPR, CSR.

Q15b is the decision matrix. This is the implementation reference — interviewers often follow up with "now write the code for ISR" or "how would you stream this dashboard?".

SSG — generateStaticParams for known slugs at build:

tsx
// app/blog/[slug]/page.tsx — pre-renders one HTML per slug at build
export async function generateStaticParams() {
  const posts = await getAllPosts();
  return posts.map(post => ({ slug: post.slug })); // builds N HTML files
}

export default async function BlogPost({ params }: { params: Promise<{ slug: string }> }) {
  const { slug } = await params;
  const post = await getPost(slug); // runs ONCE at build, never again
  return <article><h1>{post.title}</h1><time>{post.date}</time><div>{post.content}</div></article>;
}
// In `next build` output → marked ○ (static) — served from CDN

ISR — "use cache" + cacheLife + on-demand cacheTag (Next.js 16):

tsx
import { cacheLife, cacheTag, revalidateTag } from 'next/cache';

async function getProduct(id: string) {
  'use cache';
  cacheLife({ revalidate: 60 });  // background refresh every 60s
  cacheTag(`product-${id}`);      // for on-demand purge
  return await fetch(`https://api.example.com/products/${id}`).then(r => r.json());
}

export default async function ProductPage({ params }: { params: Promise<{ id: string }> }) {
  const { id } = await params;
  const product = await getProduct(id);
  return <div><h1>{product.name}</h1><p>${product.price}</p><p>Stock: {product.stock}</p></div>;
}

// app/actions/updateProduct.ts — CMS webhook triggers this
'use server';
export async function onProductUpdate(id: string) {
  await db.product.update({ where: { id }, data: {/*...*/} });
  revalidateTag(`product-${id}`); // purges only this product's cache
}

SSR (dynamic — DEFAULT in Next.js 16):

tsx
// app/dashboard/page.tsx — dynamic by default with cacheComponents
import { cookies } from 'next/headers';

export default async function Dashboard() {
  const token = (await cookies()).get('session')?.value; // runtime API → auto-dynamic
  const me = await fetch('https://api.example.com/me', {
    headers: { Authorization: `Bearer ${token}` },
  }).then(r => r.json());
  return <div><h1>Hi {me.name}</h1><p>Balance: ${me.balance}</p></div>;
}

What triggers dynamic rendering (the "why is this not cached?" cheat sheet):

Trigger Effect Example
await cookies() Auto-dynamic — needs request Auth session read
await headers() Auto-dynamic Accept-Language, User-Agent
await searchParams in page Auto-dynamic ?q=foo&page=2
await draftMode() Auto-dynamic CMS preview mode
No "use cache" directive Dynamic by default (Next.js 16) New convention — opt IN to cache

Streaming SSR — multiple slow widgets with independent fallbacks:

tsx
// app/dashboard/page.tsx — header instant, widgets stream as ready
import { Suspense } from 'react';

async function RevenueChart() {
  const data = await fetch('https://slow-api/revenue').then(r => r.json()); // ~3s
  return <Chart data={data} />;
}
async function RecentOrders() {
  const orders = await fetch('https://api/orders').then(r => r.json()); // ~2s
  return <ul>{orders.map(o => <li key={o.id}>{o.product} — ${o.total}</li>)}</ul>;
}

export default function DashboardPage() {
  return (
    <div>
      <h1>Dashboard</h1>                                          {/* sent at 0ms */}
      <Suspense fallback={<ChartSkeleton />}><RevenueChart /></Suspense>   {/* arrives ~3s */}
      <Suspense fallback={<OrdersSkeleton />}><RecentOrders /></Suspense>  {/* arrives ~2s — independent */}
    </div>
  );
}

Stream timeline (single HTTP response, progressive chunks):

text
0ms     ─ <html><body><h1>Dashboard</h1> + 2 skeleton fallbacks   → user sees layout
2000ms  ─ chunk: <script> swaps OrdersSkeleton → real <ul>...
3000ms  ─ chunk: <script> swaps ChartSkeleton → real <Chart>
done    ─ HTTP response closes

Streaming SSR ≠ Islands (Astro) — common interviewer misconception:

Streaming SSR (Next.js) Islands (Astro)
What streams Progressive HTML chunks over ONE HTTP request N/A — full HTML rendered upfront
Hydration The entire React tree hydrates client-side Only declared "island" components hydrate
Framework React-only, single app Mix React + Vue + Svelte etc.
Goal Fast TTFB + perceived perf with slow data Minimal JS shipped — "ship HTML by default"

PPR (Partial Pre-rendering) — static shell + dynamic holes:

tsx
// next.config.ts
const nextConfig = { cacheComponents: true }; // enables PPR + "use cache" + <Activity>

// app/products/[id]/page.tsx
import { Suspense } from 'react';
import { cookies } from 'next/headers';

async function ProductInfo({ id }: { id: string }) {
  'use cache'; // cached → pre-rendered into static shell at build
  const product = await fetch(`https://api/products/${id}`).then(r => r.json());
  return <div><h1>{product.name}</h1><p>{product.description}</p></div>;
}

async function PersonalizedPrice({ id }: { id: string }) {
  const region = (await cookies()).get('region')?.value ?? 'AU'; // runtime API → dynamic
  const pricing = await fetch(`https://api/pricing/${id}?region=${region}`).then(r => r.json());
  return <p className="price">${pricing.price} {pricing.currency}</p>;
}

export default async function ProductPage({ params }: { params: Promise<{ id: string }> }) {
  const { id } = await params;
  return (
    <div>
      <ProductInfo id={id} />                                                  {/* CDN ~50ms */}
      <Suspense fallback={<p>Calculating price…</p>}><PersonalizedPrice id={id} /></Suspense>
    </div>
  );
}

Timeline: 50ms CDN shell + skeletons → ~200ms dynamic holes stream in → done. Best-of-both: SEO-friendly cached frame + per-user content.

CSR — dynamic({ ssr: false }) + React Query for polling:

tsx
// app/analytics/page.tsx — Server Component wrapper
import dynamic from 'next/dynamic';
const AnalyticsDashboard = dynamic(() => import('./AnalyticsDashboard'), {
  ssr: false,                          // never render on server
  loading: () => <DashboardSkeleton />,
});
export default function Page() { return <AnalyticsDashboard />; }

// app/analytics/AnalyticsDashboard.tsx — Client only
'use client';
import { useQuery } from '@tanstack/react-query';
export default function AnalyticsDashboard() {
  const { data, isLoading, error } = useQuery({
    queryKey: ['live-feed'],
    queryFn: () => fetch('/api/feed').then(r => r.json()),
    refetchInterval: 5000,             // poll every 5s
  });
  if (isLoading) return <DashboardSkeleton />;
  if (error) return <ErrorMessage error={error} />;
  return <><LineChart data={data.pageViews} /><BarChart data={data.conversions} /></>;
}

Use CSR only when: browser-only APIs (Canvas/WebGL/localStorage/geolocation) · WebSocket / SSE real-time · heavy interactive maps (Leaflet, D3) · behind-auth pages with no SEO need · third-party embedded widgets.

Banking decision shortcut:

Page Strategy Reason
/ marketing landing SSG Static content, CDN fast, max SEO
/products/savings-accounts ISR (1h) CMS-driven, refresh hourly
/dashboard PPR Layout cached + balance/txn dynamic — best UX
/transfer SSR Personalized form, must always be fresh
/charts/live-rates CSR WebSocket streaming, no SEO needed

📄 nextjs.org/docs → "Rendering", "Caching", "Partial Prerendering", "Streaming".

Q15c. Server Actions vs Route Handlers — when to use which?

Server Actions Route Handlers
What 'use server' functions called from client HTTP endpoints (app/api/.../route.ts)
For Mutations in same app (form submit, create/update/delete) External APIs, webhooks, mobile clients
CSRF ✅ Auto (origin check) ❌ Implement yourself
Type safety ✅ End-to-end TS ❌ Serialize/deserialize
Progressive enhancement ✅ Works without JS ❌ Needs JS

Rule of thumb: use a Server Action when the caller is your own Next.js UI and you want mutation ergonomics, form support, and less boilerplate. Use a Route Handler when you need a stable HTTP boundary for mobile apps, third-party clients, webhooks, public APIs, or shared integrations. If both need the same business logic, extract a server-only service function and call it from both. Do not wrap a Route Handler in a Server Action just to "maximize advantages": that usually adds an extra hop without improving the trust boundary.

tsx
// Server Action — banking transfer
'use server';
export async function transfer(formData: FormData) {
  const session = await getSession();
  if (!session) throw new Error('Unauthorized'); // ALWAYS check auth
  const { amount, toAccount } = transferSchema.parse(Object.fromEntries(formData)); // ALWAYS validate
  await db.transfer.create({ data: { amount, toAccount, userId: session.userId } });
  revalidatePath('/account');
}

📄 nextjs.org/docs → "Server Actions and Mutations", "Route Handlers".

Q15d. loading.tsx and error.tsx — what do they do?

text
app/
├── dashboard/
│   ├── page.tsx        ← Content
│   ├── loading.tsx     ← Auto-wraps page in <Suspense>. Shows while page data loads.
│   └── error.tsx       ← Auto-wraps page in Error Boundary. Shows on thrown errors.
tsx
// loading.tsx — shown instantly, page streams in when ready
export default function Loading() {
  return <DashboardSkeleton />;
}

// error.tsx — MUST be 'use client' (Error Boundary is a class-based lifecycle)
'use client';
export default function Error({ error, reset }: { error: Error; reset: () => void }) {
  return (
    <div role="alert">
      <h2>Something went wrong</h2>
      <button onClick={reset}>Try again</button>
    </div>
  );
}

reset() re-renders the segment without a full page reload. Error boundaries only catch errors in render, not event handlers. 📄 nextjs.org/docs → "Loading UI", "Error Handling".

Q15e. State management — when to use what?

Situation Solution Library needed?
Filter, pagination, sort in URL searchParams / useRouter ❌
Server data caching + refetch Server Components or React Query/SWR Optional
Toggle open/close, local UI useState local ❌
Theme, auth session React Context ❌
Complex cross-component state Zustand / Jotai ✅
Form state useActionState (React 19) or react-hook-form Optional

Rule: don't reach for state managers prematurely. Most state should live in URL (shareable) or close to where it's used.

Deep dive: see Part A.6 (Q-SM-1 to Q-SM-19) for mechanism explanations of Redux/Zustand/Jotai, full implementation patterns, perf optimization, security rules, and Next.js App Router SSR pitfalls.

Q15f. Next.js App Router folder structure?

text
app/
  (marketing)/          ← Route group — parentheses = NO URL segment
    layout.tsx          ← Layout only for marketing pages
    about/page.tsx      ← /about
  (dashboard)/
    layout.tsx          ← Separate layout for auth'd dashboard
    overview/page.tsx   ← /overview
  api/
    revalidate/route.ts ← API endpoint at /api/revalidate
src/
  components/           ← Shared UI components
  lib/                  ← DB client, utilities
  hooks/                ← Shared hooks
  services/             ← Business logic / data access layer
  types/                ← Shared TypeScript types

Route groups (name) let you share layouts without affecting URL paths. 📄 nextjs.org/docs → "Project Organization", "Route Groups".

Q15g. SEO depth — JSON-LD structured data + Core Web Vitals mapping?

Beyond generateMetadata, two senior-level SEO levers in Next.js:

1. JSON-LD structured data (rich snippets in Google search results — e.g. product price, ratings, breadcrumbs):

tsx
// app/products/[id]/page.tsx — Server Component
export default async function ProductPage({ params }: { params: Promise<{ id: string }> }) {
  const { id } = await params;
  const product = await getProduct(id);
  const jsonLd = {
    '@context': 'https://schema.org',
    '@type': 'Product',
    name: product.name,
    description: product.description,
    image: product.image,
    offers: {
      '@type': 'Offer',
      price: product.price,
      priceCurrency: 'AUD',
      availability: 'https://schema.org/InStock',
    },
    aggregateRating: { '@type': 'AggregateRating', ratingValue: product.rating, reviewCount: product.reviewCount },
  };
  return (
    <>
      <script type="application/ld+json" dangerouslySetInnerHTML={{ __html: JSON.stringify(jsonLd) }} />
      <h1>{product.name}</h1>
    </>
  );
}

Banking use cases: FinancialProduct, BankAccount, Article (news/insights), FAQPage, BreadcrumbList. Validate with Google Rich Results Test before shipping.

2. Core Web Vitals → Next.js feature mapping (interviewer "how do you optimize CWV in Next.js?"):

Metric What it measures Next.js lever
LCP (≤2.5s) Largest content paint next/image with priority on hero image · SSR/SSG (no client wait) · next/font preload
INP (≤200ms) Input responsiveness useTransition for heavy updates · next/script strategy="lazyOnload" for analytics · code-split with next/dynamic
CLS (≤0.1) Visual stability next/image (width/height enforced) · next/font (no FOUT swap) · reserve space with aspect-ratio

3. Rendering strategy → SEO score:

Strategy SEO Why
SSG ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ Full HTML at build, CDN-fast, crawler-friendly
ISR ⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐ Same as SSG + auto-refresh
SSR ⭐⭐⭐⭐ Full HTML per request, slower TTFB
Streaming/PPR ⭐⭐⭐⭐ OK if critical content NOT inside <Suspense>
CSR ⭐⭐ Googlebot does render JS but unreliable + slow

📄 schema.org → "Product", "FinancialProduct"; web.dev → "Core Web Vitals"; nextjs.org/docs → "Metadata", "Optimizing".

Q15h. Next.js architecture patterns — Repository / Service / BFF / Provider?

Pairs with Q-JD-14 (§1.5.4) — "freedom to make architectural and code-level decisions": the 7-step component-decomposition checklist + a banking example walkthrough.

Senior architecture vocabulary that signals you've built apps beyond toy examples:

Pattern What When Example
Repository Pattern Abstract data access behind an interface (DB-agnostic) Banking apps where you swap Prisma → raw SQL or test with in-memory mock UserRepository.findById(id) calls Prisma; tests inject InMemoryUserRepository
Service Layer Business logic distinct from data access + UI Multi-step workflows (transfer = validate + debit + credit + audit) TransferService.execute({from, to, amount}) orchestrates repos + emits audit event
BFF (Backend for Frontend) Route Handler aggregates multiple microservices into one client-friendly response A bank has 5+ microservices; one BFF endpoint returns the shape the page needs GET /api/dashboard calls accounts-svc + txn-svc + notif-svc in parallel, returns merged JSON
Provider Pattern Context provider for cross-cutting concerns (theme, auth, analytics, i18n) Anything 3+ leaf components need without prop drilling <AuthProvider><ThemeProvider><App/></ThemeProvider></AuthProvider>
Compound Component Multi-part UI with shared internal state Tabs, Accordion, Select, Dialog <Tabs><Tabs.List/><Tabs.Panel/></Tabs>
tsx
// Repository — testable boundary
interface AccountRepository {
  findById(id: string): Promise<Account | null>;
  updateBalance(id: string, amount: number): Promise<void>;
}
class PrismaAccountRepository implements AccountRepository { /* prod */ }
class InMemoryAccountRepository implements AccountRepository { /* tests */ }

// Service — business rules
class TransferService {
  constructor(private accounts: AccountRepository, private audit: AuditLogger) {}
  async execute({ fromId, toId, amount }: TransferInput) {
    const from = await this.accounts.findById(fromId);
    if (!from || from.balance < amount) throw new InsufficientFundsError();
    await this.accounts.updateBalance(fromId, -amount);
    await this.accounts.updateBalance(toId, +amount);
    await this.audit.log({ type: 'TRANSFER', fromId, toId, amount });
  }
}

// BFF — Route Handler aggregating microservices
// app/api/dashboard/route.ts
export async function GET() {
  const session = await getServerSession();
  const [accounts, txns, notifs] = await Promise.all([
    fetch(`${ACCOUNTS_SVC}/users/${session.id}/accounts`).then(r => r.json()),
    fetch(`${TXN_SVC}/users/${session.id}/recent?limit=10`).then(r => r.json()),
    fetch(`${NOTIF_SVC}/users/${session.id}/unread`).then(r => r.json()),
  ]);
  return Response.json({ accounts, txns, notifs });
}

Folder structure (hybrid — recommended for production):

text
app/                    # Routes only
  (marketing)/about/page.tsx
  (dashboard)/overview/page.tsx
  api/dashboard/route.ts     # BFF endpoint
src/
  components/           # Shared UI (Compound, Presentational)
  lib/                  # db client, auth utils
  hooks/                # shared hooks
  services/             # business logic — TransferService etc.
  repositories/         # data access boundary
  types/                # shared TS types

Real-world OSS to study (cite if asked "what Next.js apps inspire you?"):

Project What to learn Notable
cal.com Monorepo + tRPC + 50+ contributors Turborepo scaling, shared packages
dub.co App Router + Server Actions in production Modern stable patterns end-to-end
vercel/platforms Multi-tenant + Edge Middleware SaaS architecture template

📄 martinfowler.com → "Repository Pattern", "BackendsForFrontends"; github.com/calcom/cal.com; github.com/dubinc/dub.

Q15i. Performance patterns — quick reference for production apps?

Pattern What When to apply Code signal
Code Splitting Lazy-load components/routes — smaller initial bundle Heavy components (charts, editors), per-route in App Router (automatic) next/dynamic(() => import('./Chart'), { ssr: false }) or React.lazy
Memoization Skip re-render if props unchanged ⚠️ AFTER profiling — never premature React.memo(Component) · useMemo(() => expensive(x), [x]) · useCallback(fn, deps)
Virtualization Render only visible items in long lists Lists >100 items, infinite scroll react-window · @tanstack/react-virtual
Streaming + Suspense Send shell early, stream slow data Mix of fast and slow components on same page <Suspense fallback={<Skeleton/>}><SlowAsync/></Suspense>
Image optimization Auto WebP/AVIF, responsive sizes, lazy load All non-trivial images next/image with priority for above-fold
Skeleton / Placeholder Show shape while loading — perceived perf All async content loading.tsx per route + skeleton components
Prefetching Warm cache for likely-next route Navigation hover, viewport-visible links <Link prefetch> (App Router prefetches in dev/prod automatically for visible links)
Bundle analysis Find unexpected large deps Before every release @next/bundle-analyzer

Decision rule: Measure first (Lighthouse, React DevTools Profiler, next build --analyze) → optimize the actual bottleneck → re-measure. Premature useMemo everywhere creates noise without wins.
