📄 react.dev → "Profiler"; nextjs.org/docs → "Optimizing".

#TypeScript

Q16. interface vs type? A: Both describe shapes. interface is extendable/mergeable (declaration merging), good for object/class contracts. type is more flexible — unions, intersections, mapped/conditional types, primitives. Rule of thumb: interface for public object APIs, type for unions/utilities. 📄 typescriptlang.org → Handbook "Everyday Types", "Object Types".

Q17. unknown vs any vs never? A: any opts out of type checking (avoid). unknown is type-safe top type — must narrow before use. never = no value possible (exhaustiveness checks, functions that throw/never return). 📄 typescriptlang.org → "unknown", narrowing docs.

Q18. Generics — give an example. A: Parameterize types for reuse with type safety: function identity<T>(x: T): T. Used in Array<T>, Promise<T>, React useState<User>(). Constraints via extends. 📄 typescriptlang.org → Handbook "Generics".

Q19. Useful utility types? A: Partial<T>, Required<T>, Pick<T,K>, Omit<T,K>, Record<K,V>, Readonly<T>, ReturnType<T>. Show you know Omit/Pick for component prop derivation. 📄 typescriptlang.org → "Utility Types".

Q20. Discriminated unions / type narrowing? A: Union of object types sharing a literal "tag" field; switch on the tag narrows the type. Enables exhaustive, type-safe state machines (e.g. {status:'loading'} | {status:'error', err} | {status:'ok', data}).

tsx
type AsyncState<T> =
  | { status: 'idle' }
  | { status: 'loading' }
  | { status: 'success'; data: T }
  | { status: 'error'; error: string };

// Impossible: can't have loading + data at same time
const [state, dispatch] = useReducer(asyncReducer, { status: 'idle' });

📄 typescriptlang.org → "Narrowing", "Discriminated Unions".

Q20a. Zod — runtime + compile-time validation? A: Zod schema = both runtime validator AND TypeScript type via z.infer. Standard for Server Action input validation in Next.js + banking input sanitization.

tsx
import { z } from 'zod';
const TransferSchema = z.object({
  amount: z.number().positive().max(1_000_000),
  toAccount: z.string().min(8).max(20),
});
type Transfer = z.infer<typeof TransferSchema>; // TypeScript type auto-generated

export async function transfer(formData: FormData) {
  'use server';
  const result = TransferSchema.safeParse(Object.fromEntries(formData));
  if (!result.success) return { error: result.error.flatten() };
  // result.data is fully typed Transfer
}

📄 zod.dev docs.

#Design Patterns (bonus — shows senior React knowledge)

Q20b. Compound Component Pattern — what & when? A: Child components share implicit state via Context, mounted as sub-components on the parent (e.g. Select.Option). Avoids prop drilling for multi-part UI. Consumer gets flexible composition.

tsx
const SelectContext = createContext(undefined);
function Select({ children, defaultValue = '' }) {
  const [value, setValue] = useState(defaultValue);
  return (
    <SelectContext.Provider value={{ value, onChange: setValue }}>
      <div role="listbox">{children}</div>
    </SelectContext.Provider>
  );
}
function Option({ value, children }) {
  const { value: selected, onChange } = useContext(SelectContext);
  return (
    <div role="option" aria-selected={selected === value} onClick={() => onChange(value)}>
      {children}
    </div>
  );
}
Select.Option = Option;
// Usage: <Select defaultValue="au"><Select.Option value="au">AUD</Select.Option></Select>

Use for: Tabs, Accordion, Dialog, Select, Menu. Real examples: Radix UI, Headless UI, shadcn/ui.

Q20c. Headless Component (logic-only hook)? A: Hook exposes logic + prop getters but NO UI. Consumer renders any UI they want. Separates behavior from presentation — maximally reusable and accessible.

tsx
function useToggle(initial = false) {
  const [isOpen, setIsOpen] = useState(initial);
  return {
    isOpen,
    getButtonProps: () => ({ onClick: () => setIsOpen(v => !v), 'aria-expanded': isOpen }),
    getPanelProps: () => ({ hidden: !isOpen }),
  };
}
// Consumer: <button {...getButtonProps()}>Toggle</button><div {...getPanelProps()}>Content</div>

Real examples: TanStack Table, React Aria, Downshift.

Q20d. Derived state — common mistake? A: Don't useState for data computable from existing state — derive it in render (or useMemo for expensive). Maintaining two states in sync always causes bugs.

tsx
// ❌ BUG: filteredItems must be kept in sync manually
const [items, setItems] = useState([]);
const [filteredItems, setFilteredItems] = useState([]);

// ✅ CORRECT: derive in render
const [items, setItems] = useState([]);
const [filter, setFilter] = useState('');
const filteredItems = useMemo(
  () => items.filter(i => i.name.includes(filter)),
  [items, filter]
);

Q20e. Data fetching patterns (Server Components)?

tsx
// Parallel — independent data
const [user, stats] = await Promise.all([getUser(id), getStats(id)]);

// Sequential — data depends on prior result
const user = await getUser(id);
const posts = await getPosts(user.teamId); // needs user first

// Preload — trigger early, read later (avoid waterfall)
function UserPage({ id }) {
  void getUser(id); // start fetch as early as possible
  return <Suspense><UserContent id={id} /></Suspense>;
}

// Streaming — mix fast + slow components
<Suspense fallback={<Skeleton />}><SlowComponent /></Suspense>

Q20f. HOC (Higher-Order Component) pattern? A: Function that wraps a component and returns a new one with added behavior (auth check, logging, theming). Less common now — prefer hooks for logic sharing. Still valid for wrapping patterns that can't use hooks.

tsx
function withAuth(WrappedComponent: React.ComponentType) {
  return function AuthGuard(props: any) {
    const { user, isLoading } = useAuth();
    if (isLoading) return <Spinner />;
    if (!user) return <Navigate to="/login" />;
    return <WrappedComponent {...props} />;
  };
}
const ProtectedDashboard = withAuth(Dashboard);

Use for: cross-cutting concerns (auth, analytics, logging). Don't use for: logic that can be a hook — hooks are simpler.

Q20g. Container / Presentational (Server/Client split in Next.js)? A: Container = Server Component (fetches data, business logic). Presentational = pure UI component (can be Client if needs interactivity). Clean separation of concerns — UI components are reusable and testable in isolation.

tsx
// Container — Server Component, data access
async function UserListContainer() {
  const users = await db.user.findMany({ select: { id: true, name: true } });
  return <UserListView users={users} />;
}

// Presentational — pure UI, props-driven, easy to test/storybook
function UserListView({ users }: { users: User[] }) {
  return <ul>{users.map(u => <li key={u.id}>{u.name}</li>)}</ul>;
}

Q20h. Generic TypeScript component?

tsx
// Type-safe reusable Table — works with any data shape
interface Column<T> { key: keyof T; label: string }
interface TableProps<T> { data: T[]; columns: Column<T>[] }

function Table<T extends { id: string }>({ data, columns }: TableProps<T>) {
  return (
    <table>
      <thead><tr>{columns.map(c => <th key={String(c.key)}>{c.label}</th>)}</tr></thead>
      <tbody>
        {data.map(row => (
          <tr key={row.id}>
            {columns.map(c => <td key={String(c.key)}>{String(row[c.key])}</td>)}
          </tr>
        ))}
      </tbody>
    </table>
  );
}
// Usage: <Table<User> data={users} columns={[{ key: 'name', label: 'Name' }]} />

Q20i. Hook composition pattern? A: Build complex hooks by composing smaller hooks. Each hook has single responsibility; compose at the call site.

tsx
// Small hooks
function useDebounce<T>(value: T, delay = 300): T { /* ... */ }
function useSearch(query: string) { /* fetches results for query */ }

// Composed hook — search with automatic debounce
function useSearchWithDebounce(initialQuery = '') {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState(initialQuery);
  const debouncedQuery = useDebounce(query, 300); // composed
  const results = useSearch(debouncedQuery);        // composed
  return { query, setQuery, ...results };
}
// Consumer gets clean API, no knowledge of debounce internals

Rule: extract a hook when logic is used in 2+ components, OR when a component has >50 lines of hooks.

Q20j. Composition Pattern (children/slots) — distinct from Compound Component?

Composition (children/slots) Compound Component
Purpose Layout container accepts arbitrary JSX Multi-part widget with shared state
Mechanism children + named JSX props Context + Parent.Child sub-components
Example Card, Modal, PageLayout Tabs, Accordion, Select
Consumer controls All content Sub-component structure
tsx
function PageLayout({ sidebar, children, footer }: {
  sidebar: React.ReactNode; children: React.ReactNode; footer?: React.ReactNode;
}) {
  return (
    <div className="layout">
      <aside>{sidebar}</aside>
      <main>{children}</main>
      {footer && <footer>{footer}</footer>}
    </div>
  );
}
<PageLayout sidebar={<NavMenu />} footer={<FooterLinks />}><DashboardContent /></PageLayout>

3 benefits: no prop drilling, inversion of control, free render optimization (children ref stable if parent doesn't re-render).

Q20k. State colocation — what & why? A: Keep state in the lowest component that needs it. Don't lift higher than necessary. Default starting point before lifting or global state.

tsx
// ❌ WRONG: App holds modal state only Header needs → Form re-renders on every modal toggle
function App() {
  const [isOpen, setIsOpen] = useState(false);
  return <><Header isOpen={isOpen} setIsOpen={setIsOpen} /><Form /></>;
}
// ✅ RIGHT: Header owns its own modal state — Form never re-renders
function Header() {
  const [isOpen, setIsOpen] = useState(false);
  return <><button onClick={() => setIsOpen(true)}>Menu</button>{isOpen && <Modal />}</>;
}

Decision: 1 component → colocate. 2 siblings → lift. Many unrelated → Context/Zustand. URL (filter/page) → searchParams.

Q20l. URL as state — when to use instead of useState? A: When state must survive page refresh, be shareable as URL, or work with browser back/forward — filters, pagination, sort, tab selection.

tsx
// ❌ useState for filters: F5 loses state, URL not shareable, back button broken

// ✅ URL as state (Client Component)
'use client';
function ProductFilters() {
  const searchParams = useSearchParams();
  const router = useRouter();
  const pathname = usePathname();
  const category = searchParams.get('category') ?? 'all';

  function updateFilter(key: string, value: string) {
    const params = new URLSearchParams(searchParams.toString());
    params.set(key, value);
    if (key !== 'page') params.set('page', '1'); // reset page on filter change
    router.push(`${pathname}?${params.toString()}`);
  }
  return <select value={category} onChange={e => updateFilter('category', e.target.value)}>...</select>;
}

// Server Component reads searchParams prop directly (no hook)
export default async function Page({ searchParams }: { searchParams: Promise<{ category?: string }> }) {
  const { category = 'all' } = await searchParams;
  const products = await db.product.findMany({ where: category !== 'all' ? { category } : {} });
  return <ProductGrid products={products} />;
}
State type Use
Filter, pagination, sort URL searchParams
Form input (pre-submit) useState controlled
Modal open/close Colocated useState
Auth, theme Context / Zustand
Server data Server Component or React Query

Q20m. Optimistic update — manual pattern + useOptimistic? A: Update UI immediately with expected result before async completes. On failure → rollback. useOptimistic (React 19) only works in Server Action context — REST APIs still need manual pattern.

tsx
// Manual — works with any API
async function handleLike() {
  if (isPending) return; // prevent double-click
  const prevLiked = liked; const prevCount = count; // snapshot for rollback
  setLiked(!prevLiked); setCount(prevLiked ? count - 1 : count + 1); // optimistic
  setIsPending(true);
  try { await toggleLikeAPI(postId); }
  catch { setLiked(prevLiked); setCount(prevCount); toast.error('Failed.'); } // rollback
  finally { setIsPending(false); }
}

// List add: use temp ID
const tempId = `temp-${Date.now()}`;
setTodos(prev => [...prev, { id: tempId, text, done: false }]);
try {
  const real = await createTodoAPI(text);
  setTodos(prev => prev.map(t => t.id === tempId ? real : t)); // swap temp → real
} catch { setTodos(prev => prev.filter(t => t.id !== tempId)); } // remove temp

// useOptimistic (React 19 + Server Actions only) — auto-rollback
const [optimisticLiked, setOptimisticLiked] = useOptimistic(serverLiked);
async function handleLike() {
  setOptimisticLiked(!optimisticLiked); // React auto-reverts when action settles
  await toggleLikeAction(postId);        // Server Action
}

📄 react.dev → "useOptimistic".

#Part A.5 — Additional React / Next.js / TypeScript Q&A (collected questions)

Format: Q → A (concise) → Why it matters (rationale) → 📄 Evidence. Cross-references send you to fuller answers already in this doc.

#React extras

Q20n. What are React Portals, and when would you use them? A: createPortal(children, domNode) renders children into a DOM node outside the parent component tree, while keeping React event bubbling and context intact. Why: Modals, toasts, tooltips, and dropdowns must escape parent overflow:hidden / z-index / stacking context — portals achieve that without breaking React state flow.

tsx
import { createPortal } from 'react-dom';
function Modal({ children }) {
  return createPortal(<div className="modal">{children}</div>, document.body);
}

📄 react.dev → "createPortal".

Q20o. Lifecycle methods in class components — and useEffect equivalents?

Class lifecycle Hook equivalent
constructor useState initializer
componentDidMount useEffect(() => {...}, [])
componentDidUpdate useEffect(() => {...}, [deps])
componentWillUnmount useEffect's returned cleanup fn
shouldComponentUpdate(p, s) React.memo (props) + useMemo (derived)
getDerivedStateFromProps Compute during render OR useSyncExternalStore
getSnapshotBeforeUpdate useLayoutEffect (read pre-mutation DOM)

Why: Modern code uses hooks, but you'll inherit class components in legacy banking codebases. Knowing the mapping makes refactors clean. 📄 react.dev → "Migrating from Class Components".

Q20p. React.memo vs PureComponent vs useMemo — what's the difference?

What it memoizes Where used
React.memo(C) A function component's render — shallow-compare props Wrap a component to skip re-render
PureComponent A class component's render — shallow props + state Class component base class (legacy)
useMemo(fn, deps) A computed value — re-runs fn only when deps change Inside a component
useCallback(fn, deps) A function reference — stable across renders Pass to memoized children / effect deps

Rule: profile first; memoize only proven hot paths. See Q4, Q10, Q15i. 📄 react.dev → "memo", "useMemo".

Q20q. React Router (v6+) — full reference: BrowserRouter, HashRouter, useNavigate, dynamic + nested routes, NavLink, route protection.

tsx
import { BrowserRouter, Routes, Route, Link, NavLink, useNavigate, useParams, Outlet, Navigate } from 'react-router-dom';

function App() {
  return (
    <BrowserRouter>
      <Routes>
        <Route path="/" element={<Layout />}>
          <Route index element={<Home />} />
          <Route path="accounts" element={<RequireAuth><Accounts /></RequireAuth>}>
            <Route path=":id" element={<AccountDetail />} />
            <Route path=":id/transactions" element={<Transactions />} />
          </Route>
          <Route path="*" element={<NotFound />} />
        </Route>
      </Routes>
    </BrowserRouter>
  );
}

function Layout() {
  return (
    <>
      <nav>
        <NavLink to="/" end className={({ isActive }) => isActive ? 'active' : ''}>Home</NavLink>
        <NavLink to="/accounts">Accounts</NavLink>
      </nav>
      <Outlet /> {/* nested children render here */}
    </>
  );
}

function AccountDetail() {
  const { id } = useParams();              // dynamic route param
  const navigate = useNavigate();          // imperative navigation
  return <button onClick={() => navigate(`/accounts/${id}/transactions`)}>View</button>;
}

function RequireAuth({ children }) {
  const { user } = useAuth();
  return user ? children : <Navigate to="/login" replace />;
}
Topic Answer
BrowserRouter vs HashRouter BrowserRouter uses HTML5 History API (/account/123) — needs server rewrite. HashRouter uses #/account/123 — works with static hosts (S3) without rewrites.
useNavigate vs useHistory useHistory was v5 (history.push). v6 replaced it with useNavigate — function-call API, supports replace/state.
<Link> vs <NavLink> Link = plain navigation. NavLink = Link + active-state styling (isActive callback).
Pass data navigate('/route', { state: { foo: 1 } }) → useLocation().state
Route protection Wrapper component that checks auth and either renders children or <Navigate to="/login">

Note: JDs often say "Next.js preferred" — App Router is the priority. React Router questions often come up because of legacy CRA apps. 📄 reactrouter.com → v6 docs.

Q20r. Redux Toolkit (RTK) — actions, reducers, store, useSelector, useDispatch, middleware, thunks?

For a deep dive on Redux mechanism, mental model, RTK Query, selectors with reselect, optimistic updates, DevTools, and Next.js App Router SSR — see Part A.6 §A.6.3 (Q-SM-3, Q-SM-7 to Q-SM-10).

tsx
// store.ts
import { configureStore, createSlice, PayloadAction } from '@reduxjs/toolkit';
import { TypedUseSelectorHook, useDispatch, useSelector } from 'react-redux';

interface AccountState { balance: number; loading: boolean }
const accountSlice = createSlice({
  name: 'account',
  initialState: { balance: 0, loading: false } as AccountState,
  reducers: {
    setBalance(state, action: PayloadAction<number>) { state.balance = action.payload; },
    setLoading(state, action: PayloadAction<boolean>)  { state.loading = action.payload; },
  },
});
export const { setBalance, setLoading } = accountSlice.actions;

export const store = configureStore({
  reducer: { account: accountSlice.reducer },
  // Middleware: thunk is included by default; add logger/persist via .concat()
});
export type RootState = ReturnType<typeof store.getState>;
export type AppDispatch = typeof store.dispatch;
export const useAppSelector: TypedUseSelectorHook<RootState> = useSelector;
export const useAppDispatch = () => useDispatch<AppDispatch>();

// Component
function AccountWidget() {
  const balance = useAppSelector(s => s.account.balance);
  const dispatch = useAppDispatch();
  return <button onClick={() => dispatch(setBalance(balance + 100))}>+100</button>;
}
Concept RTK answer
Action Auto-generated by createSlice — slice.actions.setBalance(100) returns { type: 'account/setBalance', payload: 100 }
Reducer Pure function (state, action) => newState. RTK uses Immer so you can write "mutating" code safely
Store configureStore — combines reducers, adds DevTools + Thunk middleware automatically
Middleware Sits between dispatch and reducer (e.g., redux-thunk, redux-saga, custom logger). RTK includes thunk; add via getDefaultMiddleware().concat(myMw)
createAsyncThunk RTK helper for async actions — auto pending/fulfilled/rejected actions
Redux vs Context Context = simple shared value (theme, auth). Redux = complex global state with time-travel debugging, middleware, large team scaling

Rule: don't reach for Redux on day one — start with useState → useReducer → Context → RTK only if state is truly global + complex. See Q15e. 📄 redux-toolkit.js.org → "Tutorials", "TypeScript Quick Start".

Q20s. MobX vs Redux — quick comparison?

Redux MobX
Paradigm Functional, immutable, single store OOP, mutable observable, multiple stores
Boilerplate More (mitigated by RTK) Less
Learning curve Steeper Gentler
Predictability High (pure reducers) Lower (implicit reactivity)
Banking suitability ✅ Audit trail via DevTools time-travel ⚠️ Harder to trace

Rule: enterprise/banking → Redux + RTK. Small teams / rapid prototyping → MobX or Zustand. 📄 mobx.js.org; redux.js.org.

Q20t. SWR / React Query / Axios vs fetch — when to use what?

Tool What When
fetch Built-in browser API Simple one-off calls, Server Components
axios Library: interceptors, auto JSON, timeouts, request cancellation Complex client apps needing auth interceptors, retry logic
SWR (Vercel) Stale-while-revalidate hook — caches + revalidates on focus Lightweight client data fetching
@tanstack/react-query Full server-state library: caching, dedupe, retries, optimistic, infinite queries Complex client data layer; the de-facto standard
tsx
// React Query
const { data, isLoading, error } = useQuery({
  queryKey: ['account', id],
  queryFn: () => fetch(`/api/accounts/${id}`).then(r => r.json()),
  staleTime: 30_000,
  refetchOnWindowFocus: true,
});

Rule for Next.js: prefer Server Components for SSR data; use React Query only for client polling / WebSocket-augmented UI. 📄 swr.vercel.app; tanstack.com/query.

Q20u. Webpack, tree-shaking, Babel, dev vs prod build?

Concept One-liner
Webpack Module bundler — turns many JS/CSS/asset files into optimized bundles
Tree shaking Dead-code elimination using ES module static analysis (import/export). Requires ESM + sideEffects: false in package.json
Babel JS compiler — transpiles modern JS/JSX/TSX to a target ES version + adds polyfills via @babel/preset-env
Dev build Source maps, hot reload, no minify — fast feedback
Prod build Minified, tree-shaken, hashed filenames, no source maps (or hidden)

Note: Next.js wraps Webpack (or Turbopack in 15+) — you rarely configure it directly. Know it conceptually for the "what's under the hood?" question. 📄 webpack.js.org → "Tree Shaking"; babeljs.io → "Configure Babel".

Q20v. Web Workers in React — when to use? A: Web Workers run JS on a background thread, isolated from the UI thread. Use for heavy CPU work (CSV parsing, encryption, image manipulation) to keep the main thread responsive.

tsx
// worker.ts
self.onmessage = (e) => {
  const result = heavyParse(e.data); // CPU-intensive
  self.postMessage(result);
};
// component.tsx
const worker = new Worker(new URL('./worker.ts', import.meta.url));
worker.postMessage(largeFile);
worker.onmessage = (e) => setResult(e.data);

Banking use: client-side PDF generation, large CSV import. Don't use for: network calls (already async), small computations (overhead > saving). 📄 MDN → "Web Workers"; Comlink (worker RPC helper).

Q20w. Hydration in React/Next.js — what & common bugs? A: Hydration = the process where the client React attaches event listeners and reuses server-rendered HTML, transitioning a static page into an interactive SPA. The HTML is reused (not re-rendered) — only event handlers are attached. Common hydration bugs:

  • Date.now(), Math.random(), typeof window !== 'undefined' checks → server vs client mismatch
  • localStorage access in render → only available on client
  • Suppress with suppressHydrationWarning (last resort) or move to useEffect 📄 react.dev → "hydrateRoot"; nextjs.org/docs → "Errors / Hydration".

Q20x. React accessibility (a11y) — what does React do for free, and where do you need to add work? React itself is a11y-neutral — the burden is on you. See Q41–Q43, Q29a–Q29e for full coverage. Quick checklist:

  • Use semantic HTML (button, label, nav) — see Q29e
  • useId for stable form id/htmlFor (avoids hydration mismatches) — see Q7
  • aria-live for dynamic announcements (toasts, validation errors)
  • Focus management on route change + modal open/close — see Drill B
  • eslint-plugin-jsx-a11y catches missing alt, role misuse, etc. 📄 react.dev → "Accessibility"; w3.org/WAI.

#Next.js extras

Q15j. Pages Router data APIs — getServerSideProps, getStaticProps, getStaticPaths (legacy but still asked)?

The Pages Router is still widely deployed; interviewers from older codebases ask these:

API Equivalent in App Router
getStaticProps Server Component + "use cache"
getServerSideProps Server Component (no cache directive — default dynamic)
getStaticPaths generateStaticParams()
ts
// Pages Router (legacy)
export async function getStaticPaths() {
  const products = await db.product.findMany();
  return { paths: products.map(p => ({ params: { id: p.id }})), fallback: 'blocking' };
}
export async function getStaticProps({ params }) {
  const product = await getProduct(params.id);
  return { props: { product }, revalidate: 60 };  // ISR
}
export async function getServerSideProps(ctx) {
  return { props: { user: await getSession(ctx.req) } };  // SSR per-request
}

Rule: be ready to translate either direction. Mention you prefer App Router for new code. 📄 nextjs.org/docs → "Pages Router / Data Fetching" + "Migrating from Pages to App".

Q15k. Link vs useRouter — when to use which?

<Link> useRouter()
Usage Declarative — wraps anchor Imperative — router.push(...)
Prefetch ✅ Auto-prefetch when in viewport ❌
When Visible navigation links After form submit, conditional redirect
tsx
// Declarative
<Link href="/account">Go to Account</Link>

// Imperative
'use client';
import { useRouter } from 'next/navigation'; // App Router (NOT 'next/router')
const router = useRouter();
router.push('/account');     // navigate
router.replace('/login');    // no history entry
router.refresh();            // re-fetch Server Components
router.back();

📄 nextjs.org/docs → "Link", "useRouter".

Q15l. Next.js API routes / middleware / authenticating API endpoints?

ts
// app/api/transfer/route.ts
import { NextRequest, NextResponse } from 'next/server';
import { getServerSession } from '@/lib/auth';
import { z } from 'zod';

const TransferSchema = z.object({ from: z.string(), to: z.string(), amount: z.number().positive() });

export async function POST(req: NextRequest) {
  const session = await getServerSession();
  if (!session) return NextResponse.json({ error: 'Unauthorized' }, { status: 401 });

  const body = await req.json();
  const result = TransferSchema.safeParse(body);
  if (!result.success) return NextResponse.json({ error: result.error.flatten() }, { status: 400 });

  await transferService.execute({ ...result.data, userId: session.userId });
  return NextResponse.json({ ok: true });
}
ts
// middleware.ts — runs at the Edge before any matched route
import { NextResponse, NextRequest } from 'next/server';
export function middleware(req: NextRequest) {
  // 1. Auth gate
  const session = req.cookies.get('session');
  if (!session && req.nextUrl.pathname.startsWith('/api/')) {
    return NextResponse.json({ error: 'Unauthorized' }, { status: 401 });
  }
  // 2. Rate limit (pseudo)
  // 3. Add request ID for tracing
  const res = NextResponse.next();
  res.headers.set('x-request-id', crypto.randomUUID());
  return res;
}
export const config = { matcher: ['/api/:path*', '/dashboard/:path*'] };

Why: middleware runs at the Edge (super low latency) — perfect for auth gating, A/B testing, geo-routing, header injection. 📄 nextjs.org/docs → "Route Handlers", "Middleware".

Q15m. Internationalization (i18n) in Next.js? A: App Router approach (Next.js 13+) — use route segments + a translation library:

text
app/
  [locale]/        ← /en/..., /vi/..., /au/...
    layout.tsx     ← reads `params.locale`, loads dictionary
    page.tsx
middleware.ts      ← detects Accept-Language, redirects to /en or /vi

Libraries: next-intl, next-i18next, or roll-your-own JSON dictionaries.

tsx
// app/[locale]/page.tsx
import { getDictionary } from '@/lib/i18n';
export default async function Page({ params }: { params: Promise<{ locale: string }> }) {
  const { locale } = await params;
  const dict = await getDictionary(locale);
  return <h1>{dict.home.welcome}</h1>;
}

Banking: Some banks serve a single locale, but i18n questions still come up — be ready. 📄 nextjs.org/docs → "Internationalization"; next-intl docs.

Q15n. Deploying Next.js — Vercel vs AWS vs self-hosted?

Platform Pros Cons
Vercel Zero-config, optimal for Next.js, edge functions, ISR/PPR built-in Vendor lock, cost at scale
AWS Amplify Native AWS, IAM/VPC integration Less Next.js-tuned
AWS ECS/Fargate (Docker) Full control, fits banking compliance DevOps overhead
Self-host (Node + nginx) Cheapest at scale You manage everything
Cloudflare Pages Edge-first, generous free tier Some Node APIs unsupported

Banking note: Most banks run AWS-heavy stacks. Containerized Next.js on ECS + ALB + RDS is the typical pattern. 📄 nextjs.org/docs → "Deploying"; vercel.com/docs.

Q15o. Web Components vs React — how do they compare? A: Web Components = native browser standard (Custom Elements + Shadow DOM + HTML Templates). Framework-agnostic; encapsulated styles. React = framework owning component tree + state + reconciliation.

Web Components React
Standard Browser-native Library
Style isolation Shadow DOM (true encapsulation) Convention (CSS Modules, Tailwind)
State / reactivity Manual (custom events) Built-in (state, hooks)
Tooling Browser native Rich ecosystem
When Cross-framework design system (Lit, Stencil) App development

📄 MDN → "Web Components"; web.dev → "Custom Elements".

#TypeScript extras

Q20y. any vs unknown vs never vs void?

Means Use
any Opt-out of type checking Avoid — defeats TS
unknown Top type — must narrow before use Safe any (e.g., JSON.parse return)
never Bottom type — value never occurs Exhaustive switch checks; functions that throw
void Function returns nothing meaningful Callbacks where return is ignored
ts
function assertNever(x: never): never { throw new Error(`Unhandled: ${x}`); }
function handle(state: 'idle' | 'loading' | 'done') {
  switch (state) {
    case 'idle': return 0;
    case 'loading': return 1;
    case 'done': return 2;
    default: return assertNever(state); // compile error if a case is missed
  }
}

📄 typescriptlang.org → "Everyday Types".

Q20z. Type inference — what TS does for free? A: TS infers types from initializers, return values, default params, and contextual types:

ts
let x = 5;                          // inferred number
const arr = [1, 2, 3];              // inferred number[]
const fn = () => 'hello';           // inferred () => string
[1,2,3].map(n => n * 2);            // n inferred as number from contextual array

Rule: explicit annotations only when the inference is wrong, when defining public APIs, or when the inferred type is too wide. 📄 typescriptlang.org → "Type Inference".

Q20aa. FC<Props> vs typing the function manually?

tsx
// Manual (preferred in modern code)
type ButtonProps = { label: string; onClick: () => void };
function Button({ label, onClick }: ButtonProps) { return <button onClick={onClick}>{label}</button>; }

// FC — older convention, implicitly adds children
const Button: React.FC<ButtonProps> = ({ label, onClick }) => <button onClick={onClick}>{label}</button>;

Why prefer manual: FC historically forced an implicit children; explicit signatures are clearer and TS team's recommendation. 📄 react-typescript-cheatsheet (official cheat sheet).

Q20ab. TypeScript event types in React — onChange, onClick, form events?

tsx
function Form() {
  const onChange = (e: React.ChangeEvent<HTMLInputElement>) => e.target.value;
  const onSubmit = (e: React.FormEvent<HTMLFormElement>) => e.preventDefault();
  const onClick = (e: React.MouseEvent<HTMLButtonElement>) => {/*...*/};
  const onKey   = (e: React.KeyboardEvent<HTMLInputElement>) => e.key === 'Enter';
  const inputRef = useRef<HTMLInputElement>(null); // typed ref
  return <input ref={inputRef} onChange={onChange} onKeyDown={onKey} />;
}

Cheat-sheet pattern: React.<EventName>Event<HTMLElementType>. 📄 react-typescript-cheatsheet → "Forms and Events".

Q20ac. Typing useState, useReducer, useRef, useMemo, useCallback, React.memo?

tsx
// useState — inferred unless wider type needed
const [count, setCount] = useState(0);                      // inferred number
const [user, setUser] = useState<User | null>(null);        // explicit when initial is null

// useReducer
type Action = { type: 'inc' } | { type: 'dec' } | { type: 'set'; n: number };
function reducer(state: number, action: Action): number {
  switch (action.type) {
    case 'inc': return state + 1;
    case 'dec': return state - 1;
    case 'set': return action.n;
  }
}
const [n, dispatch] = useReducer(reducer, 0);

// useRef — two flavours
const inputRef = useRef<HTMLInputElement>(null);            // DOM ref (read-only .current init)
const timerRef = useRef<number | null>(null);               // mutable value

// useMemo / useCallback — generics inferred from return / arg types
const total = useMemo<number>(() => items.reduce((a, b) => a + b.price, 0), [items]);
const onClick = useCallback((id: string) => deleteItem(id), [deleteItem]);

// React.memo — generic preserved
const Row = React.memo(function Row({ item }: { item: Item }) { return <li>{item.name}</li>; });

📄 react-typescript-cheatsheet → "Hooks".

Q20ad. Typing Context API with dynamic default?

tsx
type Theme = 'light' | 'dark';
type ThemeContextValue = { theme: Theme; setTheme: (t: Theme) => void };

// Use null + custom hook to enforce provider wrapping
const ThemeContext = createContext<ThemeContextValue | null>(null);

export function useTheme(): ThemeContextValue {
  const ctx = useContext(ThemeContext);
  if (!ctx) throw new Error('useTheme must be used inside <ThemeProvider>');
  return ctx;
}

Why null: avoids fake default values that hide missing-provider bugs. 📄 react.dev → "createContext"; cheatsheet → "Context".

Q20ae. Mapped types, keyof, extends, and core utility types?

ts
// keyof — union of property keys
type UserKeys = keyof User;                                // 'id' | 'name' | 'email'

// Mapped type — iterate keys to build a new type
type Optional<T> = { [K in keyof T]?: T[K] };              // === Partial<T>
type ReadonlyDeep<T> = { readonly [K in keyof T]: T[K] };

// extends — conditional types and constraints
type IsString<T> = T extends string ? 'yes' : 'no';
function head<T extends unknown[]>(arr: T): T[0] { return arr[0]; }

// Built-in utility types you must name
Partial<T>      // all props optional
Required<T>     // all props required
Readonly<T>     // all props readonly
Pick<T, K>      // subset of keys
Omit<T, K>      // exclude keys
Record<K, T>    // dict { [k in K]: T }
Awaited<T>      // unwrap Promise
ReturnType<F>   // function's return
Parameters<F>   // function's arg tuple
NonNullable<T>  // T excluding null/undefined

Why for React: Partial<Props> for default-merging; Pick/Omit for derived prop types; Record for typed dictionaries; Awaited for typed await fetch().json(). 📄 typescriptlang.org → "Utility Types", "Mapped Types".

Q20af. Typing a Higher-Order Component?

tsx
function withAuth<P extends object>(Wrapped: React.ComponentType<P>) {
  return function Authed(props: P) {
    const { user } = useAuth();
    if (!user) return <Navigate to="/login" />;
    return <Wrapped {...props} />;
  };
}
const Protected = withAuth(Dashboard); // generics flow through

Tip: pattern needed only if you cannot use a hook. Hooks > HOCs in modern React. 📄 react-typescript-cheatsheet → "HOC".

Q20ag. Typing API response, Axios, fetch, and a reusable client?

ts
interface ApiSuccess<T> { ok: true; data: T }
interface ApiError      { ok: false; error: string }
type ApiResult<T> = ApiSuccess<T> | ApiError;

async function api<T>(path: string, init?: RequestInit): Promise<ApiResult<T>> {
  try {
    const res = await fetch(path, init);
    if (!res.ok) return { ok: false, error: `HTTP ${res.status}` };
    return { ok: true, data: (await res.json()) as T };
  } catch (e) {
    return { ok: false, error: e instanceof Error ? e.message : 'Unknown' };
  }
}

// Usage with full inference
const result = await api<Account>('/api/account/123');
if (result.ok) console.log(result.data.balance);

Validate at runtime with Zod (see Q20a) — TS types are erased at runtime; never trust raw JSON. 📄 typescriptlang.org → "Generics"; zod.dev.

Q20ah. Mocking API calls + ensuring type safety in Jest tests?

ts
// __mocks__/api.ts
import { jest } from '@jest/globals';
export const fetchAccount = jest.fn<(id: string) => Promise<Account>>();

// account.test.ts
import { fetchAccount } from './api';
jest.mock('./api');
test('renders balance', async () => {
  (fetchAccount as jest.Mock).mockResolvedValue({ id: '1', balance: 100 } satisfies Account);
  render(<AccountWidget id="1" />);
  expect(await screen.findByText(/100/)).toBeInTheDocument();
});

Use satisfies (TS 4.9+) so mock data conforms to the real type without losing literal info. 📄 jest docs → "Mock Functions"; ts-jest.

#Security, Auth, and Architecture (Web/Next.js)

Q20ai. JWT authentication/authorization for web — how do I use it, store it, send it, refresh it, and secure it? A: Speak about JWT as one piece of the session story, not the whole story. Flow:

  1. User logs in; server authenticates credentials.
  2. Server issues a short-lived access token (exp, iss, aud, maybe scope) and a longer-lived refresh token.
  3. Client sends the access token on API calls; server verifies signature + claims, then runs authorization.
  4. When the access token expires, client uses the refresh token to get a new access token; server should rotate the refresh token, not reuse the same one forever.

Best-practice storage/sending model for browsers:

Choice Store where Sent how Best for Main risk
httpOnly cookie Browser cookie, JS cannot read Browser sends automatically Same-site web apps, SSR/App Router CSRF if you skip SameSite/origin defenses
In-memory access token + refresh cookie Access in memory, refresh in httpOnly cookie JS adds Authorization: Bearer; refresh via cookie SPA/BFF hybrid; limits XSS blast radius Refresh complexity
localStorage / sessionStorage JS-readable storage JS adds Authorization header Only when architecture forces it XSS can steal token

How to send it correctly:

  • Same-origin cookie auth: browser sends cookie automatically.
  • Cross-origin cookie auth: fetch(url, { credentials: 'include' }) plus strict CORS and CSRF/origin protections.
  • Bearer token auth: Authorization: Bearer <access-token> header; never put JWTs in query params or URLs.

Refresh-token pattern to say out loud in interview:

  • Access token: 5-15 minutes.
  • Refresh token: days/weeks, stored in HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Lax/Strict cookie.
  • Refresh endpoint rotates refresh token on every use and revokes the previous one.
  • Logout/password reset/device compromise revokes refresh token server-side.

Things to watch out for when using JWT:

  • Don't store secrets or sensitive PII in JWT payloads; payload is readable if only signed, not encrypted.
  • Don't accept alg: none; verify algorithm/signature and validate exp, nbf, iss, aud.
  • Don't make access tokens long-lived if you need fast revocation.
  • Don't trust client-decoded roles alone; UI can hide buttons, but server must enforce permissions.

Why: interviewers want to hear that you understand JWT trade-offs, not just "JWT = stateless auth". 📄 RFC 7519 — JWT · OWASP JWT Cheat Sheet · OWASP REST Security Cheat Sheet · MDN — Secure cookie configuration

Q20aj. Security for web client and server — what belongs on each side? A: The clean mental model is: client is a convenience boundary, server is the trust boundary.

Layer Must do Must not trust
Client Escape output, sanitize rich HTML, avoid exposing secrets, show role-aware UI, handle CSRF-safe submission patterns, keep sensitive tokens out of JS when possible Hidden buttons, client validation, client role flags
Server Authenticate, authorize, validate every input, enforce rate limits, log/audit sensitive actions, set security headers, protect session lifecycle Anything coming from browser: body, headers, roles, prices, IDs

Client-side security checklist:

  • Prevent XSS: React escapes by default; sanitize dangerouslySetInnerHTML.
  • Never expose secrets via NEXT_PUBLIC_*.
  • Avoid storing sensitive data in state stores/localStorage.
  • Treat route guards and hidden UI as UX only, not security.

Server-side security checklist:

  • HTTPS everywhere.
  • Validate input at the boundary (zod, Bean Validation, etc.).
  • Authorize on every mutation and sensitive read.
  • Add idempotency for payments/transfers.
  • Keep audit logs for sensitive actions.

Why: strong frontend candidates know where the real enforcement lives and don't oversell client-side checks. 📄 OWASP Authentication Cheat Sheet · OWASP Session Management Cheat Sheet

Q20ak. Server Actions vs normal fetch / Route Handlers — security-wise and architecture-wise, what changes? A: Server Actions are not automatically "safe because server-side". They are still public endpoints; they just give you a nicer mutation model inside a Next.js app.

Axis Server Actions Route Handlers / normal API + fetch
Best use Same-app form/mutation flow Public APIs, mobile clients, third-party consumers, cross-app reuse
Built-in protections POST-only, Origin vs Host check, secure action IDs, same-host default Whatever you implement: auth, CORS, CSRF, validation
Still required AuthN, AuthZ, input validation, audit logs AuthN, AuthZ, input validation, audit logs
Trade-off Less boilerplate, tight App Router integration Clearer API contract, more reusable, easier to expose externally

Good interview framing:

  • Use Server Actions for same-app mutations when the caller is your own UI and you want less API boilerplate.
  • Use Route Handlers / fetch when you need a stable API boundary, SDK/mobile reuse, cross-origin clients, or external integrations.
  • In both cases, do permission checks in the action/handler and again in the business layer if the action is sensitive.

Why: the interviewer is checking whether you understand execution model + trust boundary, not just feature syntax. 📄 Next.js — Data Security · Next.js — Server Actions and Mutations

Q20al. Authentication and authorization methods — what should I know, and when do I use each? A: Separate them clearly:

  • Authentication (AuthN) = prove who the user is.
  • Authorization (AuthZ) = decide what that user can do.

Authentication methods worth naming:

Method Use when Strength Watch-out
Server session cookie Traditional web app, banking, SSR-heavy app Easy revocation, simple browser story Stateful backend
JWT access + refresh SPA/API ecosystems, federated systems Scales well across services Revocation/rotation complexity
OAuth2 / OIDC SSO, Google/Microsoft login, enterprise IdP Standard delegated auth More protocol complexity
Passkeys / WebAuthn / MFA High-security login Phishing resistance, stronger assurance UX and rollout complexity
Magic link / OTP Low-friction consumer login Easy onboarding Email/SMS trust and replay windows

Authorization methods worth naming:

Method Best for Strength Weakness
RBAC Internal tools, admin panels Simple mental model Too coarse as rules grow
ABAC Banking/compliance, contextual rules Fine-grained and flexible More policy complexity
ReBAC Ownership/sharing rules Natural for "owner/collaborator/member" models Harder to model/test at scale
Scopes/permissions claims API gateways and coarse API access Lightweight and portable Usually not enough for object-level checks

Best-practice line for interviews: "Use claims/scopes as a coarse filter, then do object-level authorization on the server with least privilege and deny-by-default." 📄 OWASP Authorization Cheat Sheet · OWASP Authentication Cheat Sheet

Q20am. Performance — what should I optimize in auth/security-heavy web apps? A: Optimize the boundary work, not just rendering:

  • Keep auth checks cheap: cache JWKS/public keys, avoid repeated session lookups inside one request, batch backend calls in a BFF.
  • Keep tokens small: only necessary claims; don't stuff profiles into JWTs.
  • Avoid unnecessary client hydration: if a page can be a Server Component, keep it on the server.
  • Prevent refresh storms: single-flight refresh on the client so 10 parallel 401s don't trigger 10 refresh calls.
  • Cache only what is safe: public/reference data can be cached; user-private data needs strict per-user scoping and careful invalidation.
  • Optimize mutation UX with optimistic UI only when the action is safe to roll back.

Simple answer to say in interview: "I optimize for fewer round-trips, fewer duplicate auth checks, less client JS, and correct caching boundaries. Security checks stay; I just move them to the cheapest correct layer." Why: performance in real products is usually a data-flow problem, not a useMemo problem. 📄 OWASP REST Security Cheat Sheet · Next.js — Data Security

Q20an. Software architecture for source code — what styles should I know, when do I use them, and how do they affect dependencies, testability, and maintainability? A: Know the main styles, but don't sound dogmatic. The strongest answer is usually: start with a modular monolith, keep dependency direction clean, then split only when the business/team shape demands it.

Architecture Use when Strengths Trade-offs Dependency / test impact
Layered / N-tier Small-medium CRUD/business apps Easy to understand, common team familiarity Business logic often leaks into controllers/services; downward dependency chain can get sticky Can be test-hostile if business logic depends on DB details
Modular monolith Most product teams early/mid stage One deployable unit, low ops overhead, good refactorability Needs discipline to stop module coupling Great if modules have clear seams and contracts
Clean / Onion / Hexagonal Complex domain rules, long-lived codebase, high testability need Dependency inversion, core logic isolated from frameworks/DB More abstraction and ceremony if app is simple Excellent unit-test seams; infra becomes swappable
Microservices Large org, independent teams, strong domain boundaries, separate scaling/deploy needs Independent deploy/scale, bounded contexts High ops/distributed-system cost, harder local dev/testing Reduces codebase size per service, increases integration complexity

How to compare them in interview:

  • Dependencies: good architecture makes dependencies point inward to stable abstractions, not outward to frameworks/details.
  • Testability: the less your core logic knows about HTTP/DB/UI, the easier fast unit tests become.
  • Maintainability: explicit module boundaries reduce ripple effects.
  • Scalability: first ask "team scale or traffic scale?" A modular monolith often handles both longer than people think.

Can I skip heavy architecture and still keep code maintainable/testable? Yes. You do not need "Clean Architecture everywhere" to get the benefits. Lightweight rules already go far:

  1. Organize by feature or domain, not by random utility buckets.
  2. Keep business rules in pure functions/services; keep side effects at the edges.
  3. Depend on interfaces/functions passed in, not concrete implementations everywhere.
  4. Keep UI components thin; move fetching/validation/authorization out of presentational code.
  5. Write tests at seams: pure unit tests for domain logic, integration tests for DB/API boundaries, E2E for critical flows.
  6. Prefer small modules with explicit public APIs over giant shared folders.
  7. Use ADRs/lint boundaries/code review to keep the dependency rules from eroding.

How to phrase SOLID / DRY / DIP without sounding buzzwordy:

  • SOLID: one reason to change per module, depend on abstractions, compose instead of giant inheritance trees.
  • DRY: deduplicate only after the pattern is proven; premature DRY creates the wrong abstraction.
  • DIP: high-level policy should not know concrete infra details.

Good closing line: "Architecture is mainly about controlling dependency direction and change cost. If I can keep the core logic isolated, boundaries explicit, and tests close to those boundaries, the code stays easy to change even before I introduce a heavyweight architecture." 📄 Microsoft Learn — Common web application architectures · Alistair Cockburn — Hexagonal Architecture · Martin Fowler — Monolith First · Martin Fowler — Dependency Injection

#Part A.6 — State management deep dive (Redux · Zustand · Jotai · Context · Server state)

Format: every question follows Q → A → Why it matters (rationale) → 📄 Evidence. Enterprise JDs call out "performant, testable, reusable code" — state management is where most React perf bugs and prop-drilling pain live, so this section goes deep on Redux + Zustand and surveys the rest.

#A.6.1 — The problem space

Q-SM-1. What problem does "state management" solve, and why is it more nuanced than useState? A: State in a React app comes in 5 distinct flavors, each with a different right answer:

Kind of state Example Right tool
Local UI state Modal open, dropdown index, controlled input useState colocated
Lifted / shared between siblings Form parent + 3 fields useState lifted, or useReducer
Cross-cutting client state Theme, auth user, locale React Context
App-wide complex client state Cart, multi-step wizard, undo/redo Redux / Zustand / Jotai
Server state (cache of remote data) List of accounts from API React Query / SWR / RTK Query / Server Components
URL state Filters, pagination, current tab searchParams (see Q20l)

"State management library" usually means box-4 (app-wide client state). The problem they solve: avoid prop drilling, enable predictable updates, support DevTools time-travel, and keep re-renders surgical as the app grows.

Why it matters: 80% of "do we need Redux?" questions are answered by realizing the data is server state (use React Query) or URL state (use searchParams) — not client state at all. Knowing which box you're in is the senior signal. 📄 Kent C. Dodds → "Application State Management with React"; tanstack.com/query → "Why React Query"; redux.js.org → "When (Not) To Use Redux".

Q-SM-2. The decision tree — when do I pick what?

text
Is the data fetched from a server? ────► YES ────► React Query / SWR / RTK Query / Server Components
                                          NO
                                           ▼
Does it belong in the URL? ──────────────► YES ────► useSearchParams / useRouter
                                          NO
                                           ▼
Is it used by 1 component only? ─────────► YES ────► useState colocated
                                          NO
                                           ▼
Used by 2-3 close siblings? ─────────────► YES ────► Lift state up + useReducer
                                          NO
                                           ▼
Cross-cutting (theme, auth, locale)? ────► YES ────► React Context
                                          NO
                                           ▼
Truly app-wide complex state?           ─► Zustand (small) / Redux RTK (large, audited) / Jotai (atomic)

Why: state managers are easy to over-reach for. Reaching too early creates ceremony; reaching too late creates prop-drilling and stale-closure bugs. 📄 redux.js.org → "When (Not) To Use Redux"; React docs → "Managing State".

#A.6.2 — Mechanism deep-dives

Q-SM-3. How does Redux work mechanically? (Flux + reducer model) A: Redux enforces a unidirectional data flow with one store, pure reducers, and dispatched actions:

text
   ┌──────────────┐     dispatch(action)       ┌──────────────┐
   │  Component   │ ─────────────────────────► │     Store    │
   └──────────────┘                            │ (single tree)│
          ▲                                    └──────┬───────┘
          │                                           │ reducer(state, action) → newState
          │ useSelector(state => slice)               ▼
          └─────────────────────────────────  newState (immutable)
                       subscribers re-render only if their selector returns a different value

Core invariants:

  1. Single source of truth — one store object holds the entire app state tree.
  2. State is read-only — only way to change is to dispatch an action { type, payload }.
  3. Pure reducers — (state, action) => newState. No side effects, no mutation.
  4. Subscribers — useSelector(fn) re-renders only when the selected slice changes (=== by default).

Modern Redux = Redux Toolkit (RTK): createSlice writes reducer + actions for you, uses Immer so you can write "mutating" code that's actually safe, includes redux-thunk for async, and createAsyncThunk / RTK Query for server data.

Why: the discipline pays for itself in time-travel debugging (DevTools replay every action), audit trails (banking compliance), and team-scale predictability. 📄 redux.js.org → "Three Principles", "Style Guide"; redux-toolkit.js.org.

Q-SM-4. How does Zustand work mechanically? A: Zustand is a tiny (~1KB) library that creates a store as a React hook using closures + useSyncExternalStore. No provider needed, no actions, no reducers — just a function that returns state + setters.

ts
import { create } from 'zustand';

interface CartState {
  items: Item[];
  addItem: (item: Item) => void;
  removeItem: (id: string) => void;
  total: () => number;          // derived selector
}

export const useCartStore = create<CartState>((set, get) => ({
  items: [],
  addItem: (item) => set((s) => ({ items: [...s.items, item] })),  // s = current snapshot
  removeItem: (id) => set((s) => ({ items: s.items.filter(i => i.id !== id) })),
  total: () => get().items.reduce((sum, i) => sum + i.price, 0),
}));

// Component
function Cart() {
  const items = useCartStore((s) => s.items);                        // selector subscribes
  const total = useCartStore((s) => s.total());
  const remove = useCartStore((s) => s.removeItem);                  // actions don't re-render
  return <ul>{items.map(i => <li key={i.id}>{i.name} <button onClick={() => remove(i.id)}>X</button></li>)}</ul>;
}

How it works under the hood:

  1. create(stateInitializer) returns a hook bound to a closure-held store.
  2. set shallow-merges (or replaces) state and notifies subscribers.
  3. Each useStore(selector) subscribes via useSyncExternalStore (React 18+) — re-renders only if selector(state) changed (Object.is by default; pass shallow for object/array selections).
  4. Outside React you can call useCartStore.getState() and useCartStore.setState(...) — no hook needed (great for event listeners, route guards, tests).

Why: Zustand wins on ergonomics — no boilerplate, no provider, works outside React. Trade-off: weaker DevTools and conventions vs Redux. Best for small/medium apps and library-internal state. 📄 docs.pmnd.rs/zustand; "Zustand source" — ~200 lines, worth reading.

Q-SM-5. How does Jotai work mechanically? (atomic model) A: Jotai inverts Redux's "one big store" into many tiny atoms. An atom is a primitive piece of state with a key, write, and read. Components subscribe to atoms they actually use → fine-grained re-renders.

ts
import { atom, useAtom } from 'jotai';

const countAtom = atom(0);                              // primitive atom
const doubleCountAtom = atom((get) => get(countAtom) * 2);  // derived atom (read-only)
const writeOnlyAtom = atom(null, (get, set, newVal: number) => {
  set(countAtom, newVal);
});

function Counter() {
  const [count, setCount] = useAtom(countAtom);
  const [double] = useAtom(doubleCountAtom);
  return <button onClick={() => setCount(c => c + 1)}>{count} (×2 = {double})</button>;
}

Mental model: Recoil-inspired graph. Each atom = node; derived atoms = edges. React tracks which atoms a component reads and re-renders only when those change.

Why: atomic granularity = no selector boilerplate, perfect for highly-composed UIs (forms with many fields, design tools). Trade-off: less convention than Redux, harder to reason about a global state shape, smaller community. 📄 jotai.org/docs.

Q-SM-6. Other notables — quick table.

Library Mental model Bundle DevTools Async first-class Best for
Redux Toolkit Single store + reducers + Immer ~12KB Excellent (time-travel) RTK Query / thunks Large, audited, team-scale
Zustand Hook-store with closures ~1KB Good (Redux DevTools middleware) Manual + middleware Small/medium, library-internal
Jotai Atom graph ~3KB Good atomWithSuspense Many independent state pieces
Recoil Atoms + selectors (Meta) ~14KB OK First-class async selectors Effectively replaced by Jotai
MobX Observable + auto-tracking ~16KB Good flow / async actions OO codebases preferring mutation
Valtio Proxy-based mutable state ~3KB OK Manual "Just mutate it" feel
XState Finite state machines ~13KB Inspector Yes (services) Complex flows / wizards

Why: Banking pages can have 8+ state shapes (auth, layout, transfers, drafts). Knowing the spectrum lets you pick the smallest fit. 📄 each lib's homepage; npm bundle-phobia.

#A.6.3 — Redux Toolkit deep-dive (Q&A)

Q-SM-7. Implement an end-to-end RTK setup with TypeScript — store, slice, selectors, async, hooks.

ts
// store/index.ts
import { configureStore, combineReducers } from '@reduxjs/toolkit';
import { TypedUseSelectorHook, useDispatch, useSelector } from 'react-redux';
import { accountSlice } from './slices/account';
import { uiSlice } from './slices/ui';

export const store = configureStore({
  reducer: combineReducers({
    account: accountSlice.reducer,
    ui: uiSlice.reducer,
  }),
  middleware: (getDefault) => getDefault({ serializableCheck: { ignoredActions: ['x/y'] } }),
  devTools: process.env.NODE_ENV !== 'production',
});

export type RootState = ReturnType<typeof store.getState>;
export type AppDispatch = typeof store.dispatch;
export const useAppDispatch = () => useDispatch<AppDispatch>();
export const useAppSelector: TypedUseSelectorHook<RootState> = useSelector;

// store/slices/account.ts
import { createSlice, createAsyncThunk, PayloadAction } from '@reduxjs/toolkit';

interface AccountState { items: Account[]; status: 'idle' | 'loading' | 'error'; error?: string }
const initial: AccountState = { items: [], status: 'idle' };

export const fetchAccounts = createAsyncThunk<Account[], string, { rejectValue: string }>(
  'account/fetch',
  async (userId, { rejectWithValue }) => {
    const res = await fetch(`/api/users/${userId}/accounts`);
    if (!res.ok) return rejectWithValue(`HTTP ${res.status}`);
    return res.json();
  }
);

export const accountSlice = createSlice({
  name: 'account',
  initialState: initial,
  reducers: {
    addLocalDraft(state, action: PayloadAction<Account>) {
      state.items.push(action.payload); // Immer — looks mutating, is safe
    },
  },
  extraReducers: (builder) => {
    builder
      .addCase(fetchAccounts.pending,   (s) => { s.status = 'loading'; s.error = undefined; })
      .addCase(fetchAccounts.fulfilled, (s, a) => { s.status = 'idle'; s.items = a.payload; })
      .addCase(fetchAccounts.rejected,  (s, a) => { s.status = 'error'; s.error = a.payload ?? 'unknown'; });
  },
});

// Memoized selector — co-locate with slice
import { createSelector } from '@reduxjs/toolkit';
export const selectAccounts        = (s: RootState) => s.account.items;
export const selectActiveAccounts  = createSelector(selectAccounts, (xs) => xs.filter(a => a.active));

// Component
function AccountList() {
  const accounts = useAppSelector(selectActiveAccounts);
  const status   = useAppSelector(s => s.account.status);
  const dispatch = useAppDispatch();
  useEffect(() => { dispatch(fetchAccounts(currentUserId)); }, []);
  if (status === 'loading') return <Skeleton />;
  return <ul>{accounts.map(a => <li key={a.id}>{a.name}</li>)}</ul>;
}

Why: this is the canonical RTK template. createSlice removes 80% of legacy Redux boilerplate. createAsyncThunk handles pending/fulfilled/rejected. createSelector (reselect) memoizes derived data so referential equality is preserved across renders. 📄 redux-toolkit.js.org → "TypeScript Quick Start", "createAsyncThunk", "createSelector".

Q-SM-8. RTK Query — what is it and when to use it instead of createAsyncThunk + slice? A: RTK Query is RTK's built-in server-state solution: define endpoints once, get auto-generated hooks with caching, dedup, polling, optimistic updates, invalidation tags. Replaces 90% of useEffect + fetch + manual loading state.

ts
// store/api.ts
import { createApi, fetchBaseQuery } from '@reduxjs/toolkit/query/react';
export const api = createApi({
  reducerPath: 'api',
  baseQuery: fetchBaseQuery({ baseUrl: '/api', prepareHeaders: (h) => { h.set('x-csrf', csrf()); return h; } }),
  tagTypes: ['Account'],
  endpoints: (build) => ({
    getAccounts: build.query<Account[], string>({
      query: (userId) => `users/${userId}/accounts`,
      providesTags: (result) => result ? result.map(a => ({ type: 'Account', id: a.id })) : [],
    }),
    transfer: build.mutation<void, { from: string; to: string; amount: number }>({
      query: (body) => ({ url: 'transfer', method: 'POST', body }),
      invalidatesTags: ['Account'],            // refetches affected queries automatically
    }),
  }),
});

export const { useGetAccountsQuery, useTransferMutation } = api;

// Component
function AccountList({ userId }) {
  const { data, isLoading, error, refetch } = useGetAccountsQuery(userId, { pollingInterval: 30_000 });
  // ...
}

Why: RTK Query unifies client + server state under one store. Use it when you're already on Redux and want to retire bespoke fetch hooks. Skip it when the app has only a couple of fetch points — React Query is lighter and more capable for pure server state. 📄 redux-toolkit.js.org/rtk-query.

Q-SM-9. Redux selector optimization — useSelector re-render rules and memoization? A: Two rules:

  1. useSelector(fn) re-renders the component when fn(state) !== fn(prevState) (default ===). So selectors that return new objects/arrays each call ALWAYS re-render.
  2. To select an object/array, either return primitives, pass shallowEqual, or use createSelector.
ts
// ❌ Re-renders every dispatch — new array each call
const filtered = useAppSelector((s) => s.account.items.filter(a => a.active));

// ✅ Memoized via createSelector — referentially stable when inputs unchanged
const selectActive = createSelector(
  (s: RootState) => s.account.items,
  (items) => items.filter(a => a.active)
);
const filtered = useAppSelector(selectActive);

// ✅ Or shallow equality for object aggregations
import { shallowEqual } from 'react-redux';
const { name, balance } = useAppSelector(s => ({ name: s.account.name, balance: s.account.balance }), shallowEqual);

Why: a single naive selector at the top of the tree can re-render 100 components on every keystroke. This is the #1 Redux performance bug. 📄 react-redux docs → "useSelector"; reselect README.

Q-SM-10. Redux DevTools, time-travel, and middleware? A: Redux DevTools (browser extension) records every dispatched action + state diff → step backward/forward through history, replay, export sessions, dispatch from console. Banking value: reproduce production bugs from a serialized action log.

Middleware sits between dispatch and reducers. Built-ins: redux-thunk (functions as actions), redux-logger. RTK Query is itself a middleware. Custom: audit logger, analytics, websocket bridge.

ts
const audit: Middleware = (store) => (next) => (action) => {
  const result = next(action);
  if (action.type.startsWith('account/')) auditLog.send({ ts: Date.now(), action, after: store.getState().account });
  return result;
};

📄 redux.js.org → "Middleware"; redux-devtools docs.

#A.6.4 — Zustand deep-dive

Q-SM-11. Zustand patterns — slices, middleware (persist, devtools, immer), and selectors with shallow.

ts
import { create } from 'zustand';
import { devtools, persist, subscribeWithSelector } from 'zustand/middleware';
import { immer } from 'zustand/middleware/immer';
import { shallow } from 'zustand/shallow';

// Slice pattern — split a large store across files
const createAccountSlice = (set, get) => ({
  accounts: [] as Account[],
  loadAccounts: async () => {
    const res = await fetch('/api/accounts').then(r => r.json());
    set((s) => { s.accounts = res; });   // immer middleware → can mutate
  },
});
const createUiSlice = (set) => ({
  sidebarOpen: false,
  toggleSidebar: () => set((s) => { s.sidebarOpen = !s.sidebarOpen; }),
});

export const useAppStore = create<AppState>()(
  devtools(                                       // Redux DevTools support
    persist(                                      // localStorage persistence
      subscribeWithSelector(                      // imperative subscribe outside React
        immer((set, get) => ({
          ...createAccountSlice(set, get),
          ...createUiSlice(set),
        }))
      ),
      { name: 'app-store', partialize: (s) => ({ ui: { sidebarOpen: s.sidebarOpen } }) }, // pick what to persist
    ),
    { name: 'app' }
  )
);

// Component — shallow comparator for object selections
function Header() {
  const { sidebarOpen, toggleSidebar } = useAppStore(
    (s) => ({ sidebarOpen: s.sidebarOpen, toggleSidebar: s.toggleSidebar }),
    shallow                                        // avoid re-render on unrelated state changes
  );
}

// Outside React (e.g., Next.js middleware or a saga)
useAppStore.getState().loadAccounts();
const unsub = useAppStore.subscribe((s) => s.accounts, (next, prev) => { /* react to change */ });

Why: persistence + DevTools + Immer cover the practical needs that lead teams to Redux. Slice + middleware composition keeps Zustand code readable at scale. 📄 docs.pmnd.rs/zustand → "Recipes", "Middleware".

Q-SM-12. Zustand performance — selector pitfalls?

  • Default comparator is Object.is. A selector returning a new object/array on each call always re-renders — pass shallow (object) or write multiple narrow selectors.
  • Prefer "one selector per primitive" over a wide tuple selector.
  • Actions live in the same store as state; subscribing via useStore(s => s.action) does NOT cause re-renders for state changes (function ref is stable).
ts
// ❌ new object every render → constant re-renders
const { name, balance } = useStore((s) => ({ name: s.name, balance: s.balance }));

// ✅ Two narrow selectors
const name    = useStore((s) => s.name);
const balance = useStore((s) => s.balance);

// ✅ Or shallow comparator
const { name, balance } = useStore((s) => ({ name: s.name, balance: s.balance }), shallow);

📄 docs.pmnd.rs/zustand → "Selecting multiple state slices".

#A.6.5 — Comparing the libraries (interview-grade table)

Q-SM-13. Redux RTK vs Zustand vs Jotai vs Context — the senior-level comparison.

Dimension Redux Toolkit Zustand Jotai React Context
Mental model Single store + reducers + actions Hook-store with closures Atom graph Provider tree
Boilerplate Low (with RTK) Lowest Low Medium (custom hooks)
TypeScript ergonomics Excellent Excellent Good Manual
Re-render granularity Selector + memoization Selector Per-atom (finest) Whole subtree of provider
Time-travel DevTools ✅ Best-in-class ✅ via middleware ✅ via Atom DevTools ❌
Async server state RTK Query / thunks Manual / middleware atomWithQuery ❌
Persistence redux-persist persist middleware atomWithStorage Manual
Bundle size ~12KB ~1KB ~3KB 0 (built-in)
Outside React store.dispatch / store.getState useStore.getState() / setState() store.get/set (lower-level API) ❌
Banking suitability (audit) ✅ Strong (action log) ⚠️ Need devtools middleware ⚠️ Less conventional ❌
When to pick Big team, audit needs, server state via RTK Query Small/medium app, internal libs, hooks-only Many independent state pieces, fine-grained reactivity Theme/auth/locale only

Why: every team eventually re-litigates this choice. Senior signal = articulate the trade-off in 30 seconds without dogma. 📄 each library's docs; npm bundle-phobia.

#A.6.6 — API integration patterns

Q-SM-14. How do I integrate API calls — Redux thunks vs RTK Query vs React Query vs Zustand actions?

Pattern Code When
Server Components fetch await db.query(...) in a Server Component Next.js 13+ default — most pages
React Query useQuery({ queryKey, queryFn }) Client components needing cache + dedup + polling. Library-agnostic.
RTK Query useGetXyzQuery() from createApi Already on Redux — unify client + server state
Redux thunk + slice createAsyncThunk + extraReducers Pre-RTK Query Redux apps; or when server state shape needs custom processing
Zustand action set(...) after await fetch inside a store function Small Zustand apps; one-off needs
SWR useSWR(key, fetcher) Vercel-stack apps, very lightweight

Universal rules for any of them:

  1. Cancel on unmount / dependency change — AbortController in fetcher, lib handles for you in React Query / SWR.
  2. Loading + error are first-class — UI must render 3 states.
  3. Stale-while-revalidate is usually what users want — show stale instantly, refresh in background.
  4. Invalidation — after a mutation (useTransferMutation), invalidate affected queries by key/tag so UI auto-refreshes.
ts
// React Query — full pattern
function Accounts({ userId }) {
  const queryClient = useQueryClient();
  const { data, isLoading, error } = useQuery({
    queryKey: ['accounts', userId],
    queryFn: ({ signal }) => fetch(`/api/accounts?u=${userId}`, { signal }).then(r => r.json()),
    staleTime: 30_000,
  });
  const transfer = useMutation({
    mutationFn: (body: TransferReq) => fetch('/api/transfer', { method: 'POST', body: JSON.stringify(body) }),
    onSuccess: () => queryClient.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ['accounts', userId] }),
  });
  // ...
}

Why: choosing the right tool per kind of state matters more than choosing one library for everything. React Query is the de-facto industry standard for client-side server state — independent from Redux/Zustand. 📄 tanstack.com/query/latest; redux-toolkit.js.org/rtk-query; swr.vercel.app.

Q-SM-15. Optimistic updates with each manager?

  • Redux RTK Query: onQueryStarted async block — patch cache, await server, undo on error.
  • React Query: onMutate callback returns rollback context, onError calls rollback.
  • Zustand: snapshot state in the action; revert in catch (manual).
  • React 19 / Server Actions: useOptimistic (see Q20m).

📄 tanstack.com/query → "Optimistic Updates"; redux-toolkit.js.org → "Optimistic Updates with RTK Query".

#A.6.7 — Performance, testing, security, SSR

Cross-ref: For the broader interview framing on JWT storage/refresh, Server Actions vs fetch, authn/authz methods, and architecture trade-offs, see Q20ai–Q20an right above this state-management section.

Q-SM-16. Performance optimization checklist for state managers.

Lever Redux Zustand Jotai
Narrow selectors createSelector (reselect) one-prop selectors / shallow per-atom hook
Avoid object/array re-creation memoize derived memoize or shallow derived atoms cached
Split stores Multiple slices, single store Multiple stores OK Atoms — granular by design
Batch updates React 18 auto-batches React 18 auto-batches React 18 auto-batches
DevTools off in prod devTools: false devtools middleware off atomDevtoolsEnabled false
Profile React DevTools "Why did this render?" same same

Why: most "Redux is slow" complaints are unmemoized selectors. Profile before optimizing. 📄 react-redux docs → "Performance"; React DevTools Profiler.

Q-SM-17. Security — what NEVER goes in a client-side state store?

  • Auth tokens (long-lived) — keep in httpOnly cookies; the server reads them. Storing JWT in Redux/Zustand = XSS risk (any injected script can read the store).
  • PII / account numbers / CVV / passwords — stay server-side only.
  • API secrets / signing keys — never on the client at all (NEXT_PUBLIC_* is exposed).
  • Admin flags as the source of truth — UI can show role-aware UI based on store, but every server endpoint must re-check authorization. Never rely on client store for authz decisions.

What IS OK: user display fields (name, email), feature flags, UI prefs, draft form state, server data the user is already authorized to see.

For banking specifically: redact account numbers in audit logs, do not persist state with sensitive data via redux-persist/Zustand persist to localStorage. If you must persist, use sessionStorage + idle-timeout clear (see Q60). 📄 OWASP "Sensitive Data Exposure"; "Storing tokens" guidelines.

Q-SM-18. Testing state managers?

  • Redux — reducers are pure functions; test directly: expect(reducer(prevState, { type: 'addItem', payload: x })).toEqual(expected). Test thunks with a mock store (@reduxjs/toolkit's test utilities) or by calling the inner async function with a mock dispatch. Test components with a real <Provider store={testStore}>.
  • Zustand — call the hook outside React via useStore.getState()/setState() in tests. Reset between tests with a beforeEach(() => useStore.setState(initial, true)). RTL components with the store work without any special wrapper.
  • Jotai — wrap the test root in <Provider> to scope atom state per test; or use useHydrateAtoms to seed.
  • React Query — wrap components in a <QueryClientProvider> with a fresh QueryClient per test; use MSW for network mocks.
ts
// Zustand test pattern
import { renderHook, act } from '@testing-library/react';
import { useCartStore } from './store';
beforeEach(() => useCartStore.setState({ items: [] }, true));
test('adds an item', () => {
  act(() => useCartStore.getState().addItem({ id: '1', name: 'X', price: 10 }));
  expect(useCartStore.getState().items).toHaveLength(1);
});

Why: pure-function testing is one of Redux's strengths; Zustand's external API makes it almost as testable. State integration tests (RTL + provider) catch the wiring bugs unit tests miss. 📄 react-redux → "Writing Tests"; docs.pmnd.rs/zustand → "Testing".

Q-SM-19. State managers in Next.js App Router — SSR pitfalls? A: Three rules:

  1. Never share a Redux/Zustand/Jotai store between requests on the server. The store must be per-request. Default Next.js patterns assume Server Components + serializable props — the client store hydrates from props, not from a global module-level store.
  2. Server Components cannot subscribe to a client store — they have no React state. Pass server-fetched data down as props; let Client Components hydrate the store with it.
  3. Persist hydration mismatch — redux-persist / Zustand persist read localStorage, which doesn't exist on the server → hydration errors. Use skipHydration: true and trigger rehydrate in a useEffect, OR use next-redux-wrapper (Pages Router) / a per-request store factory (App Router).
tsx
// store/StoreProvider.tsx — per-request store factory pattern
'use client';
import { useRef } from 'react';
import { Provider } from 'react-redux';
import { makeStore, AppStore } from './store';
export default function StoreProvider({ children, initialState }: { children: React.ReactNode; initialState: any }) {
  const ref = useRef<AppStore>();
  if (!ref.current) ref.current = makeStore(initialState);  // created once per render tree
  return <Provider store={ref.current}>{children}</Provider>;
}

// app/layout.tsx
import StoreProvider from '@/store/StoreProvider';
export default async function RootLayout({ children }) {
  const session = await getServerSession();                 // Server Component fetches
  return <html><body><StoreProvider initialState={{ user: session?.user }}>{children}</StoreProvider></body></html>;
}

Why: Production deploys run on multi-instance servers. Sharing a singleton store across requests = data leakage between users — a banking-grade incident. 📄 redux.js.org → "Usage with Next.js (App Router)"; nextjs.org/docs → "Server and Client Components".

#A.6.8 — Spoken summary (have these ready)

"For local UI it's useState. For URL-shaped state it's searchParams. For server data it's React Query — or RTK Query if we're already on Redux. For client cross-cutting state I default to Zustand because it's 1KB and ergonomic; I reach for Redux Toolkit when we need audit-grade time-travel DevTools or the team is large enough that the convention pays for itself. Jotai is great for forms and design tools where atoms map to fields. The biggest performance trap is unmemoized selectors that return new arrays — I use reselect or shallow to fix it. Security-wise, no tokens or PII in the store; persist sparingly, never to localStorage with sensitive data. In Next.js App Router I use a per-request store factory to prevent cross-request data leakage."

#4. Part B — JavaScript / HTML / CSS / Web Q&A

JD-gap cross-refs: see also Q-JD-2/Q-JD-3 (GraphQL — desirable per JD), Q-JD-10 (legacy stack: Bootstrap / Foundation / jQuery / JSON), Q-JD-11 (UI design literacy). All in §1.5.3.

Q21. Closures — what & a use case? A: A function that remembers variables from its lexical (outer) scope after the outer function has returned. The inner function holds a live reference to those variables, not a copy.

js
// Classic example — private counter
function createCounter() {
  let count = 0;                    // captured by closure
  return {
    increment() { count += 1; return count; },
    decrement() { count -= 1; return count; },
    getValue()  { return count; },
  };
}
const c = createCounter();
c.increment(); c.increment(); // 2
// `count` is unreachable from outside — true encapsulation

5 canonical use cases:

Use case Why closure
Private state (module pattern) Vars accessible only via returned methods
Function factory (makeMultiplier(2)) Inner fn captures the factory's parameter
Debounce / throttle Captures timerId across calls
Memoization Cache object captured by returned fn
React hooks useState/useEffect rely on closure over the render's scope

Classic gotcha — var in loop:

js
for (var i = 0; i < 3; i++) setTimeout(() => console.log(i), 0);
// Prints: 3, 3, 3 — single `i` shared by all closures (function-scoped)

for (let i = 0; i < 3; i++) setTimeout(() => console.log(i), 0);
// Prints: 0, 1, 2 — `let` creates a new binding per iteration

📄 MDN → "Closures".

Q22. == vs ===? A: === strict, no coercion. == coerces types (avoid). Always use === except x == null to catch both null & undefined. 📄 MDN → "Equality comparisons".

Q23. var / let / const + hoisting + TDZ? A: var function-scoped, hoisted, initialized undefined. let/const block-scoped, hoisted but in Temporal Dead Zone until declared (ReferenceError if accessed early). const = no reassignment (object contents still mutable). 📄 MDN → "let", "Hoisting", "TDZ".

Q24. Event loop / microtask vs macrotask? A: Single-threaded. Call stack runs sync code. Async callbacks queue: microtasks (Promises, queueMicrotask) drain fully before next macrotask (setTimeout, I/O). That's why .then runs before setTimeout(0).

text
Call Stack (sync)
  ↓ when empty
Microtask Queue (drain ALL before next macrotask)
  → Promise.then, async/await, queueMicrotask, MutationObserver
  ↓ when empty
Macrotask Queue (1 at a time, then check microtasks again)
  → setTimeout, setInterval, setImmediate, I/O callbacks
js
console.log('1');                        // sync
setTimeout(() => console.log('2'), 0);   // macrotask
Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log('3')); // microtask
console.log('4');                        // sync
// Output: 1, 4, 3, 2
Microtask Macrotask
Examples Promise.then, async/await, queueMicrotask setTimeout, setInterval, I/O
Priority High — drains completely first Low — 1 per event loop turn
Risk Infinite microtask loop blocks UI forever Safe — UI gets paint between macrotasks
📄 MDN → "Event loop"; "Microtask guide".

Q25. Promises vs async/await; how to run in parallel? A: Promise = eventual value (pending→fulfilled/rejected). async/await is syntactic sugar — cleaner sequential flow, try/catch errors. Parallel: Promise.all (fail-fast), Promise.allSettled (all results), Promise.race. Awaiting in a loop = sequential (often a bug). 📄 MDN → "Promise", "async function".

Q26. this binding rules? A: Determined by call site: default (global/undefined in strict), implicit (obj.fn() → obj), explicit (call/apply/bind), new (new instance), arrow (lexical this — inherits enclosing scope, can't rebind).

Call pattern this
obj.method() obj
fn() plain call undefined (strict) / window (non-strict)
const f = obj.method; f() undefined — context lost
fn.call(ctx, arg1) ctx
fn.apply(ctx, [args]) ctx
