fn.bind(ctx)() ctx — permanently bound
() => ... arrow Lexical outer scope — can't rebind
setTimeout(fn, 100) undefined — plain call internally

Trap: callback loses context

js
const user = { name: 'Tri', greet() { return this.name; } };
const fn = user.greet;
fn(); // undefined ❌ — plain call, lost context
setTimeout(user.greet, 100); // undefined ❌
// Fix: bind or arrow wrapper
setTimeout(user.greet.bind(user), 100); // 'Tri' ✅
setTimeout(() => user.greet(), 100);    // 'Tri' ✅

Q26a. call vs apply vs bind — when to use each?

Method Calls immediately? Args format Returns Use for
call(ctx, a, b) ✅ Yes List Result Method borrowing
apply(ctx, [a,b]) ✅ Yes Array Result Dynamic args (spread pre-ES6)
bind(ctx, a) ❌ No List (partial) New function Callbacks, partial application
js
// call — method borrowing
const wallet = { total: 1000 };
calculator.add.call(wallet, 500); // runs add() with this=wallet

// apply — spread array into args
Math.max.apply(null, [5, 2, 8]); // 8 — ES6 equiv: Math.max(...arr)

// bind — stable callback reference
class PaymentForm {
  constructor() {
    this.handleSubmit = this.handleSubmit.bind(this); // this = instance always
  }
  handleSubmit(e) { console.log(this.amount); }
}

// bind for partial application (curry-lite)
const double = multiply.bind(null, 2); // locks first arg
double(5); // 10

// Trap: bind only works once — second bind() ignored
const f2 = fn.bind(obj2); // won't override first bind

Arrow functions: call/apply/bind have NO effect on this — they still use lexical scope. 📄 MDN → "this"; MDN → "Function.call", "Function.apply", "Function.bind".

Q26b. this inside a returned function (closure trap) — why does it break? A: When a method returns a plain function, that inner function is stored and later called without an object before it — a plain call — so this is undefined (strict) or window (non-strict). The closure only captures variables, NOT the caller's this.

js
// The trap — method returns inner function
const booking = {
  destination: 'Da Nang',
  getLogger() {
    // this = booking here (method call)
    return function () {
      console.log(this.destination); // ❌ undefined — plain call, this is gone
    };
  },
};
const log = booking.getLogger();
log(); // undefined ❌

Why: log() is called as a plain function — no booking. prefix — so JavaScript binds this to undefined, not booking. The fact that log was born inside getLogger doesn't help.

Three fixes:

js
// Fix 1 — Arrow function (preferred, ES6+)
// Arrow has no own this; captures lexical this from getLogger's scope
getLogger() {
  return () => {
    console.log(this.destination); // ✅ this = booking (lexical capture)
  };
}

// Fix 2 — Save this (pre-ES6, still common in older codebases)
getLogger() {
  const self = this; // snapshot at method call time
  return function () {
    console.log(self.destination); // ✅ closure over variable, not this
  };
}

// Fix 3 — bind
getLogger() {
  return function () {
    console.log(this.destination);
  }.bind(this); // ✅ permanently locks this = booking
}

Nested levels — all returned functions lose this:

js
const app = {
  name: 'TravelPro',
  level1() {
    return function level2() {   // ❌ this lost at level2
      return function level3() { // ❌ this lost at level3
        console.log(this.name);  // undefined
      };
    };
  },
};

// Fix: arrow functions at every level
level1() {
  return () => {               // ✅ captures this = app
    return () => {             // ✅ still app (arrow chains lexically)
      console.log(this.name);  // 'TravelPro' ✅
    };
  };
}

Why debounce/throttle use fn.apply(this, args) not fn(...args):

js
const searchService = {
  endpoint: '/api/search',
  search: debounce(function (query) {
    fetch(`${this.endpoint}?q=${query}`); // this must = searchService
  }, 300),
};
searchService.search('flights');
// debounced wrapper receives this = searchService (method call)
// fn.apply(this, args) → forwards it to the real fn ✅
// fn(...args)          → plain call, this = undefined ❌

Interview answer (15 seconds): "A returned plain function is called with no receiver — it's a plain call, so this is undefined. Arrow functions fix this because they have no own this — they inherit lexically from the enclosing scope at the time they're defined."

Q26c. What exactly is a "plain function call"? Why strict vs non-strict matters?

A plain function call = invoking a function without an object before the dot. The single biggest source of lost-this bugs.

Call pattern Plain call? this
user.greet() ❌ No — method call user
greet() ✅ Yes undefined (strict) / window (sloppy)
const fn = user.greet; fn() ✅ Yes — receiver lost when assigned undefined / window
setTimeout(user.greet, 100) ✅ Yes — runtime calls it bare undefined / window
[1,2].forEach(user.greet) ✅ Yes — forEach invokes it bare undefined / window

Strict vs non-strict — why the difference exists:

js
// Non-strict (legacy / classic <script>): JS auto-coerces `this` to globalThis
function show() { console.log(this); }
show(); // window (browser) — silent footgun: typos on `this.foo` become globals

// Strict (modules, React/Next.js, TS — always on): JS leaves `this` as undefined
'use strict';
function show() { console.log(this); }
show(); // undefined — errors surface immediately on `this.foo`

Rule: React/Next.js code is always strict mode (ES modules are strict by default). Treat this === undefined as the expected outcome of any plain call.

DOM event handlers — special case: When you attach a regular function via addEventListener, the browser calls it with this = element:

js
btn.addEventListener('click', function () {
  console.log(this); // the <button> element ✅ — browser sets this for you
});
btn.addEventListener('click', () => {
  console.log(this); // OUTER lexical this — arrow has no own this
});

Q26d. Top 4 this myth-busters interviewers love to probe.

Myth Truth
❌ "this depends on where the function is declared" ✅ For regular functions, this depends on the call site (how it's invoked). Only arrow functions follow lexical declaration site.
❌ "Arrow functions have their own this" ✅ Arrow functions have no own this. They look up the enclosing lexical scope. call/apply/bind cannot change it.
❌ "If a function is defined inside a method, it inherits this" ✅ It does not. The inner regular function loses this because it's later called as a plain function. Use arrow or bind.
❌ "I can bind multiple times to override" ✅ First bind wins forever. fn.bind(a).bind(b) — second bind is ignored.

Interview soundbite (10 seconds): "this is determined by the call site, not the declaration site — except for arrow functions, which capture the enclosing lexical this and cannot be rebound."

📄 MDN → "this", "Strict mode"; "Function.prototype.bind".

Q27. Debounce vs throttle? A: Debounce = run after N ms of silence (search input). Throttle = run at most once per N ms (scroll/resize). Both limit handler frequency.

Debounce Throttle
Core idea Wait for silence, then run once Run at most once per N ms
When event streams continuously Nothing runs until pause Runs every N ms
Use for Search input, auto-save, resize end Scroll, drag, mousemove, resize progress
Core line clearTimeout(id) before new setTimeout if (now - lastRun >= wait) check

requestAnimationFrame throttle — for visual/animation work:

js
function rafThrottle(fn) {
  let ticking = false;
  return function(...args) {
    if (!ticking) {
      ticking = true;
      requestAnimationFrame(() => { fn.apply(this, args); ticking = false; });
    }
  };
}

Use rafThrottle (not throttle(fn, 16)) for: drag position, parallax, canvas updates — runs exactly once per frame, synced to GPU paint cycle. Use normal throttle for: scroll spy, infinite scroll (timing doesn't need to match paint). 📄 MDN → "requestAnimationFrame"; lodash docs.

Q27a. Write a full debounce with leading, trailing, cancel, and flush. A: Basic debounce only supports trailing (runs after silence). Production debounce adds leading (fire on first call immediately — good for button click feedback), cancel (needed on component unmount to prevent stale state updates), and flush (force-run now if pending — useful before form submit navigates away).

js
function debounce(fn, delay = 300, { leading = false, trailing = true } = {}) {
  let timerId = null;
  let lastArgs = null;
  let lastThis = null;

  function debounced(...args) {
    lastArgs = args;
    lastThis = this;

    // Leading edge: fire immediately if no timer is running
    if (leading && timerId === null) {
      fn.apply(lastThis, lastArgs);
    }

    clearTimeout(timerId); // reset timer on every call

    timerId = setTimeout(() => {
      // Trailing edge: fire after silence ends
      if (trailing && lastArgs !== null) {
        fn.apply(lastThis, lastArgs);
      }
      timerId = null;
      lastArgs = null;
      lastThis = null;
    }, delay);
  }

  // Cancel: discard pending call — use in useEffect cleanup
  debounced.cancel = function () {
    clearTimeout(timerId);
    timerId = null;
    lastArgs = null;
    lastThis = null;
  };

  // Flush: run immediately if a call is pending, then reset
  debounced.flush = function () {
    if (timerId !== null && lastArgs !== null) {
      clearTimeout(timerId);
      fn.apply(lastThis, lastArgs);
      timerId = null;
      lastArgs = null;
      lastThis = null;
    }
  };

  return debounced;
}

When each option matters:

Option Default Use case
leading: true false Button click — instant feedback on first press, ignore rapid re-clicks
trailing: true true Search input — run after user stops typing
leading + trailing — First press fires immediately; last call in burst also fires
cancel() — useEffect cleanup — prevents updating unmounted component state
flush() — Form onSubmit — force-run pending search before navigating

React hook pattern:

tsx
function useDebouncedCallback(fn: (...args: unknown[]) => void, delay = 300) {
  const fnRef = useRef(fn);
  fnRef.current = fn; // always call latest fn without recreating debounced

  const debounced = useMemo(
    () => debounce((...args: unknown[]) => fnRef.current(...args), delay),
    [delay]
  );

  useEffect(() => () => debounced.cancel(), [debounced]); // cancel on unmount

  return debounced;
}

Interview answer (20 seconds): "Basic debounce has one state variable: timerId. Each call clears the previous timer and sets a new one. Leading fires on the first call when timerId is null. Trailing fires inside the setTimeout callback. cancel clears without firing — critical in cleanup to avoid updating unmounted components."

Q27b. Write a full throttle with trailing — why does basic timestamp-only throttle drop the last call? A: Timestamp-only throttle fires on the leading edge and guards subsequent calls. Problem: if the last event in a burst fires inside the wait window, it is silently dropped — the user's final action is lost (last drag coordinate, last scroll position). Full throttle schedules a trailing timer for the remaining window so the most recent call always runs.

js
function throttle(fn, wait = 200, { leading = true, trailing = true } = {}) {
  let timerId = null;
  let lastRun = 0;
  let lastArgs = null;
  let lastThis = null;

  function invoke() {
    fn.apply(lastThis, lastArgs);
    lastRun = Date.now();
    lastArgs = null;
    lastThis = null;
  }

  function throttled(...args) {
    const now = Date.now();
    const remaining = wait - (now - lastRun);

    lastArgs = args;
    lastThis = this;

    if (remaining <= 0) {
      // Enough time passed — fire immediately (leading)
      if (timerId) { clearTimeout(timerId); timerId = null; }
      invoke();
    } else if (trailing && timerId === null) {
      // Inside wait window — schedule trailing call for when it expires
      timerId = setTimeout(() => {
        invoke();
        timerId = null;
      }, remaining);
    }
    // If trailing timer already exists: lastArgs updated above,
    // timer will fire with latest args when it expires
  }

  throttled.cancel = function () {
    clearTimeout(timerId);
    timerId = null;
    lastRun = 0;
    lastArgs = null;
    lastThis = null;
  };

  return throttled;
}

How the trailing timer prevents dropped calls:

  1. Event fires inside window (remaining > 0) — schedule setTimeout(invoke, remaining).
  2. More events fire before timeout — update lastArgs but skip creating a new timer (one already running).
  3. Timer fires — calls invoke() with the most recent lastArgs. Last call is never lost.

Comparison:

Timestamp-only (basic) Timestamp + trailing timer (full)
Leading fire YES YES
Mid-burst fires YES, every N ms YES, every N ms
Last call in burst Dropped if inside window Always fires (trailing timer)
State variables lastRun only lastRun + timerId + lastArgs
Use basic when Dropped events are OK (analytics ping) Final user action must be honored

Interview answer (20 seconds): "Basic throttle tracks only lastRun — if the final event hits inside the wait window, it's silently dropped. Full throttle also keeps a trailing setTimeout for the remaining window duration. When it fires, it runs with the most recent args, so the user's last action is never lost." 📄 MDN → "setTimeout", "clearTimeout"; lodash source — debounce.js, throttle.js.

Q27c. Walk me line-by-line through your debounce/throttle — what does each line do?

Interviewers love asking "explain every line". Memorize these mappings — say them aloud while pointing at the line.

Debounce — line-by-line:

Line What it does Why it's there
let timerId = null Closure-scoped timer reference Persists across calls so each new call can cancel the previous timer
let lastArgs = null Capture most recent arguments Trailing fire uses the LAST args, not the first
return function debounced(...args) Return a wrapper, not run immediately The returned function is what the caller actually invokes repeatedly
lastArgs = args; lastThis = this Snapshot args + receiver every call Trailing-edge fire needs them later
if (leading && timerId === null) fn.apply(...) Fire immediately on first call of a burst Leading mode = instant feedback (button-click style)
clearTimeout(timerId) The core of debounce — cancel the prior pending fire Every new call resets the wait; that's the definition of debounce
timerId = setTimeout(() => {...}, delay) Schedule trailing fire after delay ms of silence If another call arrives, this timer gets cancelled in step above
fn.apply(lastThis, lastArgs) inside timeout Forward this + args to the original fn fn(...args) would lose this — see Q26c
timerId = null after fire Reset so next burst's leading mode works Without this, leading would never fire again
debounced.cancel = ... Public API to abort pending fire Needed in React useEffect cleanup on unmount → prevents stale state updates
debounced.flush = ... Public API to run pending fire NOW Useful before form submit / navigation away

Throttle (with trailing) — line-by-line:

Line What it does Why it's there
let lastRun = 0 Timestamp of last fire Used in now - lastRun >= wait check
let timerId = null Pending trailing fire Ensures the last call in a burst is honored
const now = Date.now() Read current time at every call Decide leading vs trailing path
const remaining = wait - (now - lastRun) Time left in the current throttle window If ≤ 0 → window expired, fire leading
Leading branch if (remaining <= 0) Run immediately, update lastRun = now Outside the wait window → safe to fire
clearTimeout(timerId); timerId = null in leading Cancel any pending trailing fire We just fired leading; trailing would be redundant
Trailing branch else if (!timerId) Schedule a fire at the window's end Captures the last call's args so user's final input runs
setTimeout(..., remaining) Wait only the time left, not full wait Honors the throttle interval, not extends it
fn.apply(lastThis, lastArgs) inside timeout Forward this + args Same reason as debounce
Reset lastRun = Date.now() after trailing Start a fresh window Next call's leading check works correctly

Soundbite when narrating: "clearTimeout + new setTimeout is the heart of debounce — every call cancels the previous schedule. For throttle, I compare now - lastRun against the wait — if I'm inside the window, I queue a trailing timer that captures the latest args so the final call isn't dropped. Both use fn.apply(this, args) so the wrapper preserves the caller's this context."

📄 lodash docs → _.debounce, _.throttle (read source — ~80 lines each, identical mental model).

Q28. Shallow vs deep copy? A: Shallow ({...obj}, Object.assign) copies top level; nested refs shared. Deep: structuredClone(obj) (modern), or JSON round-trip (loses functions/dates). Matters for immutable React state updates. 📄 MDN → "structuredClone", "Spread syntax".

Q28a. Immutable update patterns for React state? A: React detects state changes by reference equality. Mutating existing object/array = React sees same ref = no re-render. Always create new refs.

js
// Object — spread top level, override changed key
const updated = { ...user, age: 31 };

// Nested object — spread each level
const updated = { ...user, address: { ...user.address, city: 'Sydney' } };

// Array — ADD item
const added = [...arr, newItem];

// Array — REMOVE item (by index)
const removed = arr.filter((_, i) => i !== targetIndex);

// Array — UPDATE item (by id)
const patched = arr.map(item => item.id === id ? { ...item, name: 'New' } : item);

// Array — REPLACE item (by index)
const replaced = arr.map((item, i) => i === targetIndex ? newItem : item);

Why mutation breaks React:

js
// ❌ BAD — mutates existing array ref, React won't re-render
const items = [...state.items];
items.push(newItem);
setState({ items }); // same array ref if you do state.items.push()

// ✅ GOOD — new array ref triggers re-render
setState(prev => ({ ...prev, items: [...prev.items, newItem] }));

📄 react.dev → "Updating Objects in State"; "Updating Arrays in State".

Q29. Semantic HTML & why? A: Tags that convey meaning (<header> <nav> <main> <article> <button>) vs <div> soup. Benefits: accessibility (screen readers), SEO, maintainability. Use <button> not clickable <div>. 📄 MDN → "HTML semantics"; web.dev → semantics.

Q29a. button vs div onClick — why does it matter? A: <button> has built-in: keyboard focus (in tab order), keyboard activation (Enter/Space), disabled attribute, screen reader role = "button". <div onClick> has none of these — needs role="button", tabIndex={0}, onKeyDown manually. More code, more ways to get it wrong.

html
<!-- ✅ -->
<button type="button" onClick={handleDelete}>Delete</button>

<!-- ❌ — needs role + tabIndex + onKeyDown to match button's built-ins -->
<div role="button" tabIndex={0} onClick={handleDelete} onKeyDown={handleKey}>Delete</div>

Same logic: <a href> for navigation, NOT <div onClick> that calls router.push.

Q29b. section vs article vs div — when to use which?

Element When Test
<div> Generic container, no meaning Don't ask
<section> Group of content with a theme, usually has heading "Would a heading make sense here?"
<article> Self-contained content — standalone if extracted "Would this make sense out of page context?"
html
<!-- article: blog post — makes sense standalone -->
<article>
  <h2>React 19 Release Notes</h2>
  <p>...</p>
  <footer>By Tri · 2026-06-01</footer>  <!-- footer INSIDE article -->
</article>

<!-- section: page region with a theme -->
<section>
  <h2>Featured Tours</h2>
  <article>Tour 1</article>
  <article>Tour 2</article>
</section>

Q29c. When to use ARIA vs semantic HTML? A: Rule: semantic HTML first. ARIA is for complex widgets with no HTML equivalent (tabs, tree-view, combobox). Don't use ARIA to fix bad HTML. Over-use of ARIA is worse than none.

Only add ARIA when:

  • Widget has no semantic HTML equivalent (use role="tabpanel", aria-selected)
  • Element lacks visible text label (aria-label on icon button)
  • Dynamic state changes need announcing (aria-live, aria-expanded)
html
<!-- ✅ Semantic already handles it — no ARIA needed -->
<button>Submit</button>  <!-- role=button built-in -->
<input type="checkbox" />  <!-- role=checkbox built-in -->

<!-- ✅ ARIA adds what HTML can't -->
<button aria-label="Close dialog">✕</button>  <!-- icon-only needs label -->
<div role="status" aria-live="polite">{message}</div>  <!-- dynamic announcement -->

📄 MDN → "ARIA"; w3.org → "ARIA Authoring Practices Guide".

Q29d. Semantic HTML gotchas — main, nav, article, header/footer rules?

Small details interviewers probe to test depth on accessibility:

Element Rule Why
<main> Exactly one per page Screen reader uses it as the "skip to content" target — multiple = ambiguous
<nav> Only for primary/major navigation (main menu, breadcrumb, sidebar nav) Not every <ul> of links — assistive tech announces "navigation landmark"
<article> Content must be self-contained (still meaningful if extracted) Blog post, news item, product card, inbox email
<section> Thematic grouping that typically has a heading Tab panels, chapters, distinct page zones
<header> / <footer> Can appear multiple times — once per body/section/article Each <article> can have its own header/footer
<label> Every form <input> needs one (wrap or htmlFor) Click-target expansion + screen reader announces field purpose
html
<!-- ✅ Correct — one main, nav for primary, article self-contained -->
<header>
  <nav aria-label="Primary"><ul><li><a href="/">Home</a></li></ul></nav>
</header>
<main>
  <article>
    <header><h2>Review: Sydney trip</h2><time>2026-05-20</time></header>
    <p>Standalone content...</p>
    <footer>By Alice · 5 comments</footer>
  </article>
</main>

<!-- ❌ Wrong — nav for non-primary link list, no label on input -->
<nav><ul><li><a href="#twitter">Twitter</a></li></ul></nav>   <!-- just use <ul> -->
<input type="text" />                                           <!-- needs <label> -->

📄 MDN → "main", "nav", "article", "section"; w3.org/WAI → "ARIA Landmarks".

Q29e. Show me a copy-paste template for each semantic tag (live-coding ready).

Memorize these — they save thinking time during the live-coding round.

html
<!-- header — top of page or top of section/article -->
<header>
  <h1>AcmeBank</h1>
  <nav aria-label="Primary">
    <ul><li><a href="/accounts">Accounts</a></li><li><a href="/transfer">Transfer</a></li></ul>
  </nav>
</header>

<!-- nav — primary or significant navigation only (NOT every <ul> of links) -->
<nav aria-label="Breadcrumb"><ol><li><a href="/">Home</a></li><li>Accounts</li></ol></nav>

<!-- main — exactly ONE per page -->
<main>
  <h1>Your accounts</h1>
  <section>...</section>
</main>

<!-- section — thematic group, should have a heading -->
<section aria-labelledby="recent-h">
  <h2 id="recent-h">Recent transactions</h2>
  <ul><li>...</li></ul>
</section>

<!-- article — self-contained content (still meaningful if extracted) -->
<article>
  <header><h2>Savings tips</h2><time datetime="2026-05-20">May 20, 2026</time></header>
  <p>...</p>
  <footer>Author: Editorial Team</footer>
</article>

<!-- aside — tangentially related (sidebar, callout, related links) -->
<aside aria-labelledby="related-h">
  <h2 id="related-h">Related products</h2>
  <ul><li><a href="/loans">Home loan</a></li></ul>
</aside>

<!-- footer — page or section/article foot -->
<footer>
  <p>&copy; 2026 AcmeBank</p>
  <nav aria-label="Legal"><a href="/privacy">Privacy</a><a href="/terms">Terms</a></nav>
</footer>

<!-- button — every action. type="button" prevents accidental form submit -->
<button type="button" onClick={handleTransfer}>Transfer now</button>
<button type="submit">Submit form</button>
<!-- ❌ never: <div onClick={...}>...</div> -->

<!-- form — collects + submits data; gets free validation + submit semantics -->
<form onSubmit={handleSearch}>
  <label htmlFor="amount">Amount (AUD)</label>
  <input id="amount" name="amount" type="number" min="0" required />
  <button type="submit">Search</button>
</form>

<!-- label — two valid patterns; every input needs one -->
<label htmlFor="email">Email</label>
<input id="email" type="email" />
<!-- or wrap -->
<label>Phone <input type="tel" /></label>

📄 MDN → "HTML elements reference"; html.spec.whatwg.org → "Sections".

Q30. CSS box model + box-sizing? A: content + padding + border + margin. box-sizing: border-box makes width include padding+border (predictable layouts) vs default content-box. 📄 MDN → "Box model", "box-sizing".

Q31. Flexbox vs Grid? A: Flexbox = 1D layout (row OR column), content-driven. Grid = 2D (rows AND columns), layout-driven. Use Grid for page/section structure, Flex for component alignment. 📄 MDN → "CSS Flexbox", "CSS Grid"; css-tricks guides.

Q31g. Responsive design — what do you consider when building a responsive site? (oral checklist)

Open-ended question. Run through this 9-item list aloud to show breadth.

# Concern What to check
1 Layout fluidity Grids/flex use fr/%/minmax not fixed px; columns reflow at breakpoints
2 Typography scaling clamp(1rem, 2.5vw, 1.25rem) so font scales smoothly; never pixel-locked
3 Spacing scales Use design tokens / CSS vars; gap over manual margins; relative units
4 Images responsive + no CLS next/image (or <img srcset sizes>) with explicit width/height or aspect-ratio
5 Tap targets ≥ 44×44 px on mobile Apple HIG / WCAG 2.5.5 — small buttons fail touch usability
6 Mobile nav usable by hand + keyboard Hamburger reachable with thumb; trapped focus when open; Escape closes
7 No horizontal overflow Audit with * { outline: 1px solid red }; min-width: 0 on flex items; overflow-wrap: anywhere for long URLs
8 Performance on slow networks Lighthouse mobile profile; lazy-load below-fold images; loading="lazy"; route-based code splitting
9 Contrast / focus / zoom 200% WCAG AA 4.5:1; :focus-visible always shown; layout survives Ctrl++ to 200%

Interview soundbite: "Responsive isn't just media queries — it's layout fluidity, typography scaling, image stability (no CLS), tap targets, mobile nav, no overflow, perf on 3G, and accessibility at 200% zoom. I default to mobile-first, design tokens for spacing, next/image for images, and container queries for components that appear in multiple contexts."

📄 web.dev → "Responsive design basics"; WCAG 2.1 SC 2.5.5 (target size), SC 1.4.10 (reflow).

Q31a. CSS Grid container properties — full reference?

Property Purpose Example
grid-template-columns Define column tracks 1fr 1fr 1fr, repeat(4, 1fr), 250px 1fr 200px
grid-template-rows Define row tracks 80px 1fr 60px, auto auto
grid-template-areas Name layout zones with strings See dashboard example below
gap Space between cells 16px (uniform), 24px 16px (row col)
justify-items Horizontal align items in cell start, center, stretch
align-items Vertical align items in cell start, center, stretch

repeat() and minmax():

css
.grid { grid-template-columns: repeat(12, 1fr); }                       /* 12-column system */
.grid { grid-template-columns: repeat(auto-fit, minmax(220px, 1fr)); }  /* responsive auto */
.grid { grid-template-rows: minmax(100px, auto); }                      /* min 100px, max content */

Q31b. CSS Grid item properties — grid-column, grid-row, grid-area, span?

Property Applies to Purpose
grid-column Item Span across columns
grid-row Item Span across rows
grid-area Item Assign to named template zone or explicit lines
justify-self Item Horizontal align single item
align-self Item Vertical align single item
css
/* grid-column */
.item { grid-column: 1 / 3; }    /* col-line 1 to 3 — occupies 2 columns */
.item { grid-column: span 2; }   /* span 2 from current position */
.item { grid-column: 1 / -1; }   /* full row width */

/* grid-row */
.item { grid-row: 1 / 3; }       /* row-line 1 to 3 */
.item { grid-row: span 2; }      /* span 2 rows */

/* grid-area: row-start / col-start / row-end / col-end */
.item { grid-area: 1 / 2 / 3 / 4; }

Q31c. Dashboard layout using grid-template-areas?

css
.dashboard {
  display: grid;
  grid-template-columns: 240px 1fr 300px;
  grid-template-rows: 64px 1fr;
  grid-template-areas:
    "nav    header  header"
    "nav    content aside";
  gap: 0;
  height: 100vh;
}
.nav     { grid-area: nav; }
.header  { grid-area: header; }
.content { grid-area: content; }
.aside   { grid-area: aside; }

/* Mobile: collapse to single column */
@media (max-width: 768px) {
  .dashboard {
    grid-template-columns: 1fr;
    grid-template-rows: 64px auto 1fr auto;
    grid-template-areas:
      "header"
      "nav"
      "content"
      "aside";
  }
}

Responsive card grid with featured item:

css
.card-grid {
  display: grid;
  grid-template-columns: repeat(auto-fit, minmax(280px, 1fr));
  gap: 24px;
}
.card-grid .featured { grid-column: span 2; }
@media (max-width: 600px) {
  .card-grid .featured { grid-column: span 1; }
}

Q31d. auto-fit vs auto-fill — visual difference? Both allow a responsive grid without media queries. The difference appears when there are fewer items than available column slots.

css
.grid-fit  { grid-template-columns: repeat(auto-fit,  minmax(200px, 1fr)); }
.grid-fill { grid-template-columns: repeat(auto-fill, minmax(200px, 1fr)); }
Scenario auto-fit auto-fill
2 items in a 900px container (4 cols fit) Items stretch → each 450px Items stay ~225px; 2 ghost columns hold space
When to use Card grid where items should fill all space Fixed column size must be preserved

Interview one-liner: "auto-fill creates phantom columns; auto-fit collapses them so real items expand to fill."

Q31e. Container query units (cqi, cqb, cqw) and responsive units (clamp, aspect-ratio)?

Container query units — relative to the nearest named container:

Unit Description
cqi 1% of container inline size (width in horizontal writing)
cqb 1% of container block size (height in horizontal writing)
cqw 1% of container width
cqh 1% of container height
cqmin Smaller of cqi / cqb — avoids overflow
cqmax Larger of cqi / cqb
css
.card-wrapper {
  container-type: inline-size;
  container-name: card;
}

.card-title {
  font-size: clamp(1rem, 4cqi, 2rem); /* scales with container width, clamped */
}
.card-image {
  height: 50cqi; /* proportional to container, not viewport */
}

@container card (min-width: 400px) {
  .tour-card { flex-direction: row; align-items: center; }
  .tour-card img { width: 200px; aspect-ratio: 1; }
}
@container card (min-width: 600px) {
  .tour-card .extra-info { display: block; }
  .tour-card .price { font-size: 1.5rem; }
}

clamp(MIN, PREFERRED, MAX) — fluid value between a floor and ceiling:

css
font-size: clamp(1rem, 2.5vw, 2rem);   /* fluid viewport-relative */
font-size: clamp(1rem, 4cqi, 2rem);    /* fluid container-relative */
gap: clamp(16px, 3vw, 32px);

aspect-ratio — maintain proportional dimensions:

css
.video-embed { width: 100%; aspect-ratio: 16 / 9; }
.avatar      { width: 48px; aspect-ratio: 1; border-radius: 50%; }
.card-image  { width: 100%; aspect-ratio: 4 / 3; object-fit: cover; }

Media query vs container query recap:

Criterion Media Query Container Query
Based on Viewport size Parent container size
Scope Page-level Component-level
Reusability Component must know page context Component self-adapts anywhere
Use for Page layout, nav, global spacing Card, widget, panel, reusable UI
Browser support All browsers Chrome 105+, Safari 16+, Firefox 110+

📄 MDN → "CSS Grid Layout"; "container queries"; "clamp()"; "aspect-ratio".

Q32. CSS specificity + position? A: Specificity: inline > id > class/attr/pseudo-class > element. !important overrides (avoid). Position: static, relative, absolute (vs positioned ancestor), fixed (viewport), sticky (scroll threshold). 📄 MDN → "Specificity", "position".

Q32a. CSS position types — detailed?

Value Reference frame In flow? Use case
static Normal flow (default) ✅ Default
relative Own original position ✅ Nudge element, make containing block for absolute children
absolute Nearest ancestor with non-static position ❌ Tooltip, badge, dropdown, overlay
fixed Viewport ❌ Sticky header, back-to-top button, modal backdrop
sticky Scroll container, sticks at threshold ✅ (until sticky) Table header, sidebar that sticks while scrolling
css
/* Badge at card corner — classic absolute inside relative */
.card { position: relative; }
.card .badge { position: absolute; top: 8px; right: 8px; }

/* Sticky table header */
thead th { position: sticky; top: 0; z-index: 1; }

Q32b. z-index and stacking context — why "z-index: 9999 doesn't work"? A: z-index only compares within the same stacking context. A new stacking context is created by: position + z-index, opacity < 1, transform, filter, isolation: isolate. Child elements can never exceed their parent's stacking context, no matter how high their z-index.

css
/* Classic trap: modal inside a transformed parent */
.card { transform: scale(1); } /* Creates stacking context! */
.card .dropdown { z-index: 9999; } /* Still trapped inside .card's context */

/* Fix: use isolation */
.card { isolation: isolate; } /* Explicit stacking context, no transform side-effect */

/* Or move modal to document root via createPortal in React */

Interview answer: "If z-index doesn't work, check the parent for transform/opacity/filter — those create a stacking context that traps children."

Q32c. display: none vs visibility: hidden vs .sr-only?

display: none visibility: hidden .sr-only
In layout? ❌ No — removed from flow ✅ Yes — holds space ✅ Yes (1px absolute)
Screen reader ❌ Not read ❌ Not read ✅ Read
Animation ❌ Can't transition ✅ Can transition N/A
Use for Toggle content on/off Keep space, hide visually Labels for icon-only elements
css
/* Screen-reader only (WCAG technique) */
.sr-only {
  position: absolute; width: 1px; height: 1px;
  padding: 0; margin: -1px; overflow: hidden;
  clip: rect(0,0,0,0); border: 0;
}
/* Use: <span class="sr-only">Close dialog</span> next to ✕ icon button */

Q32d. rem vs em — when to use which?

rem em
Reference Root html font-size (usually 16px) Current element's own font-size
Predictable? ✅ Always relative to one root ❌ Cascades — nesting multiplies
Use for Font sizes, spacing, layout Padding/margin proportional to element's font-size (button padding scales with its font)
css
/* rem — predictable global scale */
h1 { font-size: 2rem; margin-bottom: 1rem; } /* 32px, 16px */

/* em — scales with own font-size: button padding adapts to size variant */
.btn { font-size: 1rem; padding: 0.5em 1em; }    /* 8px 16px */
.btn.lg { font-size: 1.25rem; padding: 0.5em 1em; } /* 10px 20px — auto scales! */

Q32e. Container queries vs media queries? A: Media query = responsive to viewport size (page-level layout changes). Container query = responsive to parent container size (component-level — component adapts wherever it's placed).

css
/* Container query — card adapts regardless of where it's placed */
.card-wrapper { container-type: inline-size; container-name: card; }

/* Card layout changes based on its container width, not the viewport */
@container card (min-width: 400px) {
  .tour-card { flex-direction: row; } /* horizontal if wide container */
}
/* Same card in sidebar (narrow) = vertical; in main (wide) = horizontal */
/* No need to know where it's placed! */

Use container queries for: card, widget, panel, reusable components. Media queries for: page layout, navigation toggling, global typography.

Q32f. overflow: hidden side effects? A: Beyond clipping overflow, it also: (1) creates a new stacking context (affects z-index), (2) creates a Block Formatting Context (BFC — floats contained), (3) kills position: sticky inside it.

css
/* Trap: dropdown clipped by overflow:hidden on ancestor */
.card { overflow: hidden; border-radius: 12px; } /* clips dropdown! */
.card .dropdown { position: absolute; top: 100%; } /* invisible — clipped */

/* Fix: use overflow: clip for visual clipping without BFC/stacking effects */
.card { overflow: clip; border-radius: 12px; } /* clip doesn't trap dropdown */

Q32g. min-width: 0 flex gotcha? A: Flex items have min-width: auto by default — they won't shrink below content size. Long text/images overflow the flex container. Fix: min-width: 0 allows the item to shrink, enabling text-overflow: ellipsis.

css
.flex-row { display: flex; gap: 16px; }
.flex-row .content { min-width: 0; } /* allow shrink */
.flex-row .content h3 { white-space: nowrap; overflow: hidden; text-overflow: ellipsis; }

📄 MDN → "position", "z-index", "CSS Containment".

Q33. CORS — what is it? A: Browser security: cross-origin requests blocked unless server sends Access-Control-Allow-Origin headers. Preflight OPTIONS for non-simple requests. It's a browser enforcement, not server security. 📄 MDN → "CORS".

Q34. REST principles + idempotency? A: Resources via URLs, HTTP verbs (GET read, POST create, PUT replace, PATCH partial, DELETE remove), statelessness, status codes (2xx/4xx/5xx). Idempotent: GET/PUT/DELETE (same result on retry); POST is not. Critical for banking retries. 📄 MDN → "HTTP methods"; restfulapi.net.

JD lists GraphQL as desirable — see Q-JD-2 (§1.5.3) for REST vs GraphQL comparison + Q-JD-3 for the N+1 / DataLoader trap.

Q35. JWT auth flow? A: Server issues signed token on login; client sends in Authorization: Bearer. Stateless verification via signature. Store carefully (httpOnly cookie > localStorage for XSS). Short-lived access + refresh token. 📄 jwt.io intro; OWASP JWT cheat sheet.

Deep-dive cross-ref: For a stronger interview answer on JWT storage/sending/refresh, authn/authz methods, Server Actions vs fetch, and architecture trade-offs, see Q20ai–Q20an in Part A.

#5. Part C — Testing & Accessibility Q&A

JD-gap cross-refs: see also Q-JD-6 (Jest vs Mocha+Chai vs Vitest), Q-JD-7 (Cypress vs Playwright vs Selenium E2E), Q-JD-8 (mocks/spies/stubs glossary), Q-JD-9 (snapshot-testing traps), Q-JD-1 (WCAG 2.0 vs 2.1 nuance) — all in §1.5.3.

Q36. Testing philosophy of React Testing Library? A: "Test behavior, not implementation." Query by accessible role/text/label (what users see), not internal state/classes. Gives confidence + resists refactors. Avoid enzyme-style shallow render. 📄 testing-library.com → "Guiding Principles"; Kent C. Dodds "Testing Implementation Details".

Q37. Query priority in RTL? A: getByRole > getByLabelText > getByPlaceholderText > getByText > getByTestId (last resort). Role-based mirrors accessibility.

Priority Query When to use
1 (highest) getByRole Button, heading, dialog, link — any element with an ARIA role
2 getByLabelText Form inputs associated with a <label>
3 getByPlaceholderText Input without label (rare; prefer label)
4 getByText Non-interactive text content
5 (last resort) getByTestId When no semantic query is possible

Interview tip: "Test by user behavior — use getByRole first because it mirrors how assistive technology sees the page. Combine with userEvent for realistic interaction simulation. Avoid testing implementation details like class names or internal state."

📄 testing-library.com → "About Queries / Priority".

Q38. getBy vs queryBy vs findBy? A: getBy throws if not found (assert presence). queryBy returns null (assert absence). findBy returns Promise (async, retries — for awaited UI). 📄 testing-library.com → "Queries".

Q39. How to test async + user events? A: userEvent (not fireEvent) for realistic interaction; await screen.findBy... or waitFor for async UI; mock network with MSW (Mock Service Worker) over jest mocks. 📄 testing-library.com → "user-event"; mswjs.io.

Q40. Test pyramid? A: Many unit tests (fast, isolated), fewer integration (components together), few E2E (Playwright/Cypress, slow but real). Balance speed vs confidence. 📄 Martin Fowler "TestPyramid"; testing-library docs.

See Q-JD-7 (§1.5.3) for the deep Cypress vs Playwright vs Selenium comparison + Playwright sample test.

Q41. WCAG 2.0/2.1 — the 4 principles (POUR)? A: Perceivable, Operable, Understandable, Robust. Level AA is the standard target (banking/gov often legally required). Regulated-sector JDs explicitly want AA. 📄 w3.org/WAI → "WCAG at a Glance", "POUR".

Enterprise JDs often say WCAG 2.0 AA specifically — see Q-JD-1 (§1.5.2) for the 2.0 vs 2.1 nuance and the soundbite to use.

Q42. Concrete AA requirements you'd implement? A: Color contrast ≥ 4.5:1 (text), keyboard navigable (no mouse traps, visible focus), all images alt, form inputs labelled, ARIA only when semantic HTML insufficient, focus management on route/modal change, aria-live for dynamic updates. 📄 w3.org/WAI → "WCAG 2 quickref"; MDN → ARIA; a11yproject.com checklist.

Q43. How to audit accessibility? A: Automated: axe DevTools, Lighthouse, eslint-plugin-jsx-a11y. Manual: keyboard-only nav, screen reader (NVDA/VoiceOver), zoom 200%. Automated catches ~30-40% — manual essential. 📄 deque axe docs; web.dev → "Accessibility".

#6. Part D — Java / Spring Boot Q&A (willingness track)

⚠️ Honesty strategy: "I've used Java mainly with AI assistance; I understand the fundamentals and I'm genuinely keen to grow into backend." Then prove you grasp the concepts below. Don't oversell. Format below: Q → A (concise) → Why it matters (rationale) → 📄 Evidence.

#D1. Core Java & OOP

Q44. Four pillars of OOP — and how Java implements each?

Pillar Java mechanism
Encapsulation private fields + public getters/setters
Inheritance class B extends A (single class inheritance), implements for interfaces
Polymorphism Method override (runtime dispatch) + method overload (compile-time dispatch)
Abstraction abstract class + interface — hide implementation behind contracts

Why: every interviewer asks; lays the foundation for Spring DI, JPA entities, etc. Composition > inheritance is the modern preference (favor has-a over is-a). 📄 docs.oracle.com → "Java Tutorials / OOP Concepts".

Q44a. Abstract class vs interface — when to use which?

abstract class interface
Inheritance Single (one parent class) Multiple (implements A, B, C)
State Can hold instance fields Only public static final constants
Constructor Yes No
Methods Concrete + abstract Abstract + default + static (Java 8+) + private (Java 9+)
Use for "is-a" with shared base state/logic "can-do" capability contract

Why: Spring's JpaRepository, Java's Comparator are interfaces; HttpServlet is abstract class. 📄 docs.oracle.com → "Abstract Methods and Classes".

Q44b. Method overloading vs overriding — examples?

java
class Calculator {
  int add(int a, int b)        { return a + b; }       // overload — same name, different params
  double add(double a, double b){ return a + b; }      // resolved at COMPILE time
}
class Animal { void speak() { System.out.println("..."); } }
class Dog extends Animal {
  @Override void speak() { System.out.println("Woof"); } // override — runtime dispatch (polymorphism)
}

Rule: overload = compile-time, overriding = runtime. @Override annotation catches typos. 📄 Oracle Tutorials → "Overriding and Hiding".

Q44c. Polymorphism — how Java implements it? A: Two flavors:

  • Compile-time (static): method overloading, generics
  • Runtime (dynamic): method overriding via virtual dispatch — JVM uses vtable lookup based on actual object type
java
Animal a = new Dog();
a.speak();   // "Woof" — dispatched via Dog's vtable, even though a's static type is Animal

Why: foundation for Spring's bean injection (@Autowired Service resolves to actual implementation). 📄 Oracle JVM Spec → "Method Resolution".

Q45. == vs .equals() in Java? A: == = reference equality (or primitive value). .equals() = logical equality (must override + hashCode() together — contract: equal objects must have equal hash codes).

java
String a = "hello", b = "hello";   // string pool → same reference
String c = new String("hello");
a == b;          // true (interned)
a == c;          // false (different ref)
a.equals(c);     // true (logical)

Why: violating the equals/hashCode contract breaks HashSet, HashMap lookup. Records (Java 16+) auto-implement both. 📄 Oracle → Object.equals(), Object.hashCode() Javadoc.

Q45a. final vs finally vs finalize?

What
final Variable (can't reassign), method (can't override), class (can't extend)
finally try/finally block — runs whether or not an exception is thrown
finalize() Object method called by GC before reclaim (DEPRECATED in Java 9+, removed in 18; use Cleaner / try-with-resources instead)

📄 Oracle Tutorials → "final Keyword"; Java 9+ Cleaner API.

Q45b. this vs super?

  • this = current instance reference; this(...) = call another constructor in same class
  • super = parent class reference; super(...) = call parent constructor (must be first line); super.method() = call overridden parent method

Q45c. Marker interface — what is it, can you create one? A: An interface with no methods. Used as metadata/tag. Examples: Serializable, Cloneable, Remote. Modern alternative: annotations (@FunctionalInterface).

java
public interface AuditLoggable {} // marker
class TransferRequest implements AuditLoggable {}
// Reflection check: if (obj instanceof AuditLoggable) auditLogger.log(obj);

📄 Oracle → "Marker Interfaces"; Effective Java item 41.

Q45d. Default and static methods in Java 8+ interfaces?

java
interface Vehicle {
  void drive();                           // abstract
  default void honk() { System.out.println("Beep"); }  // default — added without breaking impls
  static Vehicle bike() { return new Bike(); }          // static utility
}

Why: enabled Stream/Collection to add forEach(), stream() without breaking 1000s of existing impls. 📄 Oracle → "Default Methods".

#D2. JVM, Memory & Garbage Collection

Q46. JVM role — what does it do? A: Java Virtual Machine — runs .class bytecode portably. Components: ClassLoader (loads classes lazily), Runtime Data Areas (heap, stack, metaspace), Execution Engine (interpreter + JIT), GC. Why: write-once-run-anywhere. JIT optimizes hot code at runtime, so long-running services often outperform AOT-compiled languages. 📄 docs.oracle.com → "Java Virtual Machine Specification".

Q46a. JVM memory areas — Heap vs Stack vs Metaspace?

Area What Per
Heap All objects, arrays Shared (whole JVM)
Stack Method frames, local variables, primitives, refs Per-thread
Metaspace (Java 8+, replaced PermGen) Class metadata, method bytecode Shared, native memory
PC Register Current instruction pointer Per-thread
Native method stack JNI native calls Per-thread

Common errors: OutOfMemoryError: Java heap space (heap), StackOverflowError (stack — infinite recursion), OutOfMemoryError: Metaspace (loading too many classes — classloader leak). 📄 Oracle → "JVM Internals".

Q46b. Garbage collection — how does it work? GC algorithms? A: GC frees memory by reclaiming unreachable objects. Generational hypothesis: most objects die young → split heap into Young (Eden + 2 Survivor) and Old. Minor GC sweeps Young; Major GC sweeps Old.

Collector Use case
Serial Single thread; small apps, embedded
Parallel (Throughput) Multi-thread Young/Old; batch jobs
CMS (deprecated) Concurrent Mark-Sweep; replaced by G1
G1GC (default since Java 9) Region-based, predictable pause time. Banking default.
ZGC (Java 11+) Sub-millisecond pause, scales to TB heaps
Shenandoah (Red Hat) Concurrent compaction, low pause

Tune via -XX:+UseG1GC, -Xms2g -Xmx2g, -XX:MaxGCPauseMillis=200. 📄 docs.oracle.com → "HotSpot GC Tuning Guide".

Q46c. Class loading — how does it work? A: Three phases — Loading (read .class bytes), Linking (verify + prepare + resolve), Initialization (run static initializers). Delegation hierarchy: Bootstrap → Platform → Application → Custom classloaders. Each parent first. Why: classloader-leak bugs (e.g., undeployed webapp keeping old classes) cause OutOfMemoryError: Metaspace. 📄 Oracle JVM Spec → "Loading, Linking, Initializing".

Q46d. JIT vs AOT compilation? A: JIT (Just-In-Time) compiles bytecode to native code at runtime when "hot" (HotSpot's C1/C2 compilers). AOT (Ahead-Of-Time, GraalVM Native Image) compiles to native at build time — faster startup, smaller memory, but no runtime profiling-based optimization. Banking note: Spring Boot 3 + GraalVM AOT enables sub-100ms startup for serverless lambdas. 📄 GraalVM docs; openjdk.org → JEP 295 (AOT).

Q46e. How do you optimize Java memory usage?

  • Profile with jvisualvm, JFR (Java Flight Recorder), async-profiler
  • Right-size heap (-Xms == -Xmx to avoid resizing)
  • Choose right GC (G1 default; ZGC for low latency)
  • Reuse objects (StringBuilder, object pools — only after profiling)
  • Avoid memory leaks: unbounded caches, static collections, classloader leaks, listeners not unregistered 📄 Oracle → "Java Performance Tuning"; Plumbr OOM handbook.

#D3. Java Collections Framework

Q47. List vs Set vs Map?

Ordered? Duplicates? Implementations
List Yes Yes ArrayList, LinkedList, Vector
Set No (HashSet) / Yes (LinkedHashSet) / Sorted (TreeSet) No HashSet, LinkedHashSet, TreeSet
Map No / Insertion (LinkedHashMap) / Sorted (TreeMap) No dup keys HashMap, LinkedHashMap, TreeMap, ConcurrentHashMap

📄 Oracle → "Collections Framework".

Q47a. ArrayList vs LinkedList internals?

Op ArrayList LinkedList
Storage Resizable array (doubling on grow) Doubly-linked list of nodes
get(i) O(1) O(n)
add(end) Amortized O(1) O(1)
add(i) middle O(n) — shift O(n) — traverse + relink (O(1) once at the node)
Memory Compact More overhead (next/prev pointers per node)

Rule: default to ArrayList unless you need fast head/middle inserts via Iterator. 📄 Oracle Javadoc → ArrayList, LinkedList.

Q47b. HashSet vs TreeSet vs LinkedHashSet?

Order Implementation Time
HashSet Unordered HashMap-backed O(1) avg
LinkedHashSet Insertion order HashMap + linked list O(1) avg
TreeSet Sorted (natural / Comparator) Red-Black tree O(log n)

Q47c. How does HashMap work internally (Java 8+)? A: Array of buckets (default capacity 16, load factor 0.75). hash(key) → bucket index. Collisions: chained as a linked list, converted to a Red-Black tree when bucket > 8 entries (treeification — Java 8 fix for hash flooding attacks). Resize doubles capacity when size > capacity * loadFactor. Why: O(1) average, O(log n) worst case (post-treeification). Pre-Java 8 it was O(n) worst → DoS vulnerability. 📄 Java 8 HashMap source (HashMap.java); JEP 180.

Q47d. WeakHashMap and weak references? A: WeakHashMap keys are wrapped in WeakReference — when no strong references exist, GC reclaims the key and entry. Use for caches keyed by objects that should be evictable when not used elsewhere (e.g., classloader → metadata maps). 📄 Oracle Javadoc → WeakHashMap, WeakReference.

Q47e. ConcurrentHashMap vs HashMap vs Collections.synchronizedMap?

Thread-safe? Lock granularity
HashMap ❌ none
Collections.synchronizedMap(map) ✅ Whole map (one lock)
ConcurrentHashMap ✅ Bucket-level (Java 8: CAS on first node, synchronized on bin head) — high concurrency

Rule: in multi-threaded code, always use ConcurrentHashMap. 📄 Oracle Javadoc → ConcurrentHashMap.

Q47f. synchronizedList vs CopyOnWriteArrayList?

  • synchronizedList: every op synchronizes on the list — reads block writes
  • CopyOnWriteArrayList: write creates a new array copy; reads are lock-free → great for read-heavy, write-rare (event listeners)

Q47g. PriorityQueue — how does it work? A: Min-heap by default (smallest first), backed by an array. O(log n) offer/poll. Supply a Comparator for max-heap or custom order:

java
PriorityQueue<Task> q = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.comparingInt(Task::priority).reversed());

Use case: Dijkstra, top-K, task schedulers.

Q47h. Comparable vs Comparator?

  • Comparable<T>: object's natural ordering; one compareTo method (e.g., String, Integer)
  • Comparator<T>: external strategy; pass to Collections.sort(list, comparator) or stream.sorted(comparator). Modern: Comparator.comparing(User::age).thenComparing(User::name).

#D4. Multithreading & Concurrency

Q48. Process vs thread — and how does Java do multithreading? A: Process = independent address space + resources. Thread = shared memory unit of execution within a process. Java threads map to OS threads (1:1 since green threads were dropped). Create via Thread, Runnable, Callable, ExecutorService, or virtual threads (Java 21+). 📄 docs.oracle.com → "Concurrency Tutorial".

Q48a. Runnable vs Callable?

Returns Throws checked exception
Runnable.run() void No
Callable<V>.call() V Yes

Callable returns a Future<V> when submitted to an ExecutorService. Modern: prefer CompletableFuture.

Q48b. synchronized keyword — how does it work? A: Acquires the monitor (intrinsic lock) on an object before entering the block, releases on exit. Two forms:

java
synchronized void m() {...}                   // locks `this`
static synchronized void m() {...}            // locks the Class object
synchronized (lockObject) {...}               // locks specified object

Provides mutual exclusion + happens-before memory visibility. Reentrant: same thread can re-acquire. Cost: contention, no fairness. Modern: prefer ReentrantLock for advanced needs. 📄 Oracle → "Synchronized Methods", JLS §17.

Q48c. Thread pool / ExecutorService?

java
ExecutorService pool = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(8);
Future<Integer> f = pool.submit(() -> heavyCompute());
Integer result = f.get(5, TimeUnit.SECONDS);
pool.shutdown();

Common pools: newFixedThreadPool(n), newCachedThreadPool(), newScheduledThreadPool(n). Avoid Executors.newCachedThreadPool() in production (unbounded — OOM risk); use ThreadPoolExecutor directly with bounded queue. 📄 Oracle → "Executor Framework".

Q48d. wait() / notify() / notifyAll()?

  • Used for inter-thread coordination on a monitor
  • Must be called inside synchronized block on the same lock object
  • wait() releases the lock and parks; notify() wakes one waiter; notifyAll() wakes all
  • Modern alternative: Lock + Condition, BlockingQueue, Semaphore, CountDownLatch 📄 Oracle → "Guarded Blocks".

Q48e. Deadlock vs race condition vs livelock?

What Fix
Deadlock Two threads each hold a lock the other needs Lock ordering, tryLock with timeout
Race condition Result depends on thread interleaving synchronized, volatile, atomics
Livelock Threads keep responding to each other but make no progress Backoff, randomization
Starvation Thread never gets CPU Fair locks, priority tuning

Q48f. volatile vs synchronized?

  • volatile: cheap memory-visibility guarantee — read/write goes to main memory; does not provide atomicity for compound ops (i++ is read-modify-write)
  • synchronized: visibility + mutual exclusion + atomicity; more expensive
  • For atomic counters: use AtomicInteger (lock-free CAS) 📄 JLS §17.4 (Memory Model); JCiP book.

Q48g. ForkJoinPool? A: Pool tuned for divide-and-conquer recursive tasks (RecursiveTask/RecursiveAction) using work-stealing — idle threads steal tasks from busy threads' deques. Backs parallelStream(), CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(). 📄 Oracle → "Fork/Join".

Q48h. ReentrantLock vs synchronized?

synchronized ReentrantLock
Acquire Implicit Explicit (lock() / unlock() in finally)
Try with timeout ❌ tryLock(time, unit)
Interruptible ❌ lockInterruptibly()
Fair lock ❌ new ReentrantLock(true)
Condition objects One via wait/notify Multiple via newCondition()

Q48i. Atomic variables — AtomicInteger etc? A: Lock-free thread-safe primitives using CAS (Compare-And-Swap) CPU instruction. Methods: incrementAndGet(), compareAndSet(expect, update), addAndGet(n). Backbone of ConcurrentHashMap, LongAdder.

java
AtomicInteger counter = new AtomicInteger();
counter.incrementAndGet(); // atomic, lock-free

For high-contention, use LongAdder (Java 8+) which scales better than AtomicLong.

Q48j. CompletableFuture — async programming?

java
CompletableFuture
  .supplyAsync(() -> fetchAccount(id))                      // run async
  .thenApplyAsync(account -> account.balance)               // transform
  .thenCombine(supplyAsync(() -> fetchRate()),              // combine 2 futures
               (balance, rate) -> balance * rate)
  .exceptionally(ex -> 0.0)                                  // error fallback
  .thenAccept(System.out::println);

Why: composable async pipelines, like JS Promises. Replaces callback hell. 📄 Oracle Javadoc → CompletableFuture.

#D5. Java 8+ Functional Features

Q49. Lambda expressions — examples?

java
Runnable r = () -> System.out.println("hi");
Function<Integer, Integer> sq = n -> n * n;
list.forEach(item -> System.out.println(item));
list.sort((a, b) -> a.compareTo(b));    // or Comparator.naturalOrder()

A lambda is shorthand for a @FunctionalInterface (single-abstract-method interface). 📄 Oracle → "Lambda Expressions".

Q49a. Functional interfaces — Function, Predicate, Consumer, Supplier?

Interface Signature Use
Function<T,R> R apply(T) Transform
Predicate<T> boolean test(T) Filter
Consumer<T> void accept(T) Side effect
Supplier<T> T get() Lazy producer
BiFunction<T,U,R> R apply(T,U) Two-arg transform
UnaryOperator<T> T apply(T) Same-type transform

Q49b. Stream vs parallel stream?

java
list.stream().filter(x -> x > 0).map(Object::toString).collect(toList());
list.parallelStream().reduce(0, Integer::sum); // ForkJoinPool.commonPool

Parallel: only worth it for large datasets + CPU-heavy ops + stateless pipelines. Beware shared state, ordering, IO inside lambdas. 📄 Oracle → "Stream Package"; Doug Lea "When to use parallel streams".

Q49c. findFirst vs findAny?

  • findFirst(): deterministic — first encounter order
  • findAny(): parallel-friendly — any element, no order guarantee → faster on parallel streams

Q49d. map vs flatMap?

  • map(T → R): 1-to-1 transformation
  • flatMap(T → Stream<R>): 1-to-many, flattens nested streams
java
List<List<Integer>> nested = List.of(List.of(1,2), List.of(3,4));
nested.stream().flatMap(List::stream).collect(toList()); // [1,2,3,4]

Q49e. Optional — purpose and usage? A: Container for value-or-empty; explicit alternative to nullable returns.

java
Optional<User> findUser(String id) { ... }
findUser(id).map(User::name).orElse("Unknown");
findUser(id).ifPresent(u -> log(u));
findUser(id).orElseThrow(() -> new NotFoundException(id));

Rules: only as return type (not field/parameter); never .get() without prior check; do not create Optional.of(null) (use ofNullable). 📄 Oracle Javadoc → Optional; Stuart Marks "Optional Best Practices".

Q49f. Collectors — what does the class offer?

java
.collect(toList())              // List<T>
.collect(toSet())               // Set<T>
.collect(toMap(User::id, u->u)) // Map<K,V>
.collect(groupingBy(User::role))               // Map<Role, List<User>>
.collect(partitioningBy(u -> u.age >= 18))     // Map<Boolean, List<User>>
.collect(counting())                            // Long count
.collect(joining(", ", "[", "]"))               // String concat

📄 Oracle Javadoc → Collectors.

#D6. Exception Handling

Q50. Checked vs unchecked exceptions?

  • Checked (extends Exception not RuntimeException): compiler enforces try/catch or throws in signature. Examples: IOException, SQLException
  • Unchecked (extends RuntimeException): optional. Examples: NullPointerException, IllegalArgumentException
  • Modern guidance: prefer unchecked for programming errors; checked for recoverable conditions (use sparingly)

Q50a. Exception propagation? A: An uncaught exception bubbles up the call stack until caught, or terminates the thread. finally always runs. try-with-resources auto-closes AutoCloseable resources.

Q50b. Custom exceptions?

java
public class InsufficientFundsException extends RuntimeException {
  private final String accountId;
  public InsufficientFundsException(String accountId, String msg) {
    super(msg); this.accountId = accountId;
  }
  public String getAccountId() { return accountId; }
}

Q50c. try-with-resources?

java
try (var reader = Files.newBufferedReader(path);
     var writer = Files.newBufferedWriter(out)) {
  reader.lines().forEach(writer::println);
}  // Both auto-closed in reverse order; suppressed exceptions captured

Why: replaces the verbose pre-Java 7 try/finally pattern, never leaks file handles.

Q50d. Suppressed exceptions? A: When try-with-resources resource's close() throws while another exception is propagating, the close exception is added as "suppressed" via Throwable.addSuppressed(). Retrieve via getSuppressed(). Prevents debugging info loss.

#D7. File I/O & Serialization

Q51. Reading/writing files in Java?

java
// Modern (NIO.2, Java 7+)
String text = Files.readString(Path.of("data.txt"));
Files.writeString(Path.of("out.txt"), text);
Stream<String> lines = Files.lines(Path.of("big.txt"));  // streaming, low memory

Q51a. BufferedReader vs Scanner?

  • BufferedReader: fast line-by-line reading, no parsing
  • Scanner: token parsing (nextInt, nextLine), slower (regex-based), good for small input / interactive

Q51b. Serialization — Serializable vs Externalizable?

  • Serializable: marker interface, JVM auto-serializes via reflection
  • Externalizable extends Serializable: full control via writeExternal/readExternal
  • transient keyword: skip a field during serialization
  • Modern preference: don't use Java serialization (security CVEs, brittle versioning) — use JSON (Jackson), Protobuf, Avro

#D8. Spring Framework

Q52. Dependency Injection — types?

  • Constructor injection (preferred): @RequiredArgsConstructor / explicit constructor — final fields, easy testing, fail-fast on missing deps
  • Setter injection: optional dependencies, circular allowed
  • Field injection (@Autowired on field): convenient but anti-pattern — hidden deps, hard to test without Spring
java
@Service
public class TransferService {
  private final AccountRepository accounts;
  private final AuditLogger audit;
  public TransferService(AccountRepository accounts, AuditLogger audit) {
    this.accounts = accounts; this.audit = audit;
  }
}

📄 spring.io → "Dependency Injection".

Q52a. @Component vs @Service vs @Repository vs @Controller vs @RestController?

Annotation Purpose
@Component Generic Spring-managed bean
@Service Business logic layer (semantic)
@Repository Data access — also enables PersistenceException translation
@Controller MVC controller — returns view names
@RestController @Controller + @ResponseBody — returns JSON/XML
@Configuration Class declaring @Bean factory methods

All are Spring-detected via classpath scanning; pick the most specific.

Q52b. Build a REST endpoint in Spring Boot?

java
@RestController
@RequestMapping("/api/accounts")
public class AccountController {
  private final AccountService service;
  public AccountController(AccountService s) { this.service = s; }

  @GetMapping("/{id}")
  public ResponseEntity<AccountDto> get(@PathVariable String id) {
    return service.find(id)
      .map(ResponseEntity::ok)
      .orElse(ResponseEntity.notFound().build());
  }

  @PostMapping
  public ResponseEntity<AccountDto> create(@Valid @RequestBody CreateAccount req) {
    return ResponseEntity.status(201).body(service.create(req));
  }
}

Q52c. Spring Boot — what & how does auto-configuration work? A: Opinionated framework on top of Spring; embedded server, starters, @SpringBootApplication. Auto-config: @EnableAutoConfiguration reads spring.factories (or AutoConfiguration.imports in 3+); each auto-config class is @Conditional on classpath / property / bean → only kicks in when needed. Example: H2 on classpath → auto-config a DataSource; user defines a DataSource bean → auto-config backs off. 📄 spring.io → "Spring Boot Auto-configuration".

Q52d. Spring AOP — aspects, advice, pointcuts?

Term What
Aspect Class with cross-cutting concern (@Aspect)
Pointcut Expression matching join points (execution(* com.bank..*Service.*(..)))
Advice Action at the pointcut: @Before, @After, @Around, @AfterReturning, @AfterThrowing
Join point A method execution where advice can be applied
java
@Aspect @Component
public class AuditAspect {
  @Around("@annotation(Audited)")
  public Object audit(ProceedingJoinPoint pjp) throws Throwable {
    long start = System.nanoTime();
    Object result = pjp.proceed();
    auditLog.record(pjp.getSignature(), System.nanoTime() - start);
    return result;
  }
}

Use cases: logging, metrics, transactions, security. 📄 docs.spring.io → "AOP".

Q52e. @Transactional — how does it work? A: Spring proxy wraps the bean; on method enter starts a TX (or joins existing per propagation), on exit commits — on RuntimeException rolls back (default). Pitfalls:

  • Self-invocation (this.foo()) bypasses the proxy → no TX
  • Default rollback only on unchecked; configure rollbackFor = Exception.class to include checked
  • propagation = REQUIRES_NEW for separate TX

📄 docs.spring.io → "Transaction Management".

Q52f. Spring Security — authentication and authorization? A: Filter chain (SecurityFilterChain) intercepts each request:

  • Authentication: AuthenticationManager + UserDetailsService validates credentials; populates SecurityContextHolder
  • Authorization: method-level (@PreAuthorize("hasRole('ADMIN')")) or URL-based (.requestMatchers("/admin/**").hasRole("ADMIN"))
  • Password storage: BCryptPasswordEncoder (never plain text)
  • Session: stateless JWT or stateful HTTPS cookies (banking → stateful for instant revocation)

Banking pattern: OAuth2 Resource Server + JWT issued by gateway + DB sessions for revocation. 📄 docs.spring.io → "Spring Security".

Q52g. @Qualifier and @Primary?

  • @Primary: when multiple candidates exist, this bean wins by default
  • @Qualifier("name"): explicit selection by bean name
java
@Bean @Primary DataSource main() { ... }
@Bean DataSource readReplica() { ... }
@Autowired @Qualifier("readReplica") DataSource ds;

#D9. JPA & Hibernate

Q52h. JPA vs Hibernate? A: JPA = specification (javax.persistence / jakarta.persistence). Hibernate = the most common implementation. Spring Data JPA = layer on top providing JpaRepository<T, ID> boilerplate.

Q52i. Entity lifecycle states (Hibernate)?

State Description
Transient New new Entity(); not in persistence context, no DB row
Persistent / Managed Attached to context (em.persist, em.find); changes auto-flushed
Detached Was managed; context closed or detach() called
Removed em.remove() called; will be deleted on flush

Q52j. First-level vs second-level cache?

  • L1 (always on): per EntityManager / Session — same query within a TX returns cached entity
  • L2 (opt-in): SessionFactory-wide — needs provider (Ehcache, Hazelcast, Caffeine). Useful for read-heavy reference data.

Q52k. HQL vs JPQL?

  • JPQL: standard JPA query language (SELECT u FROM User u WHERE u.email = :e)
  • HQL: Hibernate's superset (more functions, native features)
  • Always parameterize (:name) — never string concat (SQL injection)

Q52l. EAGER vs LAZY fetch?

  • EAGER: loaded with parent (joins immediately) — risk: N+1 queries, fetching unused data
  • LAZY (default for collections, recommended): proxied; loads on first access — must be in TX or LazyInitializationException
  • Solve N+1: @EntityGraph, JOIN FETCH, @BatchSize

Q52m. Optimistic vs pessimistic locking?

  • Optimistic (@Version field): assumes no conflict; checks version on commit, throws OptimisticLockException on conflict — high concurrency
  • Pessimistic (SELECT … FOR UPDATE, LockModeType.PESSIMISTIC_WRITE): row-level DB lock — prevents conflicts but reduces throughput
  • Banking transfers → pessimistic on the row being debited; reads → optimistic

Minimum hands-on: Do the Spring "Building a RESTful Web Service" guide end-to-end (~30 min) so you can speak from real experience.

📄 docs.spring.io/spring-data/jpa; docs.jboss.org/hibernate; "Hibernate User Guide".

#7. Part E — Cloud / CI-CD / Microservices Q&A

Q53. What is CI/CD? A: CI = merge + auto build + test on every commit (catch breaks early). CD = auto deploy to staging/prod after pipeline passes. Tools: GitHub Actions, GitLab CI, Jenkins. Stages: lint → test → build → deploy. 📄 GitHub Actions docs; martinfowler.com "ContinuousIntegration".

Q54. Microservices vs monolith — trade-offs? A: Microservices = independent deployable services per bounded context, scale/own separately, tech freedom; cost = network complexity, distributed data, observability overhead. Monolith = simpler, but couples deploys. Banks typically run microservices-based products. 📄 martinfowler.com "Microservices"; microservices.io patterns.

Q55. How do microservices communicate? A: Sync: REST/gRPC. Async: message queues/events (Kafka, SQS) for decoupling + resilience. API Gateway fronts clients. Service discovery, circuit breakers (resilience). 📄 microservices.io → "Communication patterns"; AWS docs.

Q56. Core AWS services to name (JD mentions AWS)? A: EC2 (compute), S3 (object storage), Lambda (serverless), RDS (managed SQL), DynamoDB (NoSQL), CloudFront (CDN), IAM (access), API Gateway, ECS/EKS (containers). Frontend often: S3 + CloudFront for static, API Gateway + Lambda for backend. 📄 aws.amazon.com → "Getting Started"; AWS free-tier overview.

JD's image criterion lists GCP, AWS or Azure — see Q-JD-4 (§1.5.3) for the full AWS↔GCP↔Azure service-equivalent table.

Q57. How would you deploy a Next.js app? A: Static/SSG → S3 + CloudFront (or Vercel). SSR → containerized (ECS/Docker) or serverless (Lambda@Edge / Vercel). CI builds, runs tests, deploys artifact. Env vars via secrets manager. 📄 nextjs.org → "Deploying"; AWS Amplify docs.

See Q-JD-5 (§1.5.3) for deployment paths on all three clouds (AWS / GCP / Azure).

Q58. What is Docker / a container? A: Packages app + deps into a portable image; runs isolated as a container. Consistent across dev/prod ("works on my machine" solved). Dockerfile defines build; orchestrated by Kubernetes/ECS. 📄 docs.docker.com → "Get Started".

#Part E.5 — Microservices, SQL, DevOps, Design Patterns (extended)

#Microservices & cloud

Q58a. Microservices vs monolith — full trade-off?

Monolith Microservices
Deploy One artifact Many independent services
Tech One stack Polyglot per service
Scaling Scale the whole app Scale only the hot service
Data One DB DB-per-service (eventual consistency)
Operational complexity Low High — observability, networking, retries
Failure radius Whole app Isolated per service

Rule: start monolith → split when team / scale forces it. Banks run microservices. 📄 martinfowler.com → "Microservices"; "MonolithFirst".

Q58b. How does Spring Boot help build microservices? A: Spring Cloud + Boot starters: spring-cloud-starter-* provides service discovery (Eureka, Consul), config (Spring Cloud Config), gateway (Spring Cloud Gateway), circuit breakers (Resilience4j), tracing (Sleuth/Zipkin / Micrometer + OpenTelemetry).

Q58c. Service discovery — Eureka? A: Service registry — services register themselves on startup; clients query the registry instead of hardcoding URLs. Eureka (Netflix) is the classic; alternatives: Consul, Kubernetes DNS (cluster-native).

java
@SpringBootApplication
@EnableDiscoveryClient                          // service registers itself
public class AccountServiceApp { ... }

@FeignClient(name = "account-service")          // client uses logical name
public interface AccountClient { @GetMapping("/{id}") AccountDto get(@PathVariable String id); }

📄 spring.io → "Spring Cloud Netflix"; cloud-native: prefer K8s DNS or Istio.

Q58d. API Gateway — Zuul vs Spring Cloud Gateway? A: Single entry point that routes to services, handles auth, rate limit, request rewriting, aggregation.

Zuul 1 Spring Cloud Gateway
Model Servlet, blocking Reactive (WebFlux + Netty)
Status Maintenance only Spring's recommended choice
yaml
spring.cloud.gateway.routes:
  - id: accounts
    uri: lb://account-service                  # load-balanced via Eureka/K8s
    predicates: [ Path=/api/accounts/** ]
    filters: [ TokenRelay, AddRequestHeader=X-Source,gateway ]

Q58e. Circuit breaking — Resilience4j / Hystrix? A: Wraps remote calls. After N failures in a window, the breaker "opens" — subsequent calls fail-fast (no thundering herd). After cool-off, half-opens to test recovery.

java
@CircuitBreaker(name = "accountService", fallbackMethod = "fallback")
public Account getAccount(String id) { return client.get(id); }
public Account fallback(String id, Throwable t) { return Account.unavailable(id); }

Hystrix = deprecated. Use Resilience4j (lighter, Java 8+, supports rate limit/retry/bulkhead too). 📄 resilience4j.readme.io.

Q58f. Containerization for Spring Boot — typical Dockerfile?

dockerfile
# Multi-stage: smaller final image
FROM eclipse-temurin:21-jdk AS build
WORKDIR /app
COPY pom.xml ./
RUN mvn dependency:go-offline
COPY src ./src
RUN mvn -B package -DskipTests

FROM eclipse-temurin:21-jre
WORKDIR /app
COPY --from=build /app/target/*.jar app.jar
EXPOSE 8080
HEALTHCHECK CMD curl -f http://localhost:8080/actuator/health || exit 1
ENTRYPOINT ["java","-jar","/app/app.jar"]

Tip: Spring Boot 2.3+ supports mvn spring-boot:build-image (Cloud Native Buildpacks) for layered, optimized images without writing a Dockerfile.

Q58g. Kubernetes — Pods, Deployments, Services?

Resource What
Pod Smallest unit — one or more containers sharing network/volumes
Deployment Declarative manager of ReplicaSet → desired N pods, rolling updates, rollback
Service Stable virtual IP / DNS name in front of pods (load balancing)
Ingress HTTP/HTTPS routing (host/path) into the cluster
ConfigMap / Secret Externalized config / sensitive data
HPA HorizontalPodAutoscaler — scale on CPU/memory/custom metrics
yaml
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata: { name: account-service }
spec:
  replicas: 3
  selector: { matchLabels: { app: account-service } }
  template:
    metadata: { labels: { app: account-service } }
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: app
        image: registry/account-service:1.4.2
        ports: [{ containerPort: 8080 }]
        readinessProbe: { httpGet: { path: /actuator/health, port: 8080 } }
        resources: { limits: { memory: 512Mi, cpu: 500m } }

📄 kubernetes.io → "Concepts".

Q58h. REST vs gRPC?

REST + JSON gRPC
Protocol HTTP/1.1 / 2 HTTP/2 + binary Protobuf
Schema OpenAPI (optional) .proto (mandatory)
Browsers Yes Limited (gRPC-Web)
Streaming Server-sent events Bidirectional streams
Performance Good Better (binary, multiplexed)
Use Public APIs, SPAs Internal microservice-to-microservice

Typical bank setup: BFF exposes REST to the SPA; internal services talk gRPC.

Q58i. OpenAPI / Swagger? A: Specification (YAML/JSON) describing REST API: paths, request/response schemas, auth. Tools: Swagger UI (interactive docs), springdoc-openapi (auto-generates from @RestController + Bean validation), code generators.

Q58j. OAuth2 — securing microservices? A: Roles: Resource Owner (user), Client (your SPA), Authorization Server (Okta/Auth0/Cognito), Resource Server (your API). Flows:

  • Authorization Code + PKCE (preferred for SPAs/mobile)
  • Client Credentials (service-to-service)
  • Device Code (CLIs)

JWT bearer tokens: signed by AS; resource server validates signature + expiry + scope. Spring Security Resource Server: spring-boot-starter-oauth2-resource-server + jwk-set-uri. 📄 oauth.net/2; Spring Security OAuth2 Resource Server docs.

#SQL / Database

Q58k. ACID properties?

Meaning
Atomicity All ops in TX succeed or none — banking transfer must not partially commit
Consistency TX moves DB from one valid state to another (constraints enforced)
Isolation Concurrent TXs don't interfere — see isolation levels
Durability Once committed, survives crash

Isolation levels: Read Uncommitted < Read Committed (default Postgres) < Repeatable Read < Serializable. 📄 db textbook; Postgres docs → "Transaction Isolation".

Q58l. SQL vs NoSQL?

SQL (relational) NoSQL
Schema Strict, enforced Flexible / schemaless
Transactions Strong ACID Eventual / per-document
Joins Yes No (denormalize)
Scaling Vertical primarily Horizontal natural
Examples Postgres, MySQL, Oracle MongoDB (docs), Redis (KV), Cassandra (wide-col), Neo4j (graph)

Banking → SQL (Postgres/Oracle) for ACID transactions; NoSQL for caches, audit logs, search.

Q58m. Indexing — how does it improve performance? A: B-tree (default) / hash index lets the DB find rows in O(log n) instead of O(n) full scan. Trade-off: writes get slower (index maintenance), more storage.

  • Composite index on (col_a, col_b) helps queries filtering on col_a first
  • Cover index: include all SELECTed columns → no table lookup
  • Don't over-index: write-heavy tables suffer
  • EXPLAIN ANALYZE to verify use

Q58n. JOIN vs UNION?

  • JOIN: combines columns from related tables (INNER, LEFT, RIGHT, FULL)
  • UNION: stacks rows from multiple result sets vertically (must have same column shape). UNION ALL keeps duplicates (faster).

Q58o. Database normalization (1NF, 2NF, 3NF)?

Form Rule
1NF Atomic columns, no repeating groups
2NF 1NF + no partial dependency on composite key
3NF 2NF + no transitive dependency (non-key → non-key)
BCNF Stricter 3NF

Practice: normalize for OLTP; denormalize for OLAP/reporting.

Q58p. Pagination in SQL?

  • LIMIT N OFFSET M — simple but slow at high offsets
  • Keyset / seek pagination (WHERE id > :lastId ORDER BY id LIMIT N) — fast at any depth, recommended for large tables

Q58q. WHERE vs HAVING?

  • WHERE filters rows BEFORE aggregation
  • HAVING filters AFTER GROUP BY (operates on aggregated results)
sql
SELECT customer_id, SUM(amount) AS total
FROM transactions
WHERE created_at > NOW() - INTERVAL '30 days'   -- filter rows first
GROUP BY customer_id
HAVING SUM(amount) > 10000;                      -- filter groups after

#CI/CD & DevOps

Q58r. CI/CD pipeline for Java/Spring Boot — Jenkins or GitHub Actions?

yaml
# .github/workflows/ci.yml
on: [push, pull_request]
jobs:
  build:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-java@v4
        with: { distribution: temurin, java-version: 21, cache: maven }
      - run: mvn -B verify                              # compile + tests + integration tests
      - run: mvn sonar:sonar -Dsonar.token=$SONAR_TOKEN # SAST
      - uses: docker/build-push-action@v5
        with: { push: true, tags: registry/account-service:${{ github.sha }} }
      - name: Deploy
        run: kubectl set image deployment/account-service app=registry/account-service:${{ github.sha }}

Stages: lint → unit test → integration test → security scan → build image → push → deploy → smoke test.

Q58s. Monitoring — Prometheus + Grafana? A: Spring Boot Actuator exposes /actuator/prometheus (via Micrometer). Prometheus scrapes; Grafana dashboards visualize. Alertmanager fires on rule violations (e.g., http_server_requests_seconds_count{status=~"5.."} > 10/min).

java
@Bean
public TimedAspect timedAspect(MeterRegistry r) { return new TimedAspect(r); }
// @Timed("transfer.duration") on methods → metric automatically

Logs ↔ metrics ↔ traces = the "three pillars" of observability.

Q58t. Logging in Spring Boot — SLF4J + Logback?

  • SLF4J: facade — your code logs to it, ignorant of impl
  • Logback: default impl in Spring Boot
  • Structured (JSON) logs in production for ingestion into ELK / Loki / CloudWatch
  • Levels: TRACE < DEBUG < INFO < WARN < ERROR
  • Sensitive data (PII, account numbers) must not be logged — use a masking pattern
java
import org.slf4j.Logger; import org.slf4j.LoggerFactory;
private static final Logger log = LoggerFactory.getLogger(Service.class);
log.info("Transfer initiated id={} amount={}", txId, amount);  // parameterized — no string concat

#Design Patterns (Java)

Q58u. Singleton — implementation? A: One instance per JVM. Modern preferred form:

java
public enum AppConfig {
  INSTANCE;
  public String getDbUrl() { return ...; }
}
// Or — thread-safe lazy init via holder idiom
public class Cache {
  private Cache() {}
  private static class Holder { static final Cache INSTANCE = new Cache(); }
  public static Cache get() { return Holder.INSTANCE; }
}

Why enum: serialization-safe, reflection-proof, simplest correct lazy singleton. Note: Spring beans are singleton-by-default → prefer DI over manual singleton. 📄 Effective Java item 3 (Singleton with private constructor or enum).
