Q58v. Factory pattern? A: Encapsulate object creation behind a static method (or factory class). Caller doesn't know the concrete class.

java
public interface Notification { void send(String to, String msg); }

public class NotificationFactory {
  public static Notification of(String channel) {
    return switch (channel) {
      case "email" -> new EmailNotification();
      case "sms"   -> new SmsNotification();
      case "push"  -> new PushNotification();
      default      -> throw new IllegalArgumentException(channel);
    };
  }
}
Notification n = NotificationFactory.of("email");

Use case: decouple client from concrete implementations; pluggable channels.

Q58w. Builder pattern? A: Step-by-step construction of complex immutable objects with many optional fields, avoiding telescoping constructors.

java
public final class Transfer {
  private final String fromId, toId, currency; private final BigDecimal amount; private final String note;
  private Transfer(Builder b) { this.fromId=b.fromId; this.toId=b.toId; this.amount=b.amount; this.currency=b.currency; this.note=b.note; }
  public static Builder builder() { return new Builder(); }
  public static class Builder {
    private String fromId, toId, currency = "AUD", note;
    private BigDecimal amount;
    public Builder from(String id) { this.fromId = id; return this; }
    public Builder to(String id)   { this.toId = id; return this; }
    public Builder amount(BigDecimal a) { this.amount = a; return this; }
    public Builder currency(String c){ this.currency = c; return this; }
    public Builder note(String n)  { this.note = n; return this; }
    public Transfer build() { /* validate */ return new Transfer(this); }
  }
}
Transfer t = Transfer.builder().from("A").to("B").amount(new BigDecimal("100")).build();

Lombok's @Builder generates this automatically; Java records (16+) cover simpler cases.

Q58x. Other patterns interviewers may probe (one-line each):

  • Strategy: pluggable algorithm selected at runtime (Comparator).
  • Observer: subscribe to events; Spring's ApplicationEventPublisher, Java's Flow.Subscriber.
  • Decorator: wrap to add behavior (BufferedReader wraps Reader).
  • Adapter: convert one interface to another.
  • Template Method: superclass defines skeleton, subclasses fill steps (JdbcTemplate).
  • Proxy: stand-in (Spring AOP, Hibernate lazy proxies).

📄 GoF "Design Patterns" book; refactoring.guru/design-patterns.

#8. Part F — Banking Domain + Agile + Behavioral

JD-gap cross-refs: see also Q-JD-12 (English / global communication), Q-JD-13 (code review — challenge & recommend), Q-JD-14 (component-level architecture decisions), Q-JD-15 (prototyping). All in §1.5.4.

Q59. Special concerns building banking frontend? A: Security (XSS/CSRF, no sensitive data in localStorage, OWASP), accessibility (legal AA compliance), reliability/idempotency (no double-payments on retry), auditability/logging, data privacy (PII), strict testing, performance for all users. For banking specifically: idle timeout, database sessions (not JWT — need instant revocation), SameSite=Strict HttpOnly Secure cookies, audit logging all sensitive actions. 📄 OWASP Top 10; OWASP Cheat Sheets.

Q60. OWASP front-end risks you guard against? A: XSS (sanitize, React escapes by default — danger is dangerouslySetInnerHTML), CSRF (tokens, SameSite cookies — Server Actions auto-protect via origin check), insecure storage, broken auth, sensitive data exposure. Validate at every boundary.

Cross-ref: If the interviewer pushes deeper on JWT storage/refresh, Server Actions vs fetch, or authn/authz model choices, jump to Q20ai–Q20an in Part A.

XSS in React/Next.js context:

tsx
// ❌ DANGEROUS — allows script injection
<div dangerouslySetInnerHTML={{ __html: userContent }} />

// ✅ SAFE — sanitize with DOMPurify
import DOMPurify from 'dompurify';
<div dangerouslySetInnerHTML={{ __html: DOMPurify.sanitize(userContent) }} />

// ❌ DANGEROUS — URL injection
<a href={userUrl}>Link</a>  // userUrl = "javascript:alert(1)"

// ✅ SAFE — validate protocol
const safeUrl = /^https?:\/\//.test(url) ? url : '#';

Secrets exposure (critical in Next.js):

bash
# ❌ EXPOSED in client bundle — anyone can read
NEXT_PUBLIC_API_SECRET=abc123

# ✅ Server-only — never reaches client
API_SECRET=abc123  # Only accessible in Server Components/Actions

Security headers in Next.js:

js
// next.config.js
async headers() {
  return [{ source: '/(.*)', headers: [
    { key: 'X-Frame-Options', value: 'DENY' },            // Anti-clickjacking
    { key: 'X-Content-Type-Options', value: 'nosniff' },   // Anti-MIME sniff
    { key: 'Referrer-Policy', value: 'strict-origin-when-cross-origin' },
    { key: 'Strict-Transport-Security', value: 'max-age=31536000; includeSubDomains' },
    { key: 'Permissions-Policy', value: 'camera=(), microphone=(), geolocation=()' },
    { key: 'Content-Security-Policy', value: "default-src 'self'; script-src 'self'; style-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline'; img-src 'self' data: https:; connect-src 'self' https://api.example.com" },
  ]}];
}

CSP nonce (advanced — script-src without unsafe-inline):

js
// middleware.ts — generate nonce per request
import { NextResponse } from 'next/server';
import crypto from 'crypto';

export function middleware(req) {
  const nonce = crypto.randomBytes(16).toString('base64');
  const csp = [
    "default-src 'self'",
    `script-src 'self' 'nonce-${nonce}'`,  // Only scripts with matching nonce execute
    "style-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline'",
    "img-src 'self' data: https:",
  ].join('; ');

  const res = NextResponse.next();
  res.headers.set('Content-Security-Policy', csp);
  res.headers.set('x-nonce', nonce);  // Pass nonce to layout via header
  return res;
}
tsx
// app/layout.tsx — read nonce from header
import { headers } from 'next/headers';

export default async function RootLayout({ children }) {
  const nonce = (await headers()).get('x-nonce') ?? '';
  return (
    <html>
      <body>
        <Script nonce={nonce} src="/analytics.js" />
        {children}
      </body>
    </html>
  );
}

Idle timeout (banking requirement — auto-logout after inactivity):

ts
// lib/useIdleTimeout.ts
import { useEffect, useRef, useCallback } from 'react';

const IDLE_MINUTES = 10;

export function useIdleTimeout(onLogout: () => void) {
  const timerRef = useRef<ReturnType<typeof setTimeout> | null>(null);

  const resetTimer = useCallback(() => {
    if (timerRef.current) clearTimeout(timerRef.current);
    timerRef.current = setTimeout(() => {
      onLogout(); // Trigger auto-logout
    }, IDLE_MINUTES * 60 * 1000);
  }, [onLogout]);

  useEffect(() => {
    const events = ['mousemove', 'keypress', 'click', 'scroll', 'touchstart'];
    events.forEach(evt => window.addEventListener(evt, resetTimer));
    resetTimer(); // Start the timer on mount

    return () => {
      events.forEach(evt => window.removeEventListener(evt, resetTimer));
      if (timerRef.current) clearTimeout(timerRef.current);
    };
  }, [resetTimer]);
}

// Usage in root layout or app shell:
// useIdleTimeout(() => signOut({ callbackUrl: '/login' }));

No source maps in production:

js
// next.config.js
productionBrowserSourceMaps: false

Banking security one-liner (summary quote to use in interview):

"Frontend security focuses on: (1) XSS prevention — React auto-escapes, sanitize dangerouslySetInnerHTML with DOMPurify; (2) CSRF — SameSite cookies + Server Actions auto-protect via origin check; (3) CSP headers — restrict script sources, nonce-per-request for strictest mode; (4) Sensitive data — never expose secrets via NEXT_PUBLIC_, use Server Components; (5) Security headers — HSTS, X-Frame-Options, nosniff. For a banking app, add idle timeout (mousemove/keypress reset setTimeout), database sessions for instant revocation, and audit logging on all sensitive actions."

Banking-specific requirements:

Requirement Implementation
Idle timeout useIdleTimeout hook — mousemove/keypress/click reset setTimeout → auto logout after 10 min
Session revocation Database sessions (NOT JWT) — delete row = instant logout
Cookie security HttpOnly=true, Secure=true, SameSite=Strict
Input validation Zod schema at EVERY boundary (client form + server action)
Audit logging Log all: transfers, profile updates, login/logout with timestamp + IP
No source maps in prod productionBrowserSourceMaps: false in next.config.js

Auth pattern for Next.js banking app:

tsx
// middleware.ts — route protection
import { auth } from '@/lib/auth';
export default auth((req) => {
  if (!req.auth && req.nextUrl.pathname.startsWith('/account')) {
    return Response.redirect(new URL('/login', req.url));
  }
});

// Server Component page — double-check
export default async function AccountPage() {
  const session = await getServerSession();
  if (!session) redirect('/login'); // Never trust middleware alone
  return <Account user={session.user} />;
}

#F.3 — Transaction Failures, ACID, Concurrency Anomalies

Critical for banking domain questions. Interviewers test whether you understand WHY transactions exist and what breaks without them.

Q62. Walk me through all the ways a fund transfer can fail.

A: Every transfer has this pipeline:

text
VALIDATE → DEBIT sender → CREDIT receiver → COMMIT
Stage Failure Result without guard
Pre-validation Insufficient funds, invalid account Reject — no DB write
Network timeout after DEBIT Request sent, no confirmation Sender debited, receiver never credited
DB crash after DEBIT, before CREDIT First write committed, second never ran Money lost from system
Double-submit (user clicks twice) Two identical requests reach server Double-debit
Concurrent transfer same account Two reads see same balance, both debit Balance underflows — "lost update"
Constraint violation on CREDIT Credit rejected (e.g., account closed) Debit committed, credit failed → partial

Fix for all failure modes: wrap debit+credit in a single atomic transaction. Fix for double-submit: idempotency keys. Fix for concurrent balance race: row-level locking or optimistic concurrency.

Rationale: Interviewers probe whether you think beyond the happy path. The network-timeout-mid-transfer is the textbook partial-failure case that ACID atomicity solves.

📄 Evidence: Martin Kleppmann — Designing Data-Intensive Applications ch.7

Q63. How do you prevent double-payment when a user double-clicks "Transfer"?

A: Idempotency key — client generates a UUID once per transfer intent and sends it on every retry. Server checks a processed_keys table before executing:

text
Client → POST /transfers { idempotency_key: "uuid-abc", amount: 100, to: "acc-2" }
Server:
  if key EXISTS in processed_keys → return cached response (no re-execution)
  else → execute transfer → insert key + result → return response

Key stored with TTL (e.g., 24h). Any retry within that window returns the cached result without re-debiting.

Rationale: HTTP retries (browser, load balancer, mobile network) and UI double-clicks both produce duplicate requests. Without idempotency, each retry is a fresh debit. This pattern is used by Stripe and all major payment APIs.

📄 Evidence: Stripe — Idempotent Requests; AWS — Idempotency Tokens

Q64. Dirty read, non-repeatable read, phantom read — what are they?

A:

Anomaly What happens Banking example
Dirty read Read UNCOMMITTED data from another in-progress transaction T1 debits $100 (not yet committed). T2 reads balance — sees reduced value. T1 rolls back. T2 acted on data that never persisted.
Non-repeatable read Same SELECT returns DIFFERENT value within one transaction T1 reads balance = $500. T2 commits a transfer → balance now $400. T1 re-reads balance → $400. Two reads, two results.
Phantom read New ROWS appear in a repeated range query T1 selects all today's transactions → 5 rows. T2 inserts a new transaction and commits. T1 re-selects → 6 rows. "Ghost" row appeared.
Lost update Concurrent writes — later write overwrites earlier write T1 reads $500, T2 reads $500. T1 writes $400 (−$100). T2 writes $450 (−$50). T1's write lost — final is $450 not $350.
Dirty write Overwrite another transaction's uncommitted data T1 starts updating row. T2 overwrites same row before T1 commits. Rollback becomes unpredictable.

Rationale: These map 1-to-1 onto SQL isolation levels. In banking: dirty reads can cause decisions based on rolled-back data; lost updates silently corrupt balances; phantom reads affect fraud aggregation queries.

📄 Evidence: PostgreSQL — Transaction Isolation; ANSI SQL-92 isolation level definitions

Q65. SQL isolation levels — what does each prevent?

A:

Level Dirty Read Non-repeatable Read Phantom Read Performance
READ UNCOMMITTED ✅ allowed ✅ allowed ✅ allowed Fastest — no locks
READ COMMITTED ✗ blocked ✅ allowed ✅ allowed Good — PG default
REPEATABLE READ ✗ ✗ ✅ (MySQL locks; PG uses MVCC snapshot) Medium
SERIALIZABLE ✗ ✗ ✗ Slowest — full serializability

For banking fund transfers: use SERIALIZABLE or REPEATABLE READ. READ COMMITTED (PostgreSQL's default) is acceptable for reads but leaves balance transfers vulnerable to lost updates. Common middle ground: optimistic locking with a version column — no locks held, but concurrent writes are detected and rejected.

📄 Evidence: PostgreSQL — Transaction Isolation; MySQL — InnoDB Isolation Levels

Q66. Explain ACID with concrete banking examples.

A:

Atomicity — all or nothing.

sql
BEGIN;
  UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - 100 WHERE id = 'alice';
  UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance + 100 WHERE id = 'bob';
  -- If EITHER update fails → ROLLBACK → both changes undone
COMMIT;

Without atomicity: DB crashes after the first UPDATE. Alice loses $100. Bob never receives it. Money vanishes. With atomicity: Crash triggers rollback. Alice's balance is restored. Transfer either completes fully or never happened.

Consistency — data always satisfies defined rules.

sql
ALTER TABLE accounts ADD CONSTRAINT no_negative_balance CHECK (balance >= 0);
-- Any UPDATE that would produce balance = -50 → transaction aborted, constraint upheld

Without consistency: Two concurrent withdrawals both read $100 balance, both pass the "sufficient funds" check, both debit $100. Final balance = -$100. With consistency: Constraint (or application-level check + serializable isolation) rejects the violating write.

Isolation — concurrent transactions don't interfere.

text
T1: READ balance=500 → WRITE balance=400  (−$100)
T2: READ balance=500 → WRITE balance=450  (−$50)   ← reads before T1 commits!
Result WITHOUT isolation: $450 (T1's write lost — balance only reduced by $50, not $150)
Result WITH SERIALIZABLE: T1 runs first → $400; T2 runs second → $350 ✓

Real scenario: Two ATM withdrawals from the same account at the same second. Without isolation, both see the pre-withdrawal balance and both approve. Bank loses money.

Durability — committed transactions survive crashes.

text
Client: POST /transfers
Server: writes to WAL (Write-Ahead Log) on disk first, then ACKs
Server crashes 1ms after ACK
On restart: DB replays WAL → committed transfer is recovered

Without durability: Commit acknowledged, but only in memory. Crash wipes it. Customer sees "transfer complete" but balance unchanged. With durability: WAL ensures committed data is on disk before the ACK is sent. Recovery replays the log.

Interview one-liner (use this):

"ACID: Atomicity = debit+credit is one unit, rollback on any failure. Consistency = CHECK constraints and business rules always hold. Isolation = concurrent transfers don't interleave — I'd use REPEATABLE READ plus optimistic locking with a version column to avoid holding long locks. Durability = WAL guarantees committed transfers survive crashes. At the application layer I add idempotency keys for HTTP retries and verify account state at commit time to catch races the isolation level might miss."

📄 Evidence: PostgreSQL ACID · Kleppmann DDIA ch.7 · Martin Fowler — Optimistic Offline Lock

#8.5. AI-Assisted Workflow Q&A

2025–2026 trend: interviewers assume you already use Copilot/Cursor/Claude daily and probe judgment, not tool trivia — can you tell when AI output is wrong, do you own the risk, do you know its limits in a regulated codebase like a bank's.

Q-AI-1. How do you use AI tools (Copilot / Cursor / ChatGPT / Claude) in your daily workflow?

A: Three buckets: (1) Scaffolding — boilerplate, repetitive CRUD, test skeletons, type definitions from a shape I already designed. (2) Exploration — unfamiliar API/library, first-draft of an algorithm, explaining a legacy code path fast. (3) Review partner — paste a diff and ask "what would a senior reviewer flag here" before opening the real PR. I don't let it own architecture decisions or security-sensitive code (auth, payments, validation) without reading every line myself.

Rationale: Interviewers want a mature answer, not "I don't use AI" (looks behind) or "AI writes my PRs" (looks reckless, especially at a bank). Naming concrete buckets signals you've actually thought about it, not just used autocomplete.

📄 Evidence: Exponent — Google's AI-Assisted Coding Interview (2026)

Q-AI-2. How do you verify AI-generated code before merging it?

A: Same bar as human-written code, plus one extra pass: (1) run it — type-check, lint, unit tests; AI code compiles-looking but subtly wrong is the common failure. (2) Read every line as if a junior wrote it — AI hallucinates APIs/props that don't exist, silently drops edge cases (null checks, loading/error states), and over-engineers with unnecessary abstractions. (3) For anything touching money/auth/PII, trace it manually against the actual requirement — I do not trust AI's judgment on business rules it wasn't given full context for. (4) Never accept a security-relevant suggestion (crypto, auth, SQL) without cross-checking OWASP/official docs.

Rationale: This is the real test — can you catch when AI is confidently wrong. Give one concrete example if asked (e.g., "Copilot suggested a regex that broke on unicode input" or "suggested storing a JWT in localStorage").

Q-AI-3. Give an example where AI-generated code was wrong or you had to push back on a teammate's AI-assisted PR.

A (skeleton — fill with your own example): Situation: [feature/PR]. Task: [what you were building/reviewing]. Action: AI suggested [X] which looked plausible but [missed edge case / used deprecated API / introduced N+1 query / stored secret in wrong place]; caught it by [running tests / tracing data flow / checking docs]. Result: fixed before merge, and I added [a test / a lint rule / a comment] so it doesn't slip through again for the team.

Rationale: Same STAR shape as any behavioral answer — see Behavioral Skeletons below. Have a real, specific instance ready; vague "AI sometimes makes mistakes" answers score low.

Q-AI-4. What are the risks of AI-assisted coding in a banking/regulated environment specifically?

A: (1) Data leakage — pasting real customer data or internal code into a public model violates data-handling policy; use enterprise/offline tooling only. (2) Hallucinated dependencies — AI suggesting a package name that doesn't exist or is typosquatted (supply-chain risk). (3) False confidence — AI code looks clean and well-formatted, which makes reviewers skim instead of scrutinize — a bank can't afford a rubber-stamped auth bug. (4) License/IP — generated code can resemble training data closely enough to raise licensing questions. Mitigation: same code review bar as any PR, no exceptions for "AI wrote it," secrets/PII never leave approved tooling.

Rationale: Regulated-sector angle — shows you connect general AI awareness to the compliance reality of financial services, which is exactly what a "Cloud First" regulated-sector JD is screening for.

Q-AI-5. Has AI changed how you learn new technology (e.g., your Java willingness story)?

A: Yes — I use it as a fast first pass to understand unfamiliar syntax/frameworks (this is literally my Java story: I lean on AI to draft, then read Spring/Java docs to understand why the generated code is structured that way, not just copy it). It compresses the "get oriented" phase but doesn't replace understanding fundamentals — I still need to explain and defend the code in review.

Rationale: Ties directly to the Java-honesty line already in this doc (see checklist) — reuse it here so the AI question and the Java question tell one consistent, credible story instead of two disconnected ones.

#8.6. Behavioral Answer Skeletons (STAR)

Fill the bracketed parts with your own real experience before the interview — an unfilled skeleton reads as rehearsed and hollow. Keep each answer to 60–90 seconds spoken.

Q-B1. What's your biggest strength?

Skeleton: Pick ONE strength that's provably true from your work, not a generic trait. Structure: "[Strength] — here's where it showed up." Example shape: "I'm strong at turning ambiguous requirements into a working component fast. On [project], the spec was [vague thing]; I [asked 2 clarifying questions / built a quick prototype], shipped [result], and it became the pattern the team reused for [later feature]." Close by tying it to the role: "That maps to the JD's emphasis on [React/TS ownership]."

Rationale: Weak answers list adjectives ("I'm a hard worker"). Strong answers are one specific strength + one concrete proof point + relevance to this job.

Q-B2. What's your biggest weakness?

Skeleton: Pick a REAL weakness that isn't secretly a strength ("I work too hard" is a red flag to interviewers). Structure: "[Weakness] → what I noticed it costing me → concrete step I took → measurable improvement, still ongoing." Example shape: "Early on I'd over-engineer components for flexibility they never needed — [example]. I noticed PRs took longer to review and the abstractions rarely paid off. Now I default to the simplest version that satisfies the current requirement and only abstract on the second or third repeat (rule of three)." For this JD specifically, Java is the honest weakness already prepared: "My Java is currently AI-assisted, not deep — I understand the fundamentals and I'm actively closing the gap by [Spring Boot course / side project]."

Rationale: Interviewers grade self-awareness + growth action, not the weakness itself. A weakness with no fix attached is worse than a strong one.

Q-B3. What's the hardest/most challenging task or bug you've faced? How did you solve it?

Skeleton (STAR): S: one sentence — what broke and why it mattered (prod incident, deadline, unfamiliar system). T: what you specifically owned. A: the investigation steps in order — reproduce → isolate (bisect/logs/network tab) → hypothesis → fix → verify. Name a real tool (React DevTools Profiler, `console.trace`, binary search over commits, a debugger breakpoint). R: outcome in a number if possible (time saved, error rate, users unblocked) + what you changed afterward so it can't recur (test, lint rule, doc, alert). Reuse the "hard bug you debugged" STAR story already flagged in Part F behavioral list — this question and that one are the same slot.

Rationale: Interviewers score the debugging process (systematic vs guessing) more than the specific bug. Emphasize reproduce-first — jumping straight to fixes without reproducing is the #1 tell of a weak debugger.

Q-B4. Tell me about a conflict or disagreement with a teammate/reviewer. How did you resolve it?

Skeleton (STAR): S: a specific technical disagreement (naming, architecture, library choice, PR approach) — never pick an interpersonal/personality conflict. T: what was at stake (deadline, maintainability, correctness). A: you asked a genuine question first ("Curious why X over Y?") instead of declaring them wrong, backed your position with evidence (doc link, benchmark, prior incident), proposed a small experiment or 15-min sync instead of arguing in PR comments, and stayed willing to be convinced. R: the decision made (yours, theirs, or a hybrid) + that the relationship stayed healthy afterward. This doc already has the exact line for it (see Part F, Q-JD-13): "In a PR I disagreed with the auth approach — I asked for a 15-min sync, sketched the threat model, and we adopted a middle-ground design."

Rationale: Interviewers are screening for how you disagree, not whether you're always right. Yielding gracefully when proven wrong scores just as high as holding ground when you're right — both show maturity. Never badmouth the other person.

Q-B5. What feature/technology have you used recently that you genuinely liked? Why?

Skeleton: Pick something you can go deep on for 2 follow-up questions — shallow name-drops ("I like React") get probed and collapse. Structure: "[Feature] — the problem it solves for me is [X]. Concretely, on [project] it let me [before/after: e.g., replaced N lines of manual cache invalidation with SWR's revalidate-on-focus]. What I like specifically is [one technical detail, not marketing language] — e.g., Server Components' zero client-JS-shipped-by-default, or Zustand's no-provider external store, or TanStack Query's built-in stale-while-revalidate." Close with one honest limitation you've hit — shows critical thinking, not fanboying.

Rationale: This question probes genuine curiosity/passion vs box-checking. A specific technical detail + one caveat reads as real hands-on experience; generic praise reads as resume-speak.

#9. Part G — Live Coding: Data Structures & Algorithms

Format below: every question follows Q → A (approach) → Why this pattern (rationale) → 📄 Evidence (LeetCode # / pattern reference).

You said limited DSA knowledge. Master the patterns, not problem-by-problem memorization. Analyst-level live coding rarely demands Hard DP/graphs — focus Easy → Medium. Solve in JavaScript unless asked for Java.

#G.0 — Philosophy & Mindset

Q-DSA-1. What is DSA and why does it matter in interviews? A: DSA = Data Structures + Algorithms. A data structure organizes data for efficient access (Array, HashMap, Tree, Graph, Heap). An algorithm is a procedure to transform input → output (search, sort, traverse, optimize). Interviews test DSA because: (1) it proves you can decompose a problem, (2) the same handful of patterns solves 80% of real engineering work (caches, indexing, scheduling), (3) it's a fair language-independent signal. Why it matters: Live coding rounds explicitly test algorithm thinking. The interviewer is scoring problem decomposition + pattern recognition + communication > raw cleverness. 📄 Cracking the Coding Interview · NeetCode roadmap · LeetCode "Top Interview 150".

Q-DSA-2. What is the universal interview approach? (the "UMPIRE" / "PEDAC" loop) A: A 7-step protocol — say each step aloud:

  1. Clarify — input shape, ranges, edge cases (empty, null, duplicates, negatives, overflow), output shape.
  2. Examples — restate with 1–2 concrete examples; ask if expected output matches.
  3. Brute force first — state it + Big-O. Never code without naming brute force.
  4. Optimize — identify the pattern (next subsection) and the data structure that unlocks it.
  5. Pseudocode — outline before typing.
  6. Code — narrate as you type. Variable names readable.
  7. Test — walk the example + edge cases + state final Big-O (time and space).

Why: interviewers grade on communication and structure as much as correctness. A wrong-but-clear attempt scores better than a silent right answer. 📄 Hello Interview → "Algorithm Coding Interview"; LeetCode → "Interview tips".

Q-DSA-3. Big-O cheat sheet — memorize cold.

Structure / Op Access Search Insert Delete Notes
Array O(1) O(n) O(n) O(n) Fast index, slow shift
Dynamic array (JS Array, ArrayList) O(1) O(n) amortized O(1) push O(n) Resize doubles capacity
HashMap / HashSet — O(1) avg, O(n) worst O(1) O(1) Treeified bucket → O(log n) Java 8+
Stack / Queue — O(n) O(1) O(1) LIFO / FIFO
Singly linked list O(n) O(n) O(1) at known node O(1) at known node No random access
Binary search tree (balanced) — O(log n) O(log n) O(log n) Unbalanced → O(n)
Heap (binary) O(1) peek — O(log n) O(log n) Top-K, Dijkstra
Sorting (merge / quick) — — — — O(n log n)
Sorting (counting / radix) — — — — O(n + k) for bounded keys
BFS / DFS — — — — O(V + E)
Dijkstra (with heap) — — — — O((V+E) log V)

Space: recursive call stack counts (DFS = O(h) where h = tree height; balanced log n, skewed n).

Why: every solution must end with stated time + space. Interviewer ALWAYS asks "what's the complexity?". 📄 Big-O cheat sheet — bigocheatsheet.com.

Q-DSA-4. Pattern recognition — given a problem signal, which pattern do I reach for?

Problem signal in the prompt Pattern Tools
"Find pair / triplet summing to K" Hash lookup OR two pointers Map for unsorted; sort + two pointers for tuples
"Find duplicate / unique / count" Hashing Set / Map
"Subarray / substring with property" Sliding window l,r pointers + window state
"Sorted array, search, find boundary" Binary search lo,hi,mid
"Last in, first out / matching pairs" Stack [] + push/pop
"Linked list — middle, cycle, kth from end" Two pointers (fast/slow) Floyd's tortoise-hare
"Top K / Kth largest / median stream" Heap Min-heap of size K
"Tree / level traversal" BFS (queue) q = [root]
"Tree / depth / path / recursion" DFS recursion
"Grid / connected components / flood fill" DFS or BFS on grid visited set
"Shortest path unweighted" BFS queue
"Shortest path weighted" Dijkstra min-heap
"Overlapping intervals / merge / schedule" Sort + sweep sort by start
"Optimal substructure + overlapping subproblems" Dynamic Programming memo or table
"Permutations / combinations / subsets / N-Queens" Backtracking recurse + undo
"Stream of integers, find median / running stat" Two heaps min + max heap
"Prefix sum queries / range sum" Prefix sum array precompute
"Bit manipulation hint — XOR pairing" Bit ops ^, &, `

Why: the FIRST 60 seconds of a problem are pattern matching. Train yourself to map signal → pattern instantly. 📄 NeetCode "Roadmap" · Hello Interview → "DSA Pattern Library".

Q-DSA-4b. JavaScript skeleton for every pattern — memorize the structure, fill in problem logic.

Hash lookup — find pair/triplet summing to K:

js
// Complement lookup — O(n) time, O(n) space
function twoSum(arr, target) {
  const seen = new Map(); // value → index
  for (let i = 0; i < arr.length; i++) {
    const need = target - arr[i];
    if (seen.has(need)) return [seen.get(need), i];
    seen.set(arr[i], i);
  }
  return [];
}
// Brute force O(n²): nested loops checking every pair
// Optimize: hash trades O(n) space for O(n) time savings

Hashing — count / deduplicate / group:

js
// Frequency map
const freq = new Map();
for (const x of arr) freq.set(x, (freq.get(x) ?? 0) + 1);

// Set for O(1) existence check
const seen = new Set(arr); // O(n) build, O(1) has()

Sliding window — subarray/substring with constraint:

js
// Variable window — O(n)
function longestWithoutRepeat(s) {
  const inWindow = new Set();
  let l = 0, best = 0;
  for (let r = 0; r < s.length; r++) {
    while (inWindow.has(s[r])) { inWindow.delete(s[l]); l++; } // shrink
    inWindow.add(s[r]);
    best = Math.max(best, r - l + 1);
  }
  return best;
}
// Fixed window: slide r; when window > k, shrink l by 1 each step

Binary search — sorted array, find boundary:

js
// Exact match — O(log n)
function binarySearch(arr, target) {
  let lo = 0, hi = arr.length - 1;
  while (lo <= hi) {
    const mid = (lo + hi) >> 1;
    if (arr[mid] === target) return mid;
    arr[mid] < target ? (lo = mid + 1) : (hi = mid - 1);
  }
  return -1;
}
// Left boundary (first occurrence): on match → hi = mid - 1; return lo
// Right boundary: on match → lo = mid + 1; return hi

Stack — LIFO / matching pairs:

js
// Valid parentheses — O(n)
function isValid(s) {
  const stack = [];
  const pair = { ')': '(', ']': '[', '}': '{' };
  for (const c of s) {
    if ('([{'.includes(c)) stack.push(c);
    else if (stack.pop() !== pair[c]) return false;
  }
  return stack.length === 0;
}
// Monotonic stack: keep decreasing/increasing elements, pop when condition breaks

Two pointers fast/slow — Floyd's (cycle, middle of list):

js
// Detect cycle — O(n) time, O(1) space
function hasCycle(head) {
  let slow = head, fast = head;
  while (fast && fast.next) {
    slow = slow.next;
    fast = fast.next.next;
    if (slow === fast) return true;
  }
  return false;
}
// Find middle: same loop — when fast reaches end, slow is at middle

Heap — Top K largest (min-heap of size K):

js
// JS has no built-in heap — for interviews use sorted array or this class:
class MinHeap {
  constructor() { this.h = []; }
  push(v) { this.h.push(v); this._up(this.h.length - 1); }
  pop()  { const t = this.h[0], l = this.h.pop(); if (this.h.length) { this.h[0] = l; this._down(0); } return t; }
  peek() { return this.h[0]; }
  size() { return this.h.length; }
  _up(i) { while (i > 0) { const p = (i-1)>>1; if (this.h[p] <= this.h[i]) break; [this.h[p],this.h[i]]=[this.h[i],this.h[p]]; i=p; } }
  _down(i) { const n = this.h.length; while (true) { let m=i,l=2*i+1,r=2*i+2; if(l<n&&this.h[l]<this.h[m])m=l; if(r<n&&this.h[r]<this.h[m])m=r; if(m===i)break; [this.h[m],this.h[i]]=[this.h[i],this.h[m]]; i=m; } }
}
// Top K: push each element; when size > K, pop the smallest → only K largest remain

BFS — level traversal / shortest path unweighted:

js
// Tree level order — O(n)
function levelOrder(root) {
  if (!root) return [];
  const result = [], q = [root];
  while (q.length) {
    const level = [];
    for (let i = q.length; i > 0; i--) {  // snapshot size = this level's node count
      const node = q.shift();
      level.push(node.val);
      if (node.left)  q.push(node.left);
      if (node.right) q.push(node.right);
    }
    result.push(level);
  }
  return result;
}
// Graph BFS: same but with visited Set and adjacency list

DFS — depth / path / recursion:

js
// Max depth binary tree — O(n)
function maxDepth(root) {
  if (!root) return 0;
  return 1 + Math.max(maxDepth(root.left), maxDepth(root.right));
}
// Iterative DFS with explicit stack:
function dfsIter(root) {
  const stack = [root], out = [];
  while (stack.length) {
    const n = stack.pop();
    if (!n) continue;
    out.push(n.val);
    stack.push(n.right, n.left); // left last → processed first
  }
  return out;
}

DFS on grid — connected components / flood fill:

js
// Number of Islands — O(m*n)
function numIslands(grid) {
  let count = 0;
  const dfs = (r, c) => {
    if (r < 0 || r >= grid.length || c < 0 || c >= grid[0].length || grid[r][c] !== '1') return;
    grid[r][c] = '0'; // mark visited by sinking (mutates input — ask if allowed)
    dfs(r+1,c); dfs(r-1,c); dfs(r,c+1); dfs(r,c-1);
  };
  for (let r = 0; r < grid.length; r++)
    for (let c = 0; c < grid[0].length; c++)
      if (grid[r][c] === '1') { dfs(r, c); count++; }
  return count;
}

Intervals — sort by start + sweep:

js
// Merge intervals — O(n log n)
function merge(intervals) {
  intervals.sort((a, b) => a[0] - b[0]);
  const out = [intervals[0]];
  for (const [s, e] of intervals.slice(1)) {
    const last = out[out.length - 1];
    if (s <= last[1]) last[1] = Math.max(last[1], e); // overlap → extend end
    else out.push([s, e]);
  }
  return out;
}

Dynamic Programming — memo or table:

js
// Climbing stairs (Fibonacci DP) — O(n) time, O(1) space
function climbStairs(n) {
  let a = 1, b = 1;
  for (let i = 2; i <= n; i++) [a, b] = [b, a + b];
  return b;
}
// Coin change (unbounded knapsack) — O(amount * |coins|)
function coinChange(coins, amount) {
  const dp = Array(amount + 1).fill(Infinity);
  dp[0] = 0;
  for (let a = 1; a <= amount; a++)
    for (const c of coins)
      if (c <= a) dp[a] = Math.min(dp[a], dp[a - c] + 1);
  return dp[amount] === Infinity ? -1 : dp[amount];
}

Backtracking — permutations / subsets / N-Queens:

js
// All subsets — O(2^n)
function subsets(nums) {
  const result = [];
  const bt = (start, curr) => {
    result.push([...curr]);
    for (let i = start; i < nums.length; i++) {
      curr.push(nums[i]);
      bt(i + 1, curr);
      curr.pop(); // undo — THE defining backtracking step
    }
  };
  bt(0, []);
  return result;
}
// All permutations: instead of start index, use a `used` boolean array

Prefix sum — range queries in O(1):

js
function buildPrefix(nums) {
  const p = [0];
  for (const n of nums) p.push(p[p.length - 1] + n);
  return p;
}
// Sum of nums[l..r] inclusive = p[r+1] - p[l]

Bit manipulation — XOR pairing:

js
// Single number (all others appear twice) — O(n), O(1)
const singleNumber = nums => nums.reduce((acc, n) => acc ^ n, 0);
// Power of two: n > 0 && (n & (n - 1)) === 0
// Count set bits: n.toString(2).split('').filter(b => b === '1').length

Why: the skeleton is 90% identical across problems in the same pattern. What varies is the condition/update inside the loop or the recursion base case. Learn the skeleton cold — fill in problem-specific logic on interview day.

#G.1 — Arrays & Hashing (MUST master)

Pattern playbook:

  • Frequency map: for (const x of arr) freq.set(x, (freq.get(x) ?? 0) + 1).
  • Complement lookup: while iterating, ask "have I seen target - x before?".
  • Set for uniqueness: O(1) "have I seen this?".

Q-DSA-5. Two Sum (LC 1) — find indices of two numbers summing to target. A: Single pass + hashmap of value → index. For each x, check if target - x is already in the map; if yes return both indices, else store x.

js
function twoSum(nums, target) {
  const seen = new Map();
  for (let i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
    const need = target - nums[i];
    if (seen.has(need)) return [seen.get(need), i];
    seen.set(nums[i], i);
  }
}
// Time O(n), space O(n)

Why this pattern: brute force is O(n²) nested loop. Hash trades space for time — the canonical "complement lookup" pattern. Also appears in 3Sum, 4Sum (sort + two pointers around a fixed element). 📄 LeetCode 1; NeetCode "Arrays & Hashing".

Q-DSA-6. Valid Anagram (LC 242), Group Anagrams (LC 49) — frequency hashing. A: For Valid Anagram, count chars in both strings (or sort both → compare). For Group Anagrams, key each word by its sorted chars (or its 26-element char-count tuple) and bucket into a Map<string, string[]>.

js
function groupAnagrams(strs) {
  const m = new Map();
  for (const s of strs) {
    const key = [...s].sort().join('');
    if (!m.has(key)) m.set(key, []);
    m.get(key).push(s);
  }
  return [...m.values()];
}

Why: the signature of a string under reordering is its sorted form. Hashing-by-signature solves any "group by equivalence" problem. 📄 LeetCode 242, 49.

Q-DSA-7. Top K Frequent Elements (LC 347) — heap or bucket sort. A: Frequency map → either (a) push into a min-heap of size K (each push O(log K), total O(n log K)) or (b) bucket sort by frequency in O(n). Why: Top-K is a heap classic. Bucket sort wins when K ≈ n. 📄 LeetCode 347; NeetCode "Heap / Priority Queue".

#G.2 — Two Pointers

Pattern playbook: Two indices walk an array — converging (left/right) or one-direction at different speeds. Almost always after sorting (for sums) or on linked lists (for cycle/midpoint).

Q-DSA-8. Two Sum II — sorted (LC 167), 3Sum (LC 15). A: For sorted Two Sum: l=0, r=n-1; if sum < target, l++; if sum > target, r--; else return. For 3Sum: sort, fix one element with outer loop, two-pointer the rest. Skip duplicates.

js
function threeSum(nums) {
  nums.sort((a,b) => a - b);
  const out = [];
  for (let i = 0; i < nums.length - 2; i++) {
    if (i > 0 && nums[i] === nums[i-1]) continue;             // skip dup outer
    let l = i + 1, r = nums.length - 1;
    while (l < r) {
      const s = nums[i] + nums[l] + nums[r];
      if (s < 0) l++;
      else if (s > 0) r--;
      else {
        out.push([nums[i], nums[l], nums[r]]);
        while (l < r && nums[l] === nums[l+1]) l++;            // skip dup inner
        while (l < r && nums[r] === nums[r-1]) r--;
        l++; r--;
      }
    }
  }
  return out;
}
// Time O(n²), space O(1) extra

Why: sorting unlocks the two-pointer trick — monotonic decisions on each step. Pattern extends to 4Sum, "closest sum", trapping rain water. 📄 LeetCode 167, 15.

Q-DSA-9. Container With Most Water (LC 11), Move Zeroes (LC 283), Valid Palindrome (LC 125). A:

  • Container: l,r from ends; area = min(h[l], h[r]) * (r-l); move the smaller side inward.
  • Move Zeroes: write-pointer w; copy non-zeros forward, fill the rest with 0.
  • Palindrome: l,r converge; lowercase + skip non-alphanum; s[l] !== s[r] → false.

Why: each illustrates a different two-pointer flavor — converging-with-decision, write-pointer-skipping, mirror-comparison. 📄 LeetCode 11, 283, 125.

#G.3 — Sliding Window

Pattern playbook: Two pointers l, r. Expand r greedily, contract l when window violates a constraint. Track window state in a Map/Set or running sum/min/max.

Q-DSA-10. Longest Substring Without Repeating Characters (LC 3). A: Slide r. Keep a Set of chars in the current window. While s[r] is in the set, shrink from l. Update best length each step.

js
function lengthOfLongestSubstring(s) {
  const seen = new Set();
  let l = 0, best = 0;
  for (let r = 0; r < s.length; r++) {
    while (seen.has(s[r])) { seen.delete(s[l]); l++; }
    seen.add(s[r]);
    best = Math.max(best, r - l + 1);
  }
  return best;
}
// Time O(n), space O(min(n, alphabet))

Why: every sliding window problem is "smallest/longest window satisfying X". Recognize the invariant — here: no duplicates inside [l..r]. 📄 LeetCode 3.

Q-DSA-11. Best Time to Buy/Sell Stock (LC 121), Maximum Subarray (LC 53 — Kadane's). A:

  • Stock: track minSoFar; profit at i = price[i] - minSoFar; keep best.
  • Max Subarray (Kadane's): cur = max(x, cur + x) (extend or restart); best = max(best, cur).
js
function maxSubArray(nums) {
  let cur = nums[0], best = nums[0];
  for (let i = 1; i < nums.length; i++) {
    cur = Math.max(nums[i], cur + nums[i]);
    best = Math.max(best, cur);
  }
  return best;
}
// Time O(n), space O(1)

Why: both are "fixed window of size 1+greedy" — the running min / running sum is your window state. Kadane's is the gateway DP problem. 📄 LeetCode 121, 53.

#G.4 — Binary Search

Pattern playbook: Sorted array (or monotonic predicate). Maintain [lo, hi]; compute mid; halve. Two flavors: find exact (lo <= hi) or find boundary (lo < hi, then lo is answer).

Q-DSA-12. Binary Search (LC 704) — memorize this template.

js
function search(nums, target) {
  let lo = 0, hi = nums.length - 1;
  while (lo <= hi) {
    const mid = (lo + hi) >> 1;
    if (nums[mid] === target) return mid;
    if (nums[mid] < target) lo = mid + 1;
    else hi = mid - 1;
  }
  return -1;
}
// Time O(log n)

Q-DSA-13. Search in Rotated Sorted Array (LC 33), First Bad Version (LC 278). A: Rotated array — at each step, decide which half is sorted (compare nums[lo] vs nums[mid]); narrow into the half that contains the target by sorted-range check. First Bad Version — predicate isBadVersion(m) is monotonic; converge to leftmost true with lo < hi template.

Why: once the predicate is monotonic (false…false…true…true), binary search applies even without a sorted array. This is the "binary search on answer" extension — used in min-max optimization. 📄 LeetCode 33, 278.

#G.5 — Stack & Queue

Pattern playbook: Stack for LIFO (matching, parsing, monotonic), queue for FIFO (BFS, throttling).

Q-DSA-14. Valid Parentheses (LC 20), Min Stack (LC 155), Evaluate Reverse Polish Notation (LC 150). A:

  • Valid Parentheses: stack of opens; on close, pop and verify match. Empty stack at end → valid.
  • Min Stack: keep auxiliary stack of running minimums (push current min on each push).
  • RPN: push operands; on operator, pop two, compute, push result.
js
function isValid(s) {
  const pair = { ')':'(', ']':'[', '}':'{' };
  const st = [];
  for (const c of s) {
    if (c in pair) { if (st.pop() !== pair[c]) return false; }
    else st.push(c);
  }
  return st.length === 0;
}

Why: stacks model "most recent unmatched thing" — used in parsers, undo systems, monotonic stack problems (Daily Temperatures, Largest Rectangle). 📄 LeetCode 20, 155, 150.

#G.6 — Linked List

Pattern playbook: Two-pointer (slow/fast), dummy head node, reverse via three-pointer (prev, cur, next).

Q-DSA-15. Reverse Linked List (LC 206), Merge Two Sorted Lists (LC 21).

js
function reverseList(head) {
  let prev = null, cur = head;
  while (cur) {
    const next = cur.next;
    cur.next = prev;
    prev = cur;
    cur = next;
  }
  return prev; // new head
}
function mergeTwoLists(a, b) {
  const dummy = { next: null }; let t = dummy;
  while (a && b) {
    if (a.val <= b.val) { t.next = a; a = a.next; }
    else                { t.next = b; b = b.next; }
    t = t.next;
  }
  t.next = a ?? b;
  return dummy.next;
}

Why: 90% of linked-list problems reduce to reverse + merge + walk-with-two-pointers. Dummy head removes head-special-case bugs. 📄 LeetCode 206, 21.

Q-DSA-16. Linked List Cycle (LC 141) — Floyd's tortoise & hare. A: slow moves 1 step, fast 2 steps. If they meet → cycle. If fast reaches null → no cycle. Time O(n), space O(1) — beats hash-set approach on memory. Why: the canonical "cycle detection without extra memory" — extends to find cycle entry (LC 142 — restart slow from head, both move 1, meet at entry). 📄 LeetCode 141, 142.

#G.7 — Trees (binary)

Pattern playbook: DFS for depth/path/structure (recursion); BFS for level-by-level (queue). Postorder for bottom-up aggregation.

Q-DSA-17. Maximum Depth (LC 104), Same Tree (LC 100), Invert Tree (LC 226), Validate BST (LC 98).

js
function maxDepth(root) {
  if (!root) return 0;
  return 1 + Math.max(maxDepth(root.left), maxDepth(root.right));
}
function isValidBST(root, lo = -Infinity, hi = Infinity) {
  if (!root) return true;
  if (root.val <= lo || root.val >= hi) return false;
  return isValidBST(root.left, lo, root.val) && isValidBST(root.right, root.val, hi);
}

Why: BST validation is the lesson "compare each node against an inherited (lo, hi) window — not just direct children". Common bug: comparing only node.left.val < node.val. 📄 LeetCode 104, 100, 226, 98.

Q-DSA-18. Binary Tree Level Order (LC 102) — BFS template.

js
function levelOrder(root) {
  if (!root) return [];
  const out = [], q = [root];
  while (q.length) {
    const level = [], next = [];
    for (const n of q) {
      level.push(n.val);
      if (n.left) next.push(n.left);
      if (n.right) next.push(n.right);
    }
    out.push(level);
    q.length = 0; q.push(...next);
  }
  return out;
}

Why: BFS by levels — pattern for "right side view", "zig-zag", "min depth". 📄 LeetCode 102.

#G.8 — Graphs / BFS / DFS

Pattern playbook: Build adjacency list (Map<node, node[]>); track visited set; BFS for shortest path on unweighted, DFS for components/paths.

Q-DSA-19. Number of Islands (LC 200) — flood fill on grid.

js
function numIslands(grid) {
  const m = grid.length, n = grid[0].length;
  let count = 0;
  function dfs(i, j) {
    if (i < 0 || j < 0 || i >= m || j >= n || grid[i][j] !== '1') return;
    grid[i][j] = '0'; // mark visited
    dfs(i+1,j); dfs(i-1,j); dfs(i,j+1); dfs(i,j-1);
  }
  for (let i = 0; i < m; i++)
    for (let j = 0; j < n; j++)
      if (grid[i][j] === '1') { count++; dfs(i,j); }
  return count;
}
// Time O(m*n), space O(m*n) recursion worst case

Why: foundational grid-DFS pattern. Variants: max area, surrounded regions, walls and gates (multi-source BFS). 📄 LeetCode 200, 695, 130.

Q-DSA-20. Clone Graph (LC 133) — DFS with memoization. A: DFS each node; before recursing, record original → clone in a map to handle cycles. Why: classic "graph traversal with state" — memoization prevents infinite recursion on cycles. 📄 LeetCode 133.

#G.9 — Heaps / Priority Queues

Pattern playbook: Top-K, sliding-window aggregates, "process events in priority order".

Q-DSA-21. Kth Largest Element in a Stream (LC 703), Find Median from Data Stream (LC 295). A:

  • Kth Largest: maintain min-heap of size K. New value → push; if size > K, pop. Top of heap = answer.
  • Median Stream: two heaps — max-heap for lower half, min-heap for upper. Balance sizes; median = top(s).

Why: heaps give O(log K) per operation vs O(K log K) re-sort. JS lacks a native heap — write a small one or use a library; in interview say "I'd use a min-heap" + can sketch the impl. 📄 LeetCode 703, 295.

#G.10 — Intervals

Pattern playbook: Sort by start; sweep; merge if overlap.

Q-DSA-22. Merge Intervals (LC 56), Insert Interval (LC 57), Meeting Rooms II (LC 253).

js
function merge(intervals) {
  intervals.sort((a,b) => a[0] - b[0]);
  const out = [intervals[0]];
  for (let i = 1; i < intervals.length; i++) {
    const last = out[out.length - 1];
    if (intervals[i][0] <= last[1]) last[1] = Math.max(last[1], intervals[i][1]);
    else out.push(intervals[i]);
  }
  return out;
}

Why: every "scheduling / overlap" problem reduces to sort-by-start + sweep. Meeting Rooms II adds a min-heap of end times to count concurrent rooms — banking flavor (booking conflicts, transaction windows). 📄 LeetCode 56, 57, 253.

#G.11 — Backtracking

Pattern playbook: Recurse, choose, recurse, undo (the "undo" is what makes it backtracking).

Q-DSA-23. Subsets (LC 78), Permutations (LC 46), Combinations (LC 77).

js
function subsets(nums) {
  const out = [], cur = [];
  function bt(i) {
    if (i === nums.length) { out.push([...cur]); return; }
    bt(i + 1);                  // skip
    cur.push(nums[i]); bt(i+1); cur.pop(); // take + undo
  }
  bt(0);
  return out;
}

Why: any "enumerate all valid configurations" → backtracking. State = current partial; choices = next options; undo on return. Pruning early (constraint check before recurse) is the optimization knob. 📄 LeetCode 78, 46, 77.

#G.12 — Dynamic Programming (DP) — focused deep dive

Analyst-level rounds rarely ask Hard DP, but Easy/Medium DP shows up. Master the THREE-step recipe and 5 canonical problems.

Q-DP-1. What is Dynamic Programming? Definition + when to recognize it. A: DP solves a problem by combining solutions to overlapping subproblems that satisfy optimal substructure (optimal solution = function of optimal solutions to smaller subproblems). It's recursion + memoization (top-down) or iterative table fill (bottom-up).

Recognize DP when the prompt asks for:

  • "How many ways…" (counting)
  • "Minimum / maximum / longest / shortest…" (optimization) where greedy fails
  • A choice at each step with a clear state

Three-step recipe:

  1. Define state — what does dp[i] (or dp[i][j]) represent? Be precise: "the maximum X considering the first i items".
  2. Recurrence — write dp[i] in terms of dp[i-1] (or other smaller states).
  3. Base case + iterate — boundary values + fill order.

Top-down vs bottom-up:

Top-down (memoization) Bottom-up (tabulation)
Recursive function + cache Iterative loop filling array
Easier to write — directly mirrors recurrence Faster (no recursion overhead), no stack risk
Computes only needed states Computes all states

Space optimization: if dp[i] only depends on dp[i-1] and dp[i-2], you only need 2 vars instead of an array (Climbing Stairs, Fibonacci). This often turns O(n) space → O(1).

📄 Erik Demaine MIT OCW "Dynamic Programming" lectures · NeetCode "1-D / 2-D DP"; CLRS Ch.15.

Q-DP-2. Climbing Stairs (LC 70) — the "Fibonacci-style" gateway DP. A: At step i, you arrived from step i-1 (one step) or step i-2 (two steps). So dp[i] = dp[i-1] + dp[i-2]. Space-optimized to O(1):

js
function climbStairs(n) {
  if (n <= 2) return n;
  let a = 1, b = 2;
  for (let i = 3; i <= n; i++) [a, b] = [b, a + b];
  return b;
}

Why: identical recurrence to Fibonacci. Pattern: "ways to reach state n = sum of ways to reach states that lead to n". Same shape: House Robber, Decode Ways, Min Cost Climbing Stairs. 📄 LeetCode 70.

Q-DP-3. House Robber (LC 198) — non-adjacent choice. A: At house i, choose: rob (dp[i-2] + nums[i]) or skip (dp[i-1]). dp[i] = max(dp[i-1], dp[i-2] + nums[i]). Space O(1) with 2 vars. Why: "take-or-skip with constraint" pattern. Variant: House Robber II (circular array — run twice excluding first or last). 📄 LeetCode 198, 213.

Q-DP-4. Coin Change (LC 322) — unbounded knapsack flavor. A: dp[a] = min coins to make amount a. For each amount, try every coin: dp[a] = min(dp[a], dp[a - coin] + 1). Initialize dp[0]=0, others = Infinity. If dp[amount] === Infinity return -1.

js
function coinChange(coins, amount) {
  const dp = Array(amount + 1).fill(Infinity);
  dp[0] = 0;
  for (let a = 1; a <= amount; a++)
    for (const c of coins)
      if (c <= a) dp[a] = Math.min(dp[a], dp[a - c] + 1);
  return dp[amount] === Infinity ? -1 : dp[amount];
}
// Time O(amount * coins.length), space O(amount)

Why: prototype of "unbounded knapsack" — each coin reusable. Reverse-direction variants give "number of ways" (LC 518). 📄 LeetCode 322, 518.

Q-DP-5. Longest Increasing Subsequence (LC 300) — sequence DP. A: dp[i] = LIS ending exactly at i. For each i, scan all j<i with nums[j] < nums[i] and take dp[i] = max(dp[i], dp[j]+1). O(n²). Optimized to O(n log n) using a "patience sorting" tails array + binary search. Why: introduces 2-state interaction. The O(n log n) variant is the "binary-search on a derived array" trick. 📄 LeetCode 300.

Q-DP-6. Unique Paths (LC 62) — 2D grid DP. A: dp[i][j] = paths to cell (i,j). Recurrence: dp[i][j] = dp[i-1][j] + dp[i][j-1]. Initialize first row/col to 1. Space optimization: 1D row, in-place update. Why: foundational 2D DP. Variants: Unique Paths II (obstacles), Min Path Sum (replace + with min), Edit Distance (3 choices: replace, insert, delete). 📄 LeetCode 62, 63, 64, 72.

DP problem-recognition cheat sheet:

Problem signal DP shape Examples
"Climb / step / Fibonacci-like" 1D, dp[i] = f(dp[i-1], dp[i-2]) LC 70, 198, 91
"Min / max ending at index i" 1D LC 53 (Kadane), 152
"Longest subsequence / LCS / LIS" 2D dp[i][j] LC 300, 1143, 583, 72
"Coin change / knapsack" 1D dp[amount] or 2D dp[i][cap] LC 322, 518, 416
"Grid paths / min sum" 2D dp[i][j] LC 62, 64, 221
"Partition / interval" 2D dp[i][j] over [i..j] LC 5 (palindrome), 312
