#G.15 — Live Coding: Confirmed Insider Problems

Source: insider tip from a candidate who sat the round (2026-06-05). Three exact algorithmic problems confirmed for the dev track. Treat as must-solve cold, narrate aloud.

LC-1. Find maxOdd and maxEven in an array.

Problem: Given an array of integers, return the maximum odd number and the maximum even number.

Brute force = optimal — must inspect every element, so O(n) is the floor.

js
// O(n) time, O(1) space
function maxOddEven(arr) {
  let maxOdd = -Infinity, maxEven = -Infinity;
  for (const n of arr) {
    if (n % 2 !== 0) maxOdd  = Math.max(maxOdd, n);   // odd: remainder ≠ 0
    else             maxEven = Math.max(maxEven, n);
  }
  return {
    maxOdd:  maxOdd  === -Infinity ? null : maxOdd,
    maxEven: maxEven === -Infinity ? null : maxEven,
  };
}

// Test cases — state these aloud:
maxOddEven([3, 1, 4, 1, 5, 9, 2, 6]);  // { maxOdd: 9, maxEven: 6 }
maxOddEven([2, 4, 6]);                  // { maxOdd: null, maxEven: 6 }
maxOddEven([-3, -1, -4, -2]);           // { maxOdd: -1, maxEven: -2 }
maxOddEven([]);                         // { maxOdd: null, maxEven: null }

How to approach / narrate:

  1. "Single pass — track two running maximums initialized to -Infinity."
  2. "Odd check: n % 2 !== 0 works for negatives too — -3 % 2 === -1 in JS, which is !== 0. No need for Math.abs."
  3. "Return null when no odd/even found (don't return -Infinity to the caller)."
  4. "Time O(n) — can't do better; Space O(1)."

Rationale: Warmup problem — tests basic iteration and guard-clause hygiene. Trap: forgetting negatives. Null guard shows production mindset.

Edge cases: empty array, all-same-parity, all negatives, single element.

LC-2. Count minimum adjacent swaps to group white balls at front.

Problem: Given a string of W (white) and R (red) balls (e.g., "WRRRRWWRR"), count the minimum adjacent swaps to move all W balls to the front.

Brute force — bubble sort simulation — O(n²):

js
function countSwapsBrute(s) {
  const arr = s.split('');
  let swaps = 0, changed = true;
  while (changed) {
    changed = false;
    for (let i = 0; i < arr.length - 1; i++) {
      if (arr[i] === 'R' && arr[i+1] === 'W') {
        [arr[i], arr[i+1]] = [arr[i+1], arr[i]];
        swaps++;
        changed = true;
      }
    }
  }
  return swaps;
}

Optimal — inversion count — O(n), O(1) space:

js
// Key insight: each W needs to jump over every R that precedes it.
// Count of (R before W) pairs = minimum swaps.
function countSwaps(s) {
  let redsSeen = 0, swaps = 0;
  for (const ball of s) {
    if (ball === 'R') redsSeen++;
    else swaps += redsSeen; // this W must pass all preceding R's
  }
  return swaps;
}

// Trace "WRRRRWWRR":
// W(pos 0): reds=0 → swaps += 0
// R(pos 1): reds=1
// R(pos 2): reds=2
// R(pos 3): reds=3
// R(pos 4): reds=4
// W(pos 5): swaps += 4 → swaps=4
// W(pos 6): swaps += 4 → swaps=8
// R(pos 7,8): reds=6 (no more W's)
countSwaps("WRRRRWWRR"); // → 8

countSwaps("RWRW");  // R:r=1; W:+1; R:r=2; W:+2 → 3
countSwaps("WWRR");  // W:+0; W:+0; R,R → 0  (already sorted)
countSwaps("RRWW");  // R:r=1; R:r=2; W:+2; W:+2 → 4

How to approach / narrate:

  1. "Brute force: bubble-sort simulation — scan left-to-right, swap any R,W pair, repeat. O(n²)."
  2. "Optimize: notice each W must jump over exactly the R's to its left. That's an inversion count."
  3. "Single pass: maintain redsSeen counter. Each time I encounter a W, redsSeen tells me exactly how many swaps it needs. Sum all."
  4. "Time O(n), Space O(1)."

Rationale: Inversion counting pattern. Key insight: minimum adjacent swaps = number of out-of-order pairs in the target ordering. This avoids simulation entirely.

Edge cases: all W → 0, all R → 0, already sorted "WWRR" → 0, reverse sorted "RRWW" → n_R * n_W.

LC-3. Count steps to reduce integer to zero.

Problem: Given a positive integer, apply these rules repeatedly until reaching 0, count the steps:

  • If even: divide by 2
  • If odd: subtract 1

Example: 10 → 5 → 4 → 2 → 1 → 0 = 5 steps

Brute force — simulation — O(log n): (this IS near-optimal already)

js
function countSteps(n) {
  let steps = 0;
  while (n > 0) {
    n = n % 2 === 0 ? n / 2 : n - 1;
    steps++;
  }
  return steps;
}

// Traces:
// 10 → 5(1) → 4(2) → 2(3) → 1(4) → 0(5)  = 5 ✓
// 6  → 3(1) → 2(2) → 1(3) → 0(4)           = 4
// 1  → 0(1)                                 = 1
// 0  → 0 steps
countSteps(10); // 5
countSteps(6);  // 4
countSteps(1);  // 1
countSteps(0);  // 0

Optimal — bit manipulation insight — O(log n), elegant O(1) formula:

js
// Binary representation reveals the pattern:
// Each '1' bit costs 2 steps (subtract-1 then divide-2), except the leading '1' costs 1
// Each '0' bit costs 1 step (divide-2)
// Total = (count of 1-bits) + (bit length) - 1

function countStepsBit(n) {
  if (n === 0) return 0;
  const bits = n.toString(2);
  const ones = bits.split('').filter(b => b === '1').length;
  return ones + bits.length - 1;
}

// Verify:
// 10 = "1010" → ones=2, len=4 → 2+4-1=5 ✓
// 6  = "110"  → ones=2, len=3 → 2+3-1=4 ✓
// 8  = "1000" → ones=1, len=4 → 1+4-1=4 ✓ (8→4→2→1→0)
countStepsBit(10); // 5
countStepsBit(6);  // 4

How to approach / narrate:

  1. "Start with simulation — while loop, divide if even else subtract. O(log n) since n halves on every even step."
  2. "For the optimized insight: draw the binary representation. Each 1 bit needs subtract-1 (making it 0) then divide-2 — that's 2 ops. Each 0 bit needs divide-2 — 1 op. The leading 1 is special: subtract-1 then we're done."
  3. "Formula: ones + bitLength - 1. Mention this as a bonus — simulation is already acceptable."

Rationale: This is LeetCode 1342 exactly. Simulation is a clean O(log n) — correct and sufficient. Bit insight shows depth. For analyst level, simulation + edge cases + correct complexity = pass.

Edge cases: n=0 → 0 steps, n=1 → 1 step, powers of 2 (only 0-bits after leading 1 → bitLength-1 steps).

📄 Evidence: LeetCode 1342 — "Number of Steps to Reduce a Number to Zero" · NeetCode Bit Manipulation section

#G.14 — JavaScript templates to memorize

js
// HashMap frequency
const freq = new Map();
for (const x of arr) freq.set(x, (freq.get(x) ?? 0) + 1);

// Two pointers (converging)
let l = 0, r = arr.length - 1;
while (l < r) { /* ... */ l++; r--; }

// Sliding window (variable)
let l = 0; const seen = new Set();
for (let r = 0; r < s.length; r++) {
  while (seen.has(s[r])) { seen.delete(s[l]); l++; }
  seen.add(s[r]);
}

// Binary search (exact)
let lo = 0, hi = arr.length - 1;
while (lo <= hi) {
  const mid = (lo + hi) >> 1;
  if (arr[mid] === target) return mid;
  arr[mid] < target ? (lo = mid + 1) : (hi = mid - 1);
}

// Binary search (leftmost true — boundary)
let lo = 0, hi = n;
while (lo < hi) {
  const mid = (lo + hi) >> 1;
  if (predicate(mid)) hi = mid;
  else lo = mid + 1;
}
// lo === smallest index where predicate is true

// BFS (level by level)
const q = [root];
while (q.length) {
  const next = [];
  for (const node of q) { /* visit */ if (node.left) next.push(node.left); if (node.right) next.push(node.right); }
  q.length = 0; q.push(...next);
}

// DFS recursive
function dfs(node) { if (!node) return; /* visit */ dfs(node.left); dfs(node.right); }

// Backtracking skeleton
function bt(state) {
  if (isGoal(state)) { record(state); return; }
  for (const choice of choices(state)) {
    apply(state, choice);
    bt(state);
    undo(state, choice);
  }
}

// DP top-down memo
const memo = new Map();
function f(n) {
  if (memo.has(n)) return memo.get(n);
  if (base(n)) return baseValue(n);
  const v = combine(f(n-1), f(n-2));
  memo.set(n, v);
  return v;
}

// JS sort gotcha — default is lexicographic
arr.sort((a,b) => a - b); // numeric ascending — ALWAYS pass comparator on numbers

#G.15 — Sorting (know conceptually, rarely hand-code)

Algorithm Time Space Stable? When
Quick sort O(n log n) avg, O(n²) worst O(log n) recursion No General default; in-place
Merge sort O(n log n) all cases O(n) Yes Linked lists, external sorting
Heap sort O(n log n) O(1) in-place No Memory-constrained
Insertion sort O(n²), O(n) nearly-sorted O(1) Yes Small / nearly-sorted arrays
Counting sort O(n + k) O(k) Yes Bounded integer keys
Bubble O(n²) O(1) Yes Only to explain — never use

JS arr.sort() default is lexicographic — [1,2,10].sort() → [1,10,2]. Always pass a comparator for numbers.

#G.16 — Core Data Structures & Algorithms Playbook

This appendix turns the most interview-relevant data structures and algorithms into a mobile-friendly playbook. It does not literally attempt to list every algorithm ever invented; instead it covers the core set that most interview problems reduce to, plus a compact map of the remaining high-value families.

Reading strategy: if you can explain the definition, philosophy, invariant, implementation shape, and trade-off of the items below, you can usually derive the rest under interview pressure instead of memorizing an infinite catalog.
Binary Search Quick Sort Linked List Stack Queue / Deque Min / Max Heap Hash Map / Set Tree / BST Graph + BFS / DFS Trie Union-Find Dynamic Programming
Binary SearchO(log n)

Definition: Binary search finds an exact value or a boundary in a sorted or monotonic search space by repeatedly discarding half.

Philosophy: Don’t “search everywhere.” Keep an invariant about where the answer can still live, then shrink that valid region aggressively.

The approach: Define lo and hi, calculate mid, compare once, then move one boundary so the impossible half disappears. Boundary-search variants change the condition from “equal to target” into “first index where predicate becomes true.”

How to remember and understand it: Ask yourself: “Can I throw away half the search space with one yes/no question?” If the data is sorted or the predicate is monotonic, binary search is probably available.

When to use: Sorted arrays, first/last occurrence, lower/upper bound, search on answer, capacity/timing threshold problems.

Alternative way: Linear scan for tiny inputs or unsorted one-off data. Hash maps are better for repeated exact lookup when sorting is not already given.

js
function binarySearch(arr, target) {
  let lo = 0;
  let hi = arr.length - 1;

  while (lo <= hi) {
    const mid = lo + ((hi - lo) >> 1);

    if (arr[mid] === target) return mid;
    if (arr[mid] < target) lo = mid + 1;
    else hi = mid - 1;
  }

  return -1;
}

function firstTrue(lo, hi, predicate) {
  while (lo < hi) {
    const mid = lo + ((hi - lo) >> 1);
    if (predicate(mid)) hi = mid;
    else lo = mid + 1;
  }
  return lo;
}
Quick Sortavg O(n log n)

Definition: Quick sort is a divide-and-conquer sorting algorithm that partitions around a pivot, then recursively sorts the left and right sides.

Philosophy: Put one element in its final position, then solve two smaller copies of the same problem.

The approach: Choose a pivot, partition all smaller values left and larger values right, then recurse on each side until the subarray size is 0 or 1.

How to remember and understand it: The key mental model is: after partition, the pivot never moves again. Everything else is just repeating that fact on smaller ranges.

When to use: Interview sorting implementations, in-place array sorting, partition-based reasoning, quickselect cousins like “k-th largest”.

Alternative way: Merge sort for stable ordering and guaranteed O(n log n). Heap sort for O(1) extra memory. In production JS, prefer built-in sort unless the interview explicitly asks you to implement it.

js
function quickSort(input) {
  const arr = [...input];

  function partition(lo, hi) {
    const pivot = arr[hi];
    let i = lo;

    for (let j = lo; j < hi; j++) {
      if (arr[j] <= pivot) {
        [arr[i], arr[j]] = [arr[j], arr[i]];
        i++;
      }
    }

    [arr[i], arr[hi]] = [arr[hi], arr[i]];
    return i;
  }

  function sort(lo, hi) {
    if (lo >= hi) return;
    const p = partition(lo, hi);
    sort(lo, p - 1);
    sort(p + 1, hi);
  }

  sort(0, arr.length - 1);
  return arr;
}
Linked Listpointer rewiring

Definition: A linked list stores values in nodes, where each node points to the next node instead of living in contiguous memory like an array.

Philosophy: Give up O(1) random access so insertion and deletion become cheap once you already hold the right node.

The approach: Learn three templates: sentinel/dummy node, reverse with prev/curr/next, and slow/fast pointers for middle/cycle problems.

How to remember and understand it: Draw the arrows. Most linked-list bugs vanish when you literally sketch what each pointer should point to before and after the change.

When to use: LRU cache internals, queue internals, cheap node splicing, cycle detection, reverse-in-place tasks.

Alternative way: In JavaScript, arrays are usually simpler and often faster in real apps. Use lists when the problem is specifically pointer-centric.

js
class ListNode {
  constructor(val, next = null) {
    this.val = val;
    this.next = next;
  }
}

function fromArray(values) {
  const dummy = new ListNode(0);
  let tail = dummy;

  for (const value of values) {
    tail.next = new ListNode(value);
    tail = tail.next;
  }

  return dummy.next;
}

function reverseList(head) {
  let prev = null;
  let curr = head;

  while (curr) {
    const next = curr.next;
    curr.next = prev;
    prev = curr;
    curr = next;
  }

  return prev;
}
StackLIFO

Definition: A stack is a Last-In-First-Out structure where the most recently pushed element is the first one popped.

Philosophy: The latest unfinished context is the one you need next.

The approach: Support push, pop, and peek. Many interview problems wrap that with a rule like matching brackets or maintaining a monotonic property.

How to remember and understand it: Think browser history or a stack of plates: the top item is the only one immediately available.

When to use: Parentheses validation, DFS, undo/redo, expression parsing, monotonic stack questions like “next greater element”.

Alternative way: Recursion gives you an implicit call stack. A queue is the opposite choice when arrival order matters more than recency.

js
class Stack {
  constructor() {
    this.items = [];
  }

  push(value) {
    this.items.push(value);
  }

  pop() {
    return this.items.pop();
  }

  peek() {
    return this.items[this.items.length - 1];
  }

  isEmpty() {
    return this.items.length === 0;
  }
}

function isValidParentheses(s) {
  const pairs = new Map([
    [')', '('],
    [']', '['],
    ['}', '{'],
  ]);
  const stack = new Stack();

  for (const ch of s) {
    if (!pairs.has(ch)) {
      stack.push(ch);
      continue;
    }
    if (stack.pop() !== pairs.get(ch)) return false;
  }

  return stack.isEmpty();
}
Queue / DequeFIFO

Definition: A queue is First-In-First-Out. A deque generalizes that so you can push/pop from both ends.

Philosophy: Process work in arrival order unless the problem explicitly says a newer or “better” item should jump the line.

The approach: Maintain a head index instead of repeatedly calling shift() on large JS arrays. Deques add front operations for sliding-window and monotonic structures.

How to remember and understand it: Think supermarket line for a queue, train cars you can hook from both ends for a deque.

When to use: BFS, task scheduling, producer/consumer flow, rate limiting, sliding window maximum with a deque.

Alternative way: A stack if you need reverse order. A heap if the “most important” item should leave first rather than the oldest.

js
class Queue {
  constructor() {
    this.items = [];
    this.head = 0;
  }

  enqueue(value) {
    this.items.push(value);
  }

  dequeue() {
    if (this.head === this.items.length) return undefined;
    const value = this.items[this.head];
    this.head++;

    if (this.head > 50 && this.head * 2 > this.items.length) {
      this.items = this.items.slice(this.head);
      this.head = 0;
    }

    return value;
  }

  peek() {
    return this.items[this.head];
  }

  size() {
    return this.items.length - this.head;
  }
}
Min Heap / Max Heaptop element always ready

Definition: A heap is a complete binary tree, usually stored in an array, where each parent is ordered before its children. In a min heap the smallest element is on top; in a max heap the largest.

Philosophy: Don’t sort everything if you only need the current winner.

The approach: Use array indices for parent/child relationships, then bubbleUp after insert and bubbleDown after remove.

How to remember and understand it: The root is always the current champion. Every swap is just restoring that champion hierarchy locally.

When to use: Top-k, streaming min/max, scheduling, k-way merge, Dijkstra’s algorithm, median-of-stream variants.

Alternative way: Sorted arrays are simpler if the dataset is tiny. Balanced trees work too, but heaps are usually the cleanest for repeated best-item extraction.

js
class Heap {
  constructor(compare = (a, b) => a - b) {
    this.data = [];
    this.compare = compare;
  }

  peek() {
    return this.data[0];
  }

  push(value) {
    this.data.push(value);
    this.bubbleUp(this.data.length - 1);
  }

  pop() {
    if (this.data.length === 0) return undefined;
    const top = this.data[0];
    const last = this.data.pop();

    if (this.data.length > 0) {
      this.data[0] = last;
      this.bubbleDown(0);
    }

    return top;
  }

  bubbleUp(index) {
    while (index > 0) {
      const parent = (index - 1) >> 1;
      if (this.compare(this.data[index], this.data[parent]) >= 0) break;
      [this.data[index], this.data[parent]] = [this.data[parent], this.data[index]];
      index = parent;
    }
  }

  bubbleDown(index) {
    const n = this.data.length;

    while (true) {
      let best = index;
      const left = index * 2 + 1;
      const right = left + 1;

      if (left < n && this.compare(this.data[left], this.data[best]) < 0) best = left;
      if (right < n && this.compare(this.data[right], this.data[best]) < 0) best = right;
      if (best === index) break;

      [this.data[index], this.data[best]] = [this.data[best], this.data[index]];
      index = best;
    }
  }
}

const minHeap = new Heap((a, b) => a - b);
const maxHeap = new Heap((a, b) => b - a);
Hash Map / Hash SetO(1) avg lookup

Definition: Hash-based structures map keys to buckets so lookup, insert, and membership checks are usually constant time on average.

Philosophy: Spend memory to kill repeated scanning.

The approach: In JS, reach for built-in Map and Set first. Common moves are frequency counting, “seen before?”, and value-to-index lookup.

How to remember and understand it: If your inner monologue says “I keep checking whether this thing already appeared,” you probably want a hash structure.

When to use: Two Sum, deduplication, grouping, frequency counts, memoization caches, visited tracking.

Alternative way: Sorting + two pointers can reduce memory. Arrays work when the key range is tiny and numeric.

js
function twoSum(nums, target) {
  const seen = new Map();

  for (let i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
    const need = target - nums[i];
    if (seen.has(need)) return [seen.get(need), i];
    seen.set(nums[i], i);
  }

  return [];
}

function dedupe(values) {
  return [...new Set(values)];
}
Tree / BSTrecursive structure

Definition: A tree is a hierarchical structure with parent/child links. A Binary Search Tree adds ordering: left subtree values are smaller, right subtree values are larger.

Philosophy: Every node is the root of a smaller copy of the same problem.

The approach: Learn preorder/inorder/postorder traversals, then the BST rule: compare once and move left or right. Inorder traversal of a BST yields sorted order.

How to remember and understand it: Ask “what happens at one node?” then repeat for the child. Recursive tree problems often become simple once the per-node contract is clear.

When to use: Hierarchical data, ordered lookup, range queries, serialization, interview traversal drills.

Alternative way: A heap is better when you only need current min/max. A hash map is better for plain exact lookup without order.

js
class TreeNode {
  constructor(val, left = null, right = null) {
    this.val = val;
    this.left = left;
    this.right = right;
  }
}

function searchBST(root, target) {
  let node = root;

  while (node) {
    if (node.val === target) return node;
    node = target < node.val ? node.left : node.right;
  }

  return null;
}

function inorder(root, out = []) {
  if (!root) return out;
  inorder(root.left, out);
  out.push(root.val);
  inorder(root.right, out);
  return out;
}
Graph + BFS / DFSreachability + traversal

Definition: A graph is a set of nodes (vertices) connected by edges. BFS and DFS are the two fundamental ways to traverse it.

Philosophy: Separate the shape of the graph from the policy of exploration.

The approach: Use an adjacency list plus a seen set. BFS explores by layers with a queue and is ideal for shortest paths in unweighted graphs. DFS explores deeply and is ideal for exhaustive traversal, recursion, and component marking.

How to remember and understand it: BFS spreads like ripples in water; DFS digs a tunnel until it hits a wall, then backtracks.

When to use: Social network traversal, island counting, connected components, dependency exploration, shortest path in unweighted graphs.

Alternative way: Union-Find for connectivity-only problems. Topological sort for DAG ordering. Dijkstra for weighted shortest path.

js
function bfs(graph, start) {
  const queue = [start];
  const seen = new Set([start]);
  const order = [];
  let head = 0;

  while (head < queue.length) {
    const node = queue[head++];
    order.push(node);

    for (const next of graph.get(node) ?? []) {
      if (seen.has(next)) continue;
      seen.add(next);
      queue.push(next);
    }
  }

  return order;
}

function dfs(graph, node, seen = new Set(), order = []) {
  if (seen.has(node)) return order;
  seen.add(node);
  order.push(node);

  for (const next of graph.get(node) ?? []) {
    dfs(graph, next, seen, order);
  }

  return order;
}
Trieprefix search

Definition: A trie is a prefix tree where each edge represents one character and each path represents a prefix.

Philosophy: Share common prefixes so prefix queries become naturally cheap.

The approach: Walk character by character, create child nodes as needed, and mark terminal nodes for complete words.

How to remember and understand it: One character per edge. The whole idea is literally “branch when strings diverge.”

When to use: Autocomplete, dictionary lookup with prefixes, starts-with filtering, word search backtracking problems.

Alternative way: Hash sets are simpler when you only need full-word membership. Sorted arrays plus binary search can handle prefix queries if updates are rare.

js
class TrieNode {
  constructor() {
    this.children = new Map();
    this.end = false;
  }
}

class Trie {
  constructor() {
    this.root = new TrieNode();
  }

  insert(word) {
    let node = this.root;
    for (const ch of word) {
      if (!node.children.has(ch)) node.children.set(ch, new TrieNode());
      node = node.children.get(ch);
    }
    node.end = true;
  }

  walk(text) {
    let node = this.root;
    for (const ch of text) {
      if (!node.children.has(ch)) return null;
      node = node.children.get(ch);
    }
    return node;
  }

  search(word) {
    return this.walk(word)?.end === true;
  }

  startsWith(prefix) {
    return this.walk(prefix) !== null;
  }
}
Union-Find / DSUcomponents

Definition: Union-Find (Disjoint Set Union) tracks which elements belong to the same connected component.

Philosophy: Don’t store every path; just store a leader for each component and keep merging leaders.

The approach: Use a parent array, compress paths during find, and union by size/rank so trees stay shallow.

How to remember and understand it: Every node climbs to its leader. Path compression makes future climbs much shorter.

When to use: Dynamic connectivity, cycle detection in undirected graphs, Kruskal’s MST, grouping by equivalence.

Alternative way: BFS/DFS are easier for one-off connectivity checks when the graph is static and small.

js
class UnionFind {
  constructor(n) {
    this.parent = Array.from({ length: n }, (_, i) => i);
    this.size = Array(n).fill(1);
  }

  find(x) {
    if (this.parent[x] !== x) {
      this.parent[x] = this.find(this.parent[x]);
    }
    return this.parent[x];
  }

  union(a, b) {
    a = this.find(a);
    b = this.find(b);
    if (a === b) return false;

    if (this.size[a] < this.size[b]) [a, b] = [b, a];
    this.parent[b] = a;
    this.size[a] += this.size[b];
    return true;
  }

  connected(a, b) {
    return this.find(a) === this.find(b);
  }
}
Dynamic Programmingreuse overlapping work

Definition: Dynamic programming solves problems with overlapping subproblems by storing answers so each subproblem is solved once.

Philosophy: If the same smaller question keeps reappearing, pay the cost once and reuse the answer everywhere else.

The approach: Define the state, write the transition/recurrence, set base cases, then choose top-down memoization or bottom-up tabulation.

How to remember and understand it: Ask: “What is the smallest answer I can build, and what smaller answers does it depend on?” If many branches reuse the same sub-results, DP is a candidate.

When to use: Count ways, maximize/minimize value, shortest path in DAG-like state spaces, knapsack-style optimization, edit distance.

Alternative way: Greedy if a local-choice proof exists, plain recursion for tiny trees, backtracking when you truly need exhaustive enumeration.

js
function climbStairs(n) {
  if (n <= 2) return n;

  let twoBack = 1;
  let oneBack = 2;

  for (let i = 3; i <= n; i++) {
    const cur = oneBack + twoBack;
    twoBack = oneBack;
    oneBack = cur;
  }

  return oneBack;
}

Other must-know families you should map to the core set above:

Family Definition / idea Fast memory hook When to use Alternative
Merge sort Stable divide-and-conquer sort that merges two sorted halves “Split, sort, merge” Stable sorting, linked lists, external sorting Quick sort when in-place average speed matters more
Two pointers Move two indices under a shared invariant “Converge or expand” Sorted pairs, palindrome, dedupe, partition tasks Hashing if memory is okay and ordering is irrelevant
Sliding window Maintain a contiguous valid range while moving boundaries “Grow right, shrink left” Substring/subarray constraints, longest/shortest window problems Prefix sums when windows do not need dynamic validity
Prefix sum Precompute cumulative totals so range queries become subtraction “Pay once, query fast” Range sums, balances, subarray counts Sliding window for positive-only contiguous constraints
Backtracking Try, recurse, undo “Decision tree with undo” Permutations, combinations, N-Queens, word search DP if many subtrees repeat
Topological sort Order a DAG so prerequisites appear first “No node before its dependencies” Course schedule, build systems, dependency graphs DFS/BFS only if you just need traversal, not valid ordering
Dijkstra Shortest path with non-negative edge weights using a heap “Best frontier first” Weighted routing and min-cost path questions BFS if all edges weigh the same
Monotonic stack / queue Maintain values in increasing/decreasing order “Throw away dominated candidates” Next greater, histogram, sliding window max/min Heap if order by index does not matter as much
Greedy Commit to the best local move when a proof says it stays globally optimal “Local move, global proof” Intervals, scheduling, jump games, Huffman-style choices DP when local choices can paint you into a corner

How to study this section: memorize the mental model first, then dry-run the implementation on paper, then connect each item to one canonical problem. That sequence usually sticks far better than memorizing raw code in isolation.

#9.5. Live Coding Drills — React Patterns

These are common live coding asks for frontend React roles. Prep these 3 cold.

#Drill A — Debounced Search Input

tsx
import { useEffect, useState } from 'react';

export function SearchBox() {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState('');
  const [debouncedQuery, setDebouncedQuery] = useState('');

  useEffect(() => {
    const id = setTimeout(() => setDebouncedQuery(query), 300);
    return () => clearTimeout(id); // CRITICAL: cleanup
  }, [query]);

  useEffect(() => {
    if (!debouncedQuery) return;
    console.log('fetch', debouncedQuery); // replace with actual fetch
  }, [debouncedQuery]);

  return (
    <label>
      Search
      <input value={query} onChange={e => setQuery(e.target.value)} />
    </label>
  );
}

// Or as a reusable hook
function useDebouncedValue<T>(value: T, delay = 300): T {
  const [debounced, setDebounced] = useState(value);
  useEffect(() => {
    const id = setTimeout(() => setDebounced(value), delay);
    return () => clearTimeout(id);
  }, [value, delay]);
  return debounced;
}

#Drill B — Accessible Modal (WCAG 2.1 compliant)

Key requirements: focus trap, Escape close, scroll lock, restore focus on close, ARIA.

tsx
'use client';
import { useEffect, useRef } from 'react';
import { createPortal } from 'react-dom';

interface ModalProps {
  isOpen: boolean; onClose: () => void; title: string; children: React.ReactNode;
}

export function AccessibleModal({ isOpen, onClose, title, children }: ModalProps) {
  const modalRef = useRef<HTMLDivElement>(null);
  const prevFocusRef = useRef<HTMLElement | null>(null);

  // Save focus before open
  useEffect(() => {
    if (isOpen) prevFocusRef.current = document.activeElement as HTMLElement;
  }, [isOpen]);

  // Scroll lock
  useEffect(() => {
    if (!isOpen) return;
    const orig = document.body.style.overflow;
    document.body.style.overflow = 'hidden';
    return () => { document.body.style.overflow = orig; };
  }, [isOpen]);

  // Focus trap + Escape
  useEffect(() => {
    if (!isOpen) return;
    const handleKey = (e: KeyboardEvent) => {
      if (e.key === 'Escape') { onClose(); return; }
      if (e.key === 'Tab' && modalRef.current) {
        const focusable = modalRef.current.querySelectorAll<HTMLElement>(
          'a[href], button:not([disabled]), input, textarea, select, [tabindex]:not([tabindex="-1"])'
        );
        const first = focusable[0]; const last = focusable[focusable.length - 1];
        if (e.shiftKey && document.activeElement === first) { e.preventDefault(); last.focus(); }
        else if (!e.shiftKey && document.activeElement === last) { e.preventDefault(); first.focus(); }
      }
    };
    document.addEventListener('keydown', handleKey);
    return () => document.removeEventListener('keydown', handleKey);
  }, [isOpen, onClose]);

  // Auto-focus modal; restore on close
  useEffect(() => {
    if (isOpen) modalRef.current?.focus();
    else prevFocusRef.current?.focus();
  }, [isOpen]);

  if (!isOpen) return null;
  return createPortal(
    <div className="modal-overlay" onClick={onClose}>
      <div
        ref={modalRef} tabIndex={-1}
        role="dialog" aria-modal="true" aria-labelledby="modal-title"
        onClick={e => e.stopPropagation()} className="modal-content"
      >
        <header>
          <h2 id="modal-title">{title}</h2>
          <button onClick={onClose} aria-label="Close">✕</button>
        </header>
        <div>{children}</div>
      </div>
    </div>,
    document.body
  );
}

A11y checklist for this modal: focus trap ✓, Escape ✓, role="dialog" ✓, aria-modal="true" ✓, aria-labelledby ✓, restore focus ✓, scroll lock ✓.

#Drill D — Counter with Closure (plain JS)

Tests JS fundamentals: closure, encapsulation, no external state.

js
function createCounter() {
  let count = 0;

  return {
    increment() {
      count += 1;
      return count;
    },
    decrement() {
      count -= 1;
      return count;
    },
    getValue() {
      return count;
    },
  };
}

// Usage
const counter = createCounter();
counter.increment(); // 1
counter.increment(); // 2
counter.decrement(); // 1
counter.getValue();  // 1

Why closure works here: count lives in the outer function's scope. The returned object methods close over it — each createCounter() call gets its own private count. No class, no this confusion.

#Drill E — Responsive Card Grid (CSS)

Tests: CSS Grid, auto-fit, minmax, responsive without media queries.

css
.cards {
  display: grid;
  gap: 16px;
  /* auto-fit: fills row with as many columns as fit; minmax: each column min 220px, max 1fr */
  grid-template-columns: repeat(auto-fit, minmax(220px, 1fr));
}

How to explain it: "auto-fit creates as many columns as can fit at the minimum width. minmax(220px, 1fr) means each column is at least 220 px wide and stretches to fill remaining space equally. On a 700 px container you get 3 columns; on 400 px you get 1. No media queries needed."

Bonus — add aspect-ratio to cards so images don't jump:

css
.card img {
  width: 100%;
  aspect-ratio: 16 / 9;
  object-fit: cover;
}

#Drill F — Next.js Product List (Server Component + ISR)

Tests: App Router, Server Component, fetch with next.revalidate, error handling.

tsx
// app/products/page.tsx  — Server Component (no 'use client')
async function getProducts() {
  const res = await fetch('https://api.example.com/products', {
    next: { revalidate: 60 }, // ISR: revalidate every 60 seconds
  });

  if (!res.ok) {
    throw new Error('Failed to fetch products'); // Caught by error.tsx boundary
  }

  return res.json() as Promise<{ id: string; name: string; price: number }[]>;
}

export default async function ProductsPage() {
  const products = await getProducts();

  if (products.length === 0) {
    return <p>No products available.</p>;
  }

  return (
    <main>
      <h1>Products</h1>
      <ul className="cards">
        {products.map((product) => (
          <li key={product.id}>
            <strong>{product.name}</strong> — ${product.price}
          </li>
        ))}
      </ul>
    </main>
  );
}
tsx
// app/products/error.tsx — error boundary (required companion)
'use client';
export default function Error({ error, reset }: { error: Error; reset: () => void }) {
  return (
    <div>
      <p>Failed to load products: {error.message}</p>
      <button onClick={reset}>Retry</button>
    </div>
  );
}
tsx
// app/products/loading.tsx — streaming skeleton
export default function Loading() {
  return <p>Loading products…</p>;
}

Interview talking points:

  • No useEffect/useState — data fetches happen on the server, zero client JS for this page.
  • next: { revalidate: 60 } = ISR — stale-while-revalidate; use cache: 'no-store' for real-time.
  • error.tsx catches thrown errors from this segment; loading.tsx enables streaming/Suspense.
  • Always handle empty state + error state — three states: loading / error / data.

#Drill C — React Testing Library test

tsx
import { render, screen } from '@testing-library/react';
import userEvent from '@testing-library/user-event';
import { AccessibleModal } from './AccessibleModal';

describe('AccessibleModal', () => {
  const props = { isOpen: true, onClose: jest.fn(), title: 'Confirm Transfer', children: <p>$500</p> };

  test('renders ARIA structure correctly', () => {
    render(<AccessibleModal {...props} />);
    expect(screen.getByRole('dialog')).toHaveAttribute('aria-modal', 'true');
    expect(screen.getByRole('heading', { name: 'Confirm Transfer' })).toBeInTheDocument();
  });

  test('calls onClose on close button click', async () => {
    render(<AccessibleModal {...props} />);
    await userEvent.click(screen.getByRole('button', { name: 'Close' }));
    expect(props.onClose).toHaveBeenCalledTimes(1);
  });

  test('calls onClose on Escape key', async () => {
    render(<AccessibleModal {...props} />);
    await userEvent.keyboard('{Escape}');
    expect(props.onClose).toHaveBeenCalled();
  });
});

#Live coding checklist (run through before submitting)

  1. Semantic HTML used (button not div, label on inputs)
  2. Meaningful names
  3. Loading/empty/error state handled (if async)
  4. useEffect cleanup (timers, event listeners)
  5. No premature memoization
  6. Server Component by default in Next.js (add 'use client' only when needed)
  7. Input validated at boundary

#9.6. Common Traps — What NOT to do

Know these cold. Interviewers check if you know the pitfalls.

Trap Correct
div onClick instead of button Always button for actions — keyboard + screen reader built-in
Missing label on input Every input needs <label htmlFor> or wrapping <label>
useEffect for derived state Compute directly in render: const full = first + ' ' + last
Index as key in dynamic list Use stable ID: key={item.id}
No cleanup in useEffect (timer, listener) Always return () => clearTimeout(id)
"this depends on where it's declared" WRONG. this depends on call site. Only arrow functions inherit lexically.
Debounce for scroll, throttle for search REVERSED. Debounce = search input; throttle = scroll/resize
CSS flex for 2D layout Use grid for 2D (rows AND columns)
Every component 'use client' in Next.js Server by default; push 'use client' to smallest leaf
useEffect to fetch data when Server Component could Move fetch to Server Component, no useEffect needed
Forgetting loading + error states Always handle 3 states: loading / error / success
NEXT_PUBLIC_ prefix for secrets Any NEXT_PUBLIC_ is exposed in client bundle — never put secrets there
Inline object/array as prop to memoized child New ref each render = memo cache miss: useMemo the object
Missing await on params/cookies() in Next.js 15+ const { id } = await params — both are now async

#9.7. Part G.7 — GreatFrontEnd-style frontend coding questions

GreatFrontEnd (greatfrontend.com) curates the canonical frontend coding interview question set used at FAANG + global product companies. Enterprise frontend live coding draws from the same well: implement a small JS utility OR build a small UI component from scratch in 30–45 minutes. Each question below follows Q → A → Why → 📄 Evidence.

#G.7.1 — Vanilla JS utilities (must-do for any frontend live coding round)

Q-GFE-1. Implement debounce(fn, wait) and throttle(fn, wait). A: Already covered in depth — see Q27, Q27a, Q27b, Q27c. Why: top-1 most-asked JS utility in frontend interviews. Every variant (leading, trailing, cancel, flush, rAF-throttle) is fair game. 📄 lodash docs · GreatFrontEnd → "Debounce", "Throttle".

Q-GFE-2. Implement Promise.all, Promise.allSettled, Promise.race.

js
function promiseAll(promises) {
  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    const out = new Array(promises.length);
    let remaining = promises.length;
    if (remaining === 0) return resolve([]);
    promises.forEach((p, i) => {
      Promise.resolve(p).then(
        v => { out[i] = v; if (--remaining === 0) resolve(out); },
        reject                                                        // fail-fast
      );
    });
  });
}
function promiseAllSettled(promises) {
  return Promise.all(promises.map(p =>
    Promise.resolve(p).then(
      value => ({ status: 'fulfilled', value }),
      reason => ({ status: 'rejected', reason })
    )
  ));
}
function promiseRace(promises) {
  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    promises.forEach(p => Promise.resolve(p).then(resolve, reject));
  });
}

Why: tests Promise mental model (microtask, settling once, fail-fast vs collect-all). Panels ask because async aggregation is bread-and-butter for BFF endpoints. 📄 MDN → "Promise.all", "Promise.allSettled", "Promise.race".

Q-GFE-3. Implement deepClone(value) — handle nested objects, arrays, dates, sets, maps, cycles.

js
function deepClone(value, seen = new WeakMap()) {
  if (value === null || typeof value !== 'object') return value;        // primitives + null
  if (seen.has(value)) return seen.get(value);                          // cycle guard
  if (value instanceof Date) return new Date(value);
  if (value instanceof RegExp) return new RegExp(value);
  if (value instanceof Map) {
    const m = new Map(); seen.set(value, m);
    for (const [k, v] of value) m.set(deepClone(k, seen), deepClone(v, seen));
    return m;
  }
  if (value instanceof Set) {
    const s = new Set(); seen.set(value, s);
    for (const v of value) s.add(deepClone(v, seen));
    return s;
  }
  const clone = Array.isArray(value) ? [] : Object.create(Object.getPrototypeOf(value));
  seen.set(value, clone);
  for (const key of Reflect.ownKeys(value)) clone[key] = deepClone(value[key], seen);
  return clone;
}

Modern shortcut: structuredClone(value) — built-in browser API since 2022 (handles cycles, Dates, Maps, Sets; not functions/DOM nodes). Mention you'd reach for it first; only hand-roll if asked. Why: tests recursion + edge case enumeration + knowledge of structuredClone. 📄 MDN → "structuredClone".

Q-GFE-4. Implement curry(fn) so that curry(add)(1)(2)(3) === add(1,2,3).

js
function curry(fn, arity = fn.length) {
  return function curried(...args) {
    if (args.length >= arity) return fn(...args);
    return (...rest) => curried(...args, ...rest);
  };
}
const add = (a, b, c) => a + b + c;
const cAdd = curry(add);
cAdd(1)(2)(3);     // 6
cAdd(1, 2)(3);     // 6

Why: tests closures + variadic args + recursion. Common in FP-heavy codebases (Ramda, fp-ts). 📄 GreatFrontEnd → "Curry".

Q-GFE-5. Implement memoize(fn) — cache results by args.

js
function memoize(fn, hasher = (...args) => JSON.stringify(args)) {
  const cache = new Map();
  return function (...args) {
    const key = hasher(...args);
    if (cache.has(key)) return cache.get(key);
    const result = fn.apply(this, args);
    cache.set(key, result);
    return result;
  };
}

Why: foundational performance pattern; also used by useMemo conceptually. 📄 GreatFrontEnd → "Memoize".

Q-GFE-6. Implement flatten(arr, depth = Infinity) — array flattening.

js
function flatten(arr, depth = Infinity) {
  return arr.reduce((acc, v) => {
    if (Array.isArray(v) && depth > 0) acc.push(...flatten(v, depth - 1));
    else acc.push(v);
    return acc;
  }, []);
}
// Or built-in: arr.flat(depth)

Why: recursion warm-up. Variant: flatten an object ({a:{b:1}} → {'a.b':1}).

Q-GFE-7. Implement an EventEmitter (on / off / emit / once).

js
class EventEmitter {
  #listeners = new Map();                                 // event → Set<fn>

  on(event, fn) {
    if (!this.#listeners.has(event)) this.#listeners.set(event, new Set());
    this.#listeners.get(event).add(fn);
    return () => this.off(event, fn);                     // return unsubscribe
  }
  off(event, fn) {
    this.#listeners.get(event)?.delete(fn);
  }
  emit(event, ...args) {
    this.#listeners.get(event)?.forEach(fn => fn(...args));
  }
  once(event, fn) {
    const wrapper = (...args) => { this.off(event, wrapper); fn(...args); };
    this.on(event, wrapper);
  }
}

Why: tests OOP + collection management + closure (returned off closes over event,fn). Pattern behind Node's EventEmitter, RxJS subjects. 📄 GreatFrontEnd → "EventEmitter".

Q-GFE-8. Implement classNames(...args) — utility for conditional class strings.

js
function classNames(...args) {
  const out = [];
  for (const arg of args) {
    if (!arg) continue;
    if (typeof arg === 'string' || typeof arg === 'number') out.push(String(arg));
    else if (Array.isArray(arg)) { const inner = classNames(...arg); if (inner) out.push(inner); }
    else if (typeof arg === 'object') {
      for (const [k, v] of Object.entries(arg)) if (v) out.push(k);
    }
  }
  return out.join(' ');
}
classNames('btn', { active: true, disabled: false }, ['large']); // 'btn active large'

Why: replicates the popular classnames / clsx library — universal in React codebases. 📄 npm classnames, npm clsx.

Q-GFE-9. Implement pipe and compose.

js
const pipe    = (...fns) => x => fns.reduce((acc, f) => f(acc), x);
const compose = (...fns) => x => fns.reduceRight((acc, f) => f(acc), x);
pipe(double, addOne, square)(3);    // square(addOne(double(3)))
compose(square, addOne, double)(3); // same as pipe in reverse

Why: FP basics. Used by Redux middleware, Ramda, function-composition libs.

Q-GFE-10. Implement JSON.stringify (subset) — primitives, objects, arrays, escape strings. A: Recursive serialization with type dispatch. Edge cases: undefined keys are omitted, function values omitted in objects (return undefined in arrays), strings need escaping (\", \\, \n). Why: tests recursion + JSON spec familiarity. Detailed impl is long; in interview state the algorithm + handle the main cases. 📄 ECMAScript spec § JSON.stringify.

Q-GFE-11. Implement getElementsByClassName (cross-browser polyfill — DOM tree walking).

js
function getElementsByClassName(root, className) {
  const out = [], stack = [root];
  while (stack.length) {
    const node = stack.pop();
    if (node.nodeType === 1 && node.classList?.contains(className)) out.push(node);
    for (let i = node.children.length - 1; i >= 0; i--) stack.push(node.children[i]);
  }
  return out;
}

Why: tests iterative DOM traversal (avoid recursion stack on deep DOMs).

Q-GFE-12. Implement dataMerge / chunk / intersection / common Lodash utilities — at least be ready to write 2 from memory.

#G.7.2 — React UI builds (30–45 min asks)

Q-GFE-13. Build a <Tabs> Compound Component. A: See Q20b (Compound Component pattern). Skeleton:

tsx
const TabsContext = createContext<{ active: string; setActive: (id: string) => void } | null>(null);
function Tabs({ defaultValue, children }) {
  const [active, setActive] = useState(defaultValue);
  return <TabsContext.Provider value={{ active, setActive }}><div role="tablist">{children}</div></TabsContext.Provider>;
}
function Tab({ id, children }) {
  const ctx = useContext(TabsContext)!;
  return <button role="tab" aria-selected={ctx.active === id} onClick={() => ctx.setActive(id)}>{children}</button>;
}
function Panel({ id, children }) {
  const ctx = useContext(TabsContext)!;
  return ctx.active === id ? <div role="tabpanel">{children}</div> : null;
}
Tabs.Tab = Tab; Tabs.Panel = Panel;

Plus: keyboard arrows to switch tabs, focus management, aria-controls linkage. Why: tests Compound Component + a11y (role="tablist", arrow-key nav, aria-selected). 📄 WAI-ARIA Authoring Practices → "Tabs Pattern".

Q-GFE-14. Build an <Accordion> (multi or single expand). A: Same Compound pattern as Tabs but state is expandedIds: Set<string>. Each item: header <button aria-expanded aria-controls> + collapsible panel. ESC to collapse focused. Single-expand mode = swap Set for single id. 📄 WAI-ARIA → "Accordion".

Q-GFE-15. Build an <AccessibleModal> with focus trap + scroll lock + Escape close. A: See Drill B in §9.5 — full implementation provided. Why: a single live-coding question that exercises Portals, refs, focus management, ARIA, scroll lock, cleanup. Banking favorite ("you'll build dialogs for transactions").

Q-GFE-16. Build an Autocomplete / Typeahead with debounced fetch + keyboard nav. A: Approach:

  1. Controlled <input> → useState for query.
  2. useDebouncedValue(query, 300) (see Drill A) → effect fires fetch on debounced change.
  3. Suggestions list with aria-activedescendant for current option; ↑/↓/Enter/Esc keys.
  4. aria-autocomplete="list", role="combobox", aria-expanded, aria-controls.
  5. AbortController to cancel in-flight requests when query changes.
tsx
useEffect(() => {
  if (!debouncedQuery) return;
  const ctrl = new AbortController();
  fetch(`/api/search?q=${debouncedQuery}`, { signal: ctrl.signal })
    .then(r => r.json()).then(setResults).catch(e => { if (e.name !== 'AbortError') setError(e); });
  return () => ctrl.abort();
}, [debouncedQuery]);

Why: combines debounce + fetch race-condition handling (AbortController) + a11y combobox pattern. Top-3 most-asked frontend live-coding question. 📄 WAI-ARIA → "Combobox Pattern"; MDN → "AbortController".

Q-GFE-17. Build an Infinite Scroll list using IntersectionObserver.

tsx
function InfiniteList() {
  const [items, setItems] = useState<Item[]>([]);
  const [page, setPage] = useState(1);
  const [done, setDone] = useState(false);
  const sentinelRef = useRef<HTMLDivElement>(null);

  useEffect(() => {
    if (done) return;
    const obs = new IntersectionObserver(([entry]) => {
      if (entry.isIntersecting) setPage(p => p + 1);
    }, { rootMargin: '200px' });
    if (sentinelRef.current) obs.observe(sentinelRef.current);
    return () => obs.disconnect();
  }, [done]);

  useEffect(() => {
    fetchPage(page).then(({ items: more, hasMore }) => {
      setItems(prev => [...prev, ...more]);
      if (!hasMore) setDone(true);
    });
  }, [page]);

  return (
    <ul>
      {items.map(i => <li key={i.id}>{i.name}</li>)}
      <div ref={sentinelRef} />
    </ul>
  );
}

Why: prefer IntersectionObserver over scroll-event throttling (better perf). Banking use: paginated transaction lists. 📄 MDN → "IntersectionObserver".

Q-GFE-18. Build an Image Carousel (auto-rotate + arrows + dots + a11y). A: State = index (0..n-1). Buttons: prev / next mutate index. useEffect with setInterval for auto-rotate; pause on hover/focus. Visual: translateX(-index * 100%) on a flex track, CSS transition. A11y: arrow buttons labeled, current slide announced via aria-live="polite", dots are buttons with aria-current="true" for active. Why: tests state + side-effects + a11y + animation. Classic banking promo carousel.

Q-GFE-19. Build a Like / Optimistic-Update button. A: See Q20m (manual optimistic + useOptimistic). Add aria-pressed for toggle button a11y.

Q-GFE-20. Build a Star-Rating component (controlled + keyboard + a11y). A: 5 (or N) star buttons; click sets value; hover preview. A11y: role="radiogroup" with each star role="radio" aria-checked + arrow-key nav. Or use a single <input type="range"> for keyboard out of the box, restyled. Why: tests both controlled UI and a11y radiogroup pattern.

Q-GFE-21. Build a Counter / Stopwatch using useReducer + accurate timing. A: Don't compound setTimeout (drift). Use Date.now() deltas + requestAnimationFrame (or setInterval with computed elapsed):

tsx
const startRef = useRef<number>(0);
function start() { startRef.current = Date.now() - elapsed; tick(); }
function tick() {
  setElapsed(Date.now() - startRef.current);
  rafRef.current = requestAnimationFrame(tick);
}

Why: tests timer accuracy mental model — drift is a real bug pattern.

Q-GFE-22. Build a Progress Bar with a11y (role="progressbar"). A: <div role="progressbar" aria-valuenow={pct} aria-valuemin={0} aria-valuemax={100}> + visual fill via inline width style. Indeterminate state: omit aria-valuenow. Why: simple but tests role="progressbar" knowledge — banking loaders.

Q-GFE-23. Build a Todo List (CRUD + filter + persist) — full app live-coding. A: useReducer for todos state; actions: add, toggle, remove, clearCompleted, setFilter. Persist via localStorage in a useEffect. Each item editable on double-click. Keyboard: Enter to add, Esc to cancel edit. role="list" + each item role="listitem" + checkbox aria-checked. Why: ubiquitous "build me an app in 30 minutes" question — covers state shape, controlled inputs, persistence, a11y, semantic HTML.

Q-GFE-24. Build a Virtualized List (window large list — only render visible rows). A: For interview, hand-roll a fixed-row-height version:

  1. Container height known; rowHeight known; total = items.length.
  2. On scroll, compute startIndex = floor(scrollTop / rowHeight), endIndex = startIndex + visibleCount + overscan.
  3. Render only [startIndex..endIndex] items, absolute-position each at top: i*rowHeight.
  4. Container content height = total * rowHeight (so the scrollbar is correct).

Production: use react-window / @tanstack/react-virtual (variable height, dynamic measurement). Why: a senior-level performance question — critical for transaction-history banking lists with 50k+ rows. 📄 react-window docs; tanstack.com/virtual.

#G.7.3 — Frontend system design (mini)

Q-GFE-25. Design an Autocomplete component (system design flavor — not just code). A: Discuss in this order:

  1. Functional requirements — debounce, keyboard nav, async fetch, loading state, error fallback.
  2. API contract — GET /search?q=...&limit=10 → { results: Suggestion[] }. Cancellable.
  3. Performance — debounce 200–300ms, AbortController for stale requests, cache by query (LRU), prefetch top-N on focus.
  4. A11y — combobox role, aria-expanded, aria-activedescendant, screen reader live updates.
  5. Failure modes — empty results, network error, slow API → skeleton then error toast.
  6. Edge cases — IME composition (don't fetch mid-typing for CJK), trailing spaces, special chars.
  7. Telemetry — log query, click-through, abandonment.

Why: GFE-style system design rounds want communication + tradeoff articulation. Banking ask: "design the customer search in your branch app". 📄 GreatFrontEnd → "Front End System Design"; "Autocomplete".
